The titles in this series deliver authoritative information to professionals, researchers, and students across material and device engineering as well as other scientific disciplines. Preface

In recent years, the fields of very large-scale integration (VLSI) and machine learning (ML) have witnessed an unprecedented convergence, sparking a new era of innovation in electronic system design. This book, Advancing VLSI through Machine Learning: Innovations and Research Perspectives, is a culmination of efforts to bridge the gap between these two dynamic disciplines, exploring their synergistic potential to revolutionize computing capabilities and shape the future of intelligent devices and technologies.

At the heart of this book lies a comprehensive exploration of foundational principles and cutting-edge applications in VLSI-ML integration. From optimizing circuit synthesis using neural networks and evolutionary algorithms to innovative designs of high-power amplifiers utilizing GaN HEMT and CMOS technologies, each chapter delves into critical aspects that define the forefront of research and development.

The journey begins with an in-depth analysis of physical processes and phenomena in AlGaN/GaN HEMTs, providing crucial insights into performance degradation and reliability—a foundational understanding essential for robust circuit design. Subsequently, the framework for memory design and evaluation using Memristors offers a glimpse into future memory technologies, showcasing the transformative potential of emerging electronic components.

Throughout the book, readers will explore FPGA architectures tailored for matrix multiplication in ML applications, paving the way for efficient computation in data-intensive tasks. Furthermore, innovative approaches in deep learning for early brain tumour detection underscore the transformative impact of ML in healthcare, highlighting its application beyond traditional electronic systems.

The integration of technology computer-aided design (TCAD) augmented ML offers a predictive framework for device behaviour and failure analysis, empowering designers with enhanced tools for performance optimization and reliability assurance. Additionally, chapters dedicated to the role of ML in FPGA backend flows and the application of artificial intelligence (AI)/ML algorithms in VLSI design underscore the transformative impact of intelligent algorithms in streamlining design processes and optimizing system performance.

As we embark on this journey through the realms of FinFET-based SRAM for AI/ML applications, power consumption analysis in SRAM technologies, and the technological advancements of hyper-FET, the book culminates in a comprehensive analysis of smart system realization using blockchain and fog computing—an exploration into the future of distributed, secure, and intelligent electronic systems.

This book is intended to serve as a definitive resource for researchers, engineers, and students alike, offering insights into the latest advancements, challenges, and opportunities in VLSI-ML integration. It is our hope that the chapters within will inspire innovation, foster collaboration across disciplines, and propel the development of next-generation electronic systems that redefine the boundaries of possibility.

1 Optimizing Circuit SynthesisIntegrating Neural Networks and Evolutionary Algorithms for Increased Design Efficiency

Swagata Devi, Naushad Manzoor Laskar, Shreyas Nirgude, and Saanvi Srivastava

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-1

1.1 Introduction

The integration of neural networks (NNs) with evolutionary algorithms represents a paradigm shift in analog designs and simulation tools and offers a compelling solution to the challenges in circuit design. This approach not only accelerates the circuit synthesis process but also lays the foundation for future advancements in the field of circuit design automation [1]. Traditionally, designers relied on manual methods to optimize circuit configurations, which proved to be labor-intensive and time-consuming. With the advent of computational techniques, evolutionary algorithms emerged as powerful tools for automating the circuit synthesis process. These algorithms enable designers to explore a vast search space efficiently, identifying optimal circuit configurations based on specified performance criteria [2]. In traditional simulation-driven circuit synthesizers, data generated by previous generation is often disregarded, leading to inefficiencies in the optimization process. In contrast, the power of artificial neural networks (ANNs) within the optimization loop uses data from preceding generations to train NNs, by establishing a valuable mechanism for capturing the underlying relationships between circuit configurations and performance metrics [3]. Once trained, these NNs serve as efficient surrogates for the simulator, enabling rapid estimation of performance parameters for new candidate solutions. This paradigm shifts from simulation-based evaluation to NN-based prediction results in a considerable minimization in execution time, significantly increasing the efficiency of the circuit synthesis process [4,5].

Over the past decade, focused research was carried out to improve artificial intelligence (AI), applying optimization techniques. Abdolrasol et al. [6] used ANNs optimized using various algorithms, including genetic algorithms (GAs), particle swarm optimization (PSO) method, artificial bee colony (ABC) method, and backtracking search algorithm (BSA) method, alongside advanced techniques like lightning search optimization (LSA) and whale optimization (WOA). ANNs via optimization algorithms are used to tune parameters for optimal structure. The results demonstrate improved ANN performance by the above algorithms in solving energy management problems for systems. Devi et al. [7] optimized parameters for a self-biased amplifier in seizure diagnosis preamplifiers, using metaheuristics and an ANN method, targeting a minimal preamplifier area. The ANN method outperformed other methods, including the efficient whale optimization technique, achieving a 50% smaller preamplifier area. This optimization is vital for developing compact, wearable, or implantable neurodiagnostic devices, tested in a Cadence 180 nm Semi-Conductor Laboratory (SCL) environment. Wang et al. [8] introduced a novel analog circuit optimization method combining a GA-based global optimization engine and an ANN-based local optimization engine, significantly speeding up automated analog circuit sizing by utilizing parallel computation for both global optimization via SPICE simulations and local minimum searches through ANN model predictions, and this system achieves a fourfold increase in optimization speed. It successfully designed complex analog circuits like operational amplifiers and Chebyshev filters, offering results comparable to traditional methods but much faster. In similar studies, Rosa et al. [9] used an ANN-based methodology for the automatic circuit sizing and layout of analog ICs, employing deep learning to learn from optimized design patterns. The methodology features two ANN architectures for direct mapping from specifications to device sizes and includes topology selection, demonstrating a significant efficiency in circuit sizing. While Chakraborty et al. [10] discussed the application of multilayer feed-forward NNs in optimizing CMOS analog circuits, we emphasize on the NN model’s ability to replace empirical and numerical modeling methods for enhanced accuracy and computational efficiency. Harsha et al. [11] proposed an ANN model for optimizing the design of 2-stage op-amps, addressing the challenge of multidimensional trade-offs in analog circuit design. The model, validated against SPICE simulations, demonstrates the potential of ANNs to achieve design optimization swiftly and accurately. Budak et al. [12] presented an efficient machine learning-based global optimization method for analog circuit sizing, named ESSAB. This method tackles the challenges of prediction errors and high ML costs in the face of stringent design specifications, through innovations in design ranking and ANN model construction for circuit performance. The integration of soft computing methods in analog IC design automation represents a paradigm shift toward more efficient, accurate, and cost-effective methods. By addressing critical challenges, such as computational expense, prediction accuracy, and multidimensional optimization, these AI-driven approaches hold the promise of significantly advancing the field of electronic design automation (EDA).

Therefore, despite the significant progress in AI-assisted electronic circuit design utilizing methods like Bayesian optimization and GAs, a gap persists in designing implantable circuits with cloud connectivity. This niche demands optimization approaches tailored for the dual challenges of minimizing size while ensuring functionality. The presented research innovatively targets this by optimizing a high-swing self-biased folded cascode amplifier for biomedical applications, using advanced techniques, including metaheuristic algorithms and ANNs. It stands out by conducting both quantitative and qualitative analyses to evaluate optimization strategies, particularly their speed and efficiency in avoiding local optima, thus addressing the gap and enriching methodological approaches for designing constrained implantable electronics.

1.2 Optimization of High-Swing Self-Biased Folded Cascode (HSSFC) Amplifier

Figure 1.1 depicts the HSSFC amplifier, an enhanced variant of the traditional folded cascode amplifier, incorporating source-degeneration-based current sources, M3–M4, rather than conventional ones. This leads to an enhancement in input noise. It is facilitated by using current scale resistors, R1 and R2, which reduce the current flow through the folded area of M7–M10, where just a fraction of differential pair M1–M2 current flows. The choice of device sizes, resistance values, and other design parameters ensures high overall transconductance, leading to lower noise. Furthermore, to enhance swing, an additional transistor, M13, is introduced as shown in Figure 1.1 [13]. In the HSSFC, hybrid whale particle swarm optimization (HWPSO) and other bioinspired algorithms are tailored for low-frequency/biomedical applications, which is optimized to minimize the circuit area while satisfying specific constraints as shown in Table 1.1. The objective function for the design is stated in equation (1.1), which is minimized subject to equations (1.2)–(1.11).

VCM(max) (V) ≤1.6
CMRR (dB) >60

HWPSO proved more consistent and statistically superior to other algorithms in over 20 trials, as evidenced by lower standard deviations and rigorous statistical tests as shown in Table 1.2 and Figure 1.2. Validation in Cadence Virtuoso confirmed HWPSO’s effectiveness, achieving a high gain and suitable cutoff frequency for biomedical amplifiers, with a minor trade-off in power dissipation. Simulations closely matched algorithmic predictions, with minimal deviations attributed to unmodeled parasitics and higher-order effects, demonstrating HWPSO’s robustness in circuit design optimization over other approaches [17].

1.3 Artificial Neural Network methods (Assisted Objective Attainment Method)

The methodology for determining optimal circuit parameters integrates the fitting of NNs with the objective attainment method, as illustrated in a comprehensive flowchart. This approach begins with the supervised training of an ANN, utilizing a dataset generated from around 150 approximated simulations within the Cadence 180 nm SCL environment and HWPSO-based best results. This dataset encompasses a range of circuit specifications, including Slew Rate (SR), Av, and Unity-Gain Bandwidth (UGB), as mentioned in Table 1.1. These parameters are crucial for calculating circuit area and for conducting further simulations to assess performance metrics like gain, phase, noise, and power. A discriminative NN is developed to predict circuit area from six input parameters, offering a swift alternative to extensive numerical simulations. This is particularly advantageous for complex circuit models, where ANNs expedite the calculation process across dense parameter spaces. The ANN employed is a shallow feed-forward-based backward propagation network (FFBN), potentially expanding to a deep network structure for larger datasets. This network uses the sigmoid symmetric transfer function for its activation function, tested with ten nodes in the hidden layer.

Data preparation involves dividing the initial dataset into training, validation, and testing sets, with stochastic partitioning to facilitate diverse training scenarios. The training process adjusts the ANN’s weights and biases through iterations until the output closely matches target values derived from Cadence simulations. The fitting quality is assessed using mean squared error (MSE) and regression analysis. On establishing an effective ANN model, the next phase is optimization through the goal attainment method. This method, part of nonlinear programming techniques, like sequential quadratic programming (SQP), aims to minimize the deviation from set design goals, considering various constraints. The methodology’s merit lies in its ability to achieve precise optimization results, guiding the design toward minimal circuit area while satisfying specified performance criteria. Figure 1.3 presents a flowchart illustrating the protocol employed for estimating optimal circuit parameters through NN fitting and the goal attainment method.

This study presents a novel approach to reduce circuit synthesis time by integrating ANNs with bioinspired algorithms. Traditional reliance on simulations for performance evaluation is minimized by using ANNs to predict outcomes based on circuit configurations. The process involves initializing solutions, evaluating them, training ANNs with historical data, and using these ANNs for performance prediction. This results in efficiently exploring the search area, ultimately speeding up the identification of optimal circuit configurations through iterative optimization until convergence criteria are met.

1.4 Results and Discussion

Simulation results demonstrate the effectiveness of integrating ANNs with bioinspired algorithms in enhancing the efficiency of circuit synthesis by replacing simulations with ANNs for performance prediction, and significant reductions in execution times can be achieved without compromising the quality of the synthesized circuits. Comparative analyses between traditional simulation-based approaches and our proposed methodology highlight the superiority of the latter in terms of computational efficiency and design optimization. The results illustrated are determined in Octave, version 2.0, and MathWorks MATLAB, version R2019a. The ANN employed for relation fitting between the overall area of circuit and the six design criteria was a shallow FFBN shown in Figure 1.4.

The relationship between the total circuit size and the eight associated parameters using an ANN is a shallow FFBN, the topology of which is depicted in Figure 1.4. With only one hidden layer, the suggested ANN is shallow due to the dataset’s total of 150 samples. A deep structure — one with multiple hidden layers — might be employed if there are more datasets available [18]. Considering a given number of circuit variables, the i/p’s and o/p’s, the optimal ANN design can be found by varying the total neuron numbers present in the hidden and output layers, introducing additional hidden layers (and utilizing methods from deep learning [19]), or altering the activation function.

In the described work, the authors utilized a shallow FFBN NN with a sigmoid symmetric transfer function as the activation function. This NN was employed to fit the relationship between the overall circuit area and seven related parameters. The NN architecture is illustrated in Figure 1.4, featuring dual hidden layers. It is noted that with a dataset comprising 150 examples, the chosen architecture is shallow. However, the authors suggest that in scenarios where a greater volume of data is available, a deep NN architecture might be more appropriate. A deep structure typically involves several hidden layers. The optimization of the ANN configuration can involve various strategies as follows:

  1. Adding More Hidden Layers: Deep learning techniques can be employed to create a network with multiple hidden layers, enabling the network to capture more complex patterns in the data.
  2. Adjusting Neurons Present in Hidden and Output Layers: The total neurons present in each layer can significantly impact the network’s capacity to learn and generalize. This parameter can be tuned based on the problem’s complexity and the available data.
  3. Changing the Activation Function: The activation function plays a crucial role in introducing nonlinearity to the network. While the sigmoid symmetric transfer function was used in the described work, alternative activation functions like ReLU (rectified linear unit), Tanh (hyperbolic tangent), or others could be explored to potentially improve the model’s performance.

The sigmoid symmetric transfer function was used as activation function in this chapter in equation (1.13):

$y = \frac{2}{1 + e^{- 2x}}$(1.13)

The parameters employed during training were 1000 for maximum epochs, 0 for goal, 10−7 for the minimum gradient of performance, and μ begins with 0.001 and increases on factor of 0.001 and a maximum of 1010. The number of failures allowed during checking validation is six at maximum. The constants and variables for the ANN-assisted goal attainment method are the same as the HWPSO to find the optimized value of the objective function. The ANN fitting functions are obtained by using the Levenberg–Marquardt (LM) algorithm for nonlinear least-squares curve-fitting problems on a ratio of 70:15:15, which implied that out of 150 examples in a dataset, 70% (104) were employed for training, 15% (23) in validating, and rest 15% (23) for the purpose of testing. Using 150 × 7 matrix as an input and a column of 150 numerical values as an output, the training for 150 examples for feeding the ANN was conducted. The process of ANN fitting function training with ten neurons needed one second to meet training criteria by reaching six validations successfully, and the drop in gradient is shown in Figure 1.5.

The optimized values obtained are shown in Table 1.4. The results indicate that the ANN-based approach demonstrated a design having higher gain and UGB but at the cost of increased area and power. The increase is, however, within the specifications as defined in Table 1.1.

1.5 Conclusion

To conclude, the integration of ANNs with bioinspired algorithms offers a promising approach to enhance the efficiency of circuit synthesis. By leveraging ANNs as surrogate models for performance prediction, the reliance on time-consuming simulations can be mitigated, thereby accelerating the design process. Future research directions may explore further refinements to the proposed methodology and investigate its applicability to various circuit synthesis problems across different domains.

2 Study of Physical Process Analysis and Phenomena of Insights of Trapping in Performance Degradation in AlGaN/GaN HEMTs

Rayabarapu Venkateswarlu, G.P.S.C. Mishra, and B. Acharya

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-2

2.1 Introduction

Wide bandgap materials such as gallium nitride (GaN)-based power devices grown on silicon, sapphire, and silicon carbide are essentially used for large power, microwave, and millimetre (mm)-wave applications. Due to its peculiar properties, it can deliver high breakdown voltages and high switching speed to metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor (MOSFETs) with low dynamic on-resistance (Dynamic-RON). The AlGaN/GaN heterojunction consists of piezoelectric and spontaneous polarizations that induce a high electron charge density called 2-dimensional (2D) electron gas (2-DEG). Heterojunction properties of these III-nitride materials form the device GaN-based AlGaN/GaN heterostructure in high electron mobility transistor. Because of excellent properties of the high electron mobility, these are used in advanced mm-wave, radar, avionics, power switching, and power amplification applications [1]. For mm-wave communications, high-power and high-efficiency power amplifiers are needed to meet the demands of high data rate transmission. Fifth-generation mobile communications applications use solid-state devices. Traditional silicon-based devices cannot fulfil the requirement of high power and high frequency. The design of radio frequency (RF) circuits developed on GaN employing metal-organic chemical vapour deposition has recently made significant advancements [2, 3, 4, 5, 6]. Silicon nitride is a popular passivation material that has been tried on GaN HEMTs using a variety of production techniques, including PECVD (plasma-enhanced chemical vapour deposition) [7]. The name itself suggests that the devices’ speed (on/off ratio) is remarkably fast, ranging from 106 to 1010, due to their possession of the 2-DEG. The foremost important characteristic of the high electron mobility devices is its threshold voltage (VTH) and saturated drain current (ID). The threshold voltage shift occurs due to the negatively charged traps below the gate electrode. The rise in drain current is observed due to pinch-off voltage (VP) shift, resulting in a decrease in gate current Ig and Igdo [8]. In addition to that, the hot electron carrier traps will present in epitaxial for long-time stresses, degrading the device DC characteristics and RF performance [9].

2.2 Energy States in Conduction Band Description

Theoretical background of AlGaN/GaN HEMT: HEMT is a type of field effect transistor (FET) which employs heterojunction structures of III–V group elements for the applications with high switching speed and high-power requirements. GaN is a group III-nitride direct bandgap semiconductor that has the hexagonal crystal lattice structure. GaN compound is a very hard material having bilayers of gallium and nitride molecules in wurtzite crystal structure. The bilayers consist of crystal lattice mismatch that causes the spontaneous polarization (PSP) and the piezoelectric polarization (PPE). As the concentration of aluminium (Al) in the strained AlGaN increases, so does the piezoelectric polarization (PPE) and the total polarization-induced electron charge density. The existence of PSP and PPE (spontaneous and piezoelectric polarization) charges is responsible for 2-DEG (2-DEG of the order, i.e., 1013 cm−2) sheet charge carrier densities. The charge density and bandgap discontinuity occur due to the Al mole content. The usage of Al content gives the proper optimization of the devices. These devices work on the modulation doping principle; hence, these devices are also termed as modulation-doped FETs or heterojunction FETs. This is achieved by the transportation of the carriers from donor ions in an adjacent barrier layer of AlxGa(1−x)As/AlxGa(1−x)N, where x denotes the Al concentration into high purity GaAs/GaN with the electrons and ions being separated by the built-in heterojunction potential [10] (Figures 2.1 and 2.2).

Some advantages of HEMTs are as follows:

  1. Wide bandwidth
  2. High linearity
  3. High electron charge motility
  4. High-frequency (switching) performance
  5. Low noise
  6. High power charge density
  7. High thermal stabilities

Applications of GaN transistors (Figure 2.3) are as follows:

  1. Fifth-generation and mm-wave communications cellular base stations.
  2. Power supplies.
  3. Space and radar surveillance applications.
  4. Power switching.

The HEMT transistor low-frequency model equivalent circuit is shown in Figure 2.4.

Despite these potential characteristics, GaN HEMTs have several challenges that make the transistor unreliable. The safe operation (SOP) of the device under external pressures is a major difficulty in high frequency and power-switching applications due to the device’s complexity. Defects are the localized states in the semiconductor materials that can capture and release the charge carriers (electrons and holes). Traps can affect the device performance by influencing the carrier mobility, charge distribution, and other electrical characteristics. When the traps are captured or released, the current fluctuates, which causes the devices to behave nonlinearly and with excessive noise. The speed and high-power performance of the device may be affected by the traps close to the channel because they can disperse charge carriers and deplete their mobility. Consequently, the transconductance, Gm, may be reduced, and hence, rise in Dynamic-RON. Traps are localized states that can emit or capture the electrons/holes by the process called trapping/de-trapping. The possible traps are surface traps present at the passivation and barrier interface, the traps present in the barrier layer, buffer traps present in the buffer layer, and interface traps involved at the AlGaN/GaN interface. Accumulation of holes at the passivation/AlGaN causes surface traps. Buffer traps/deep-level traps are mainly responsible for the DC–RF performance degradation [11]. Temperature variation results in traps because of threshold voltage shift [12]. Short channel effects (SCEs) are also responsible for GaN HEMT high-frequency performance degradation. Possible techniques to reduce the traps are by dielectric passivation, gate region insertion into the barrier called gate recess techniques, proper surface fabrication preparation, and the growth of doping profile in epitaxial layer.

Various drain current and threshold voltage-induced traps in AlGaN/GaN HEMT are studied and presented. Different measurement techniques like the pulsed-RF excitation, drain-lag, pulsed IDS–VDS, gate-lag, transient response, and frequency dispersion are used.

2.3 The Challenging Parameters

The challenging parameters that affect the device characteristics include:

Capturing and releasing of carriers: When the trapped charge carriers are released, they can contribute to current fluctuations, prone to noise and nonideal behaviour of the device. There was an increase in GaN channel 2-DEG concentration, when the trapped electrons emitted. Traps can capture charge carriers (electrons in n-HEMTs) as they move through the 2D electron gas channel, altering the overall mobility and current flow. The increase in the device’s internal temperature leads to a rise of gate leakage current and an increase in the voltage drop across the gate terminal. The trapping effect is primarily due to the presence of the high field in the drain terminal at the gate edge side between gate and drain.

Carrier mobility: Traps can restrict the charge carrier’s ability to move, which lowers their mobility. Carriers scatter as they encounter the traps, which results in a less effective flow of current. The speed, general effectiveness, and amplification capabilities of the device are impacted by the traps.

Threshold voltage shift (VTH variation): The threshold voltage determines the state at which the device is switched to on state. When a device switching takes place from off to on and vice-versa, it can be altered because of the traps. The large gate negative bias at the gate edge causes a threshold voltage shift because it leaves more electrons below the gate terminal, which causes surface donor traps to generate virtual gates. The virtual gate formation is due to decaying of surface net positive charge. This has happened due to electron traps present at the surface donor-like states [14]. These fluctuations can result in variations in device behaviour and performance, making it challenging to control and predict its operational characteristics.

Degradation and ageing effect: As charges formed inside the transistor in the form of traps, the performance characteristics of the device may gradually change over the time. As a result, there is a decrease in the device performance that includes decrease in gain and a shorter operational life span of the device.

Noise and instabilities: Traps bring unpredictability into the capture and release of charge carriers, which causes noise and instability. The electrical behaviour of the device fluctuates because of the randomness, which adds to noise, worse signal quality, and unstable output.

2.4 Trapping Mechanisms

Different types of traps are as follows:

2.4.1 Gate Leakage Traps

Traps located close to the gate-channel interface can promote the generation of undesirable leakage currents. Power usage and energy efficiency were impacted. According to the charge neutrality condition, in the absence of external electric field, the field magnitudes thus formed have equal polarization charges. As the charges of opposite signs accumulate and the fixed positive charge polarization increases, the ionization of acceptor traps also rises, leading to a decrease in electron concentration [15]. The threshold voltage (shift) variation is due to strongly influencing the field under the gate, mainly whenever the devices applied to bias, i.e., quiescent during off-state [8,16]. The gate leakage current dominates mainly when the voltage applied to the Schottky gate electrode exceeds the critical field value [17]. The essential phenomenological models used to improve the reduction in the gate leakage is by gate electrons trapped into barrier surface [9,17], lattice mismatch, defects in AlGaN barrier [9], and inverse piezoelectric polarization effect in the barrier layer [18]. The combination of better quality SiO2/AlGaN and Si3N4/AlGaN interfaces decreases the gate leakage current to few orders in magnitude below those of normal frequently used high electron mobility transistor devices, good linearity characteristics, and high saturation currents. Double layers also allow for a large positive threshold voltage shift. Incorporation of indium (In) surfactants and small concentration of carbon (C) improves the surface and interface roughness [19]. If deep acceptors are introduced by plasma treatment into the AlGaN barrier layer’s surface, they compensate for the high-density acceptable charge, and then gate depletion layer thickness increases. Due to electron tunnelling, the gate leakage current decreases [20,21].

2.4.2 Interface Traps

Usually, interface traps are located between semiconductor and the insulating material or passivation layer. These traps arise due to defects introduced during device fabrication or due to inherent nature of the interface. The drain current variation trends due to concentration variations of the ionized acceptor-like interface traps. Interface traps can affect the charge transport characteristics across the interface and alter the characteristics of the devices [15]. In comparison with metal-oxide semiconductor devices, the interconnection between the dielectric material and the semiconductor at the channel, the buried-channel group III-nitride metal–insulator–semiconductor heterostructure consists of two interfaces one at the passivation/group III-nitride and the other at the barrier to channel layer interface. This complicates the extraction of interface traps in III-nitride heterostructures [22] (Figure 2.5).

Trapping and de-trapping effects are the defects that present in the device due to a mismatch in lattice structures and current transportation process issues. The traps are either a donor trap or an acceptor trap. Traps are formed if one or more electrons capture or emit to or from the conduction band (CB) and holes to or from the valence band (VB). For instance, a trapping is empty, and those that are negatively charged get ionized when one or more energy levels are occupied by the electrons. Similar to this, a donor trap is positively charged and ionized if one or more energy levels are vacant, and all the levels have been filled with electrons. By the recombination process, the emission energy released is due to radiative recombination (photon), nonradiative recombination (phonon), or auger recombination. If a carrier captured in a confined state can combine with a carrier of the opposing sign, traps will be created if the opposite sign carrier is also trapped locally. Trapping and de-trapping phenomenon degrades the transistor performance, and hence, the reliability of the GaN/AlGaN transistors. The interfacial traps may present at the passivation/AlGaN interface, AlGaN/GaN interface, and buffer/GaN interface [23]. Different trap measuring methods are (i) transient drain-lag and gate-lag, (ii) pulsed IDS–VDS technique, (iii) frequency dispersion, and (iv) pulsed-RF excitation. Reduction in current will take place in GaN devices before and after the execution of high drain bias, which is termed as current collapse (CC), current slump, or current dispersion [24]. The initial CC is more in highly doped buffer layer, but a faster recovery process is observed due to emission of larger proportion of trapped electrons. On set, a slower recovery process depends on the lower content of ferrous concentration. Negative effects of traps in the buffer can be minimized by the iron (Fe)-buffer doping profile. The shift in threshold voltage or drift (ΔVTH) is due to which traps fall under the gate, also affecting the Ids–VDS saturation curve. Given that type T3 traps are in proximity with the 2D electron gas, it is anticipated that they will primarily affect the DC current–voltage (I–V) characteristics [25]. In semi-on state conditions, the hot electron trappings accelerate the traps present at the passivation/barrier interface and the AlGaN high-resistivity buffer layer. This is the strong reason why the 2-DEG gets depleted at the barrier/channel layer (Figure 2.6).

The probability of an electron hopping toward a drain is when a strong electric field is present, but it reaches a threshold value, indicating that it becomes saturated. Under both conditions for gate-lag and drain-lag measurements, the 2D electron gas depletion eventually increases the Dynamic-RON that is provoked by tunnelled gate electrons caught in surface traps [26]. The extrinsic electron traps or deep-level traps in the buffer layer are acceptor traps (when electrons capture) and donor traps (when electrons emit). The intrinsic surface states induce carrier’s capture/release of electrons that further increase the CC. These phenomena are usually originated from the buffer-doping profile, dislocations, internal crystal defects, and GaN process technology.

2.4.3 Bulk Traps

Bulk traps are found within the semiconductor materials. These may result from crystal defects, impurities, and other imperfections. The AlGaN/GaN layer lattice mismatch causes significant spontaneous and strain-induced piezoelectric polarization fields, which further raises the density of 2-DEG. An increase in the Al concentration percentage in the barrier layer causes the discontinuities in conduction band, and the doping density interface is most responsible for the high charge carrier density. As the temperature increases, a drop in drain current is observed because of the AlGaN/GaN lattice mismatch at the interface layer. But the threshold voltage varies with the doping concentration, thickness of the barrier layer, and mole concentration in AlGaN barrier. The threshold voltage (VTH) is increased to a more negative direction with the increase in buffer doping concentration and AlGaN thickness. For actual operation of the device, pinch-off was essentially governed by the gate leakage currents. The predominant off-state leakage current is due to vertical tunnelling of electrons from the gate metal to semiconductor. For the better pinch-off characteristics, low Al concentration and high Schottky barrier height are preferred. High-power microwave devices can be optimized with high Al content by achieving low trap centres, ohmic contacts with low resistance, and good quality crystals [27].

Under high drain bias conditions (V_DS &gt; 0), electrons are generated through side leakage and pass through the surface states of the active region, eventually leading to dislocation. Oxidizing localized dislocations greatly influence the CC [28]. In an off-state condition, the vertical and lateral field distribution in the buffer is more complicated, and dispersed traps were identified. The 2D electron gas progressively depleted while rising in vds. Under the drain field plate (FP), a strong vertical electric field is produced [29,30]. The method by which electrons move from the barrier to the passivation causes a strong band tilt when it is depleted [31]. The drain saturation current maintains at a minimum value for a duration of time until the traps start to release the caught electrons due to the traps established in the AlGaN layer that collect charges from the channel [32]. The strain in the inter layers and additional operational strain can be removed by choosing the lattice matched barrier layer.

2.4.4 Deep Level Traps

The deep levels are the energy states that an electron was given to conduction band, or it can accept from it. The traps present in the bandgap of the semiconductor with different energy states are separated by VB and CB. These traps can capture and release carriers with relatively high activation energies adversely affecting carrier lifetime and recombination dynamics. Buffer is a semi-insulating material used to reduce hot electron injection traps in deep levels. Buffers doped with Fe have small CC. The buffer layer is used to insulate the device and carriers channel confinement. To confine the channel carriers, the GaN buffer layer must be incorporated sufficiently to have high deep acceptors. To maximize the RF power performance of the transistor, a good buffer engineering compensation technique is needed to keep the position at the Fermi level close to conduction band in the upper half of the energy band [33]. Intrinsic or extrinsic deep donor or acceptor grown defects are the two types of doping methods that are employed, resulting in the Fermi level being stuck in the top region of the bandgap [34]. The Fe-doping concentration influences the deep levels in buffer [35]. Trap levels are examined by increasing the Fe-doping in the buffer. It is found that CC significantly depends on trap locations in the buffer. Fe doping causes an increase in the concentration of native GaN defects [36]. In RF operation conditions, GaN HEMTs are subjected to the most challenging conditions, including high-current charge density, strong electric field, and channel temperatures. RF operation impels clinker modes that did not observe during thermal and DC tests [37]. A high-resistive semi-insulating buffer enables high output power, improves breakdown voltages, and minimizes SCEs and buffer leakage [38]. Utilizing intrinsic growth defects to create deep donors and acceptors allows for buffer isolation [39]. High gate leakage current was observed for low-doped C (Low-C) compared to stepped-doped C and high-doped C (stepped-C and high-C) devices, hence, increase in AlGaN/GaN interface energy band slope. Electron tunnelling is responsible for gate leakage. Due to high-C, RF performance of the devices severely degraded, lowering output power and drain efficiency [24]. A nominal C-doping trap level is to be chosen [14]. A high-resistive GaN can be achieved by introducing a deep-level acceptor trap of Fe or C. Carbon doping is more dominant over Fe because it can be organized in step fashion great control over impurities in the channel [39,40]. High-C concentration enables high breakdown voltage but also forms floating p-n junction with 2D electron gas (between 2-DEG and buffer), leading to more CC [34,41,42]. At large electrical fields, GaN HEMT microwave output power is limited by the localized states in C-doped buffers. C concentration enables the traps by forming dislocations and trappings at the GaN/aluminium nitride (AlN) nucleation interface layer. By focussing more on the interaction of Carbon, the dislocations can be minimized for GaN HEMTs used in microwave applications. Dislocation densities can be minimized by using semi-insulating GaN substrates, and the C concentration can be controlled by the addition of C-carrying gas, rather changing growth parameters [43] (Figure 2.7).

To achieve high breakdown voltage without affecting its RF performance is to minimize acceptor trapping concentration. Leakage current is due to the following: (i) if the concentration of Si doping is higher than or comparable to that of acceptor doping and (ii) if acceptor concentration is high as that of Si doping. In the first case, leakage current is because of the Si-doping-induced excess electrons. In the second case, leakage current is because of the holes induced by acceptor traps. The holes-induced drain electrode is going to function in a strong electric field combined with the holes induced by acceptor doping that cumulatively increases leakage current [44]. High-frequency HEMT without buffer is grown on an epitaxial AlN nucleation layer [45] (Figure 2.8).

A linear graded negative polarization charges with Al concentration in AlGaN layer has been engineered from 25% to 16%. By introducing negative charges uniformly with a charge of −0.2505 C/cm3 and a GaN/AlGaN interface polarization charge of −1.33 μC/cm2, background ionized donors are compensated, which are positively charged, causing the CB to stretch away from the Fermi energy level [46]. But deep-level traps suppress the leakage current in buffer and SCEs, and these unwanted buffer traps/deep-level traps would be minimized by exclusion of thick buffer.

2.4.5 Surface Traps

These defects are present on the surface of the semiconductors. These traps can lead to charge carrier recombination and scattering, affecting the speed and efficiency of the devices [47]. CC happens when a GaN HEMT device is overdriven because of the surface negative charge present between the gate and drain [48]. This surface traps behave as though there is a virtual gate in addition to the intrinsic gate by extending the depletion region at the access region [11,12,48]. The virtual gate effect is caused by hot electrons from a channel, or electrons pushed from a gate surface trapping [47,49]. Hot electrons induce lattice defects, or traps will degrade the reliability of GaN transistors. The electrons generated due to high drain bias (hot electrons) enhance the trapping effects and cause to form new trapping/de-trapping process since these trapped charges influence the electric field (hot electron equivalent temperature), leading to affect the static and RF performance of AlGaN/GaN HEMTs. Electroluminescence (EL) measurement at various gate-bias values and drain bias reveals the effects of hot electrons [8]. Hot electrons limit the output power delivered at RF frequencies and may eventually lead to the phenomenon called negative differential output conductance (NDC) at low drain bias. The threshold voltage positive shift and decreased drain current were observed in on-state. In off-state breakdown, the electron-hole pairs (EHPs) are generated due to impact ionization. In the holes flowing toward negatively charged gate, few of them were trapped in the surface passivation layer. Because of discontinuity at valence band in the SiNx/AlGaN/GaN interface, transfer of holes to silicon nitride consequently rises in drain current and results in more negative threshold voltage shift. The high field is concentrated at gate edge of the drain side when the drain bias is larger than the pinch-off voltage. This strong electric field produces hot electrons due to high potential, and these were extended into barrier layer captured by AlGaN surface traps. With the increase of drain stress voltage and pulse time, the virtual gate gradually extends and degrades in drain current. This CC is eliminated by the following: (a) surface passivation may be used to reduce AlGaN surface trap density; (b) adding donor doping at AlGaN surface to compensate surface traps; and (c) the high electric field present at drain side can be suppressed by using different types of FP configurations. The FPs may be source FP, gate FP, double gate FP, drain FP, and extension of these FPs toward the access region. Unfortunately, the FPs incorporate additional internal capacitances, affecting the RF performance of the devices. Surface traps are the dominating factor in producing CC, and the upper channel gate to drain region has many mobile electrons to screen lower channel potential fluctuations.

2.4.6 Dislocation Traps

The localized energy states that function as traps can be produced by crystal defects and dislocations in the material lattice structure. An increase in Al doping between the discontinuities in conduction band and doping densities interface is most responsible for the high charge carrier density. As the temperature increases, there is a drop in drain current observed due to lattice mismatch at the AlGaN/GaN interface layer. But the threshold voltage varies with the thickness of the barrier layer and Al-mole concentration in the AlGaN barrier. For actual operation of the device, pinch-off characteristics are mainly governed by the gate leakage currents. The vertical tunnelling of electrons from the gate metal to semiconductor causes predominant off-state gate leakage current. For the better pinch-off characteristics, low Al concentration and high Schottky barrier height are preferred. High power microwave devices can be optimized with high Al content by achieving low trap centres, ohmic contacts with low resistance, and good quality crystals [27]. Multiple FPs were implemented to further improve the robustness and increased power density. The FP integrated with the gate electrode is used for reducing the electron traps and gate resistance. The FPs integrated with drain terminal are used for tailoring the high electric field and for attaining high breakdown voltages [29,30].

2.4.7 Radiation-Induced Traps

Cosmic rays and X-rays can introduce defects within the semiconductor material, which can lead to traps. The devices degrade rapidly under DC operating conditions than when there is no DC bias [48]. Neutron radiation introduces defects, which can decrease the 2D electron gas and mobility at the semiconductor/semiconductor, semiconductor/insulator, spacer, and buffer layers [50]. The space charge region is altered in the AlGaN layer by the introduction of acceptor-like charged defects. The deep-level defects below the Fermi level are negatively charged. This makes band tilting in the high electron mobility transistor devices and bands goes up with respect to the Fermi level and hence results in reduced 2D electron gas density, and this reduces drain current [51] (Figure 2.9).

An increase in gate current may result from traps acting as tunnelling and are located within the bandgap and close to the AlGaN/metal interface. The thermionic emission (TE) and electron-hole generation-recombination (G-R) process have negligible impact on gate leakage current. A consistent distribution of point defects like vacancies, interstitials, and Frenkel pairs as well as certain deeper damage cascades are produced throughout the epitaxial layers by irradiation with 2 MeV protons [52]. In particular, when proton irradiation of 1.8 MeV with fluences up to 1014 cm−2 is used under the gate region, the traps result in both permanent vth-shifts and dynamic vth-dispersion that can have a considerable impact on the properties of the device. The balanced trapping/de-trapping rates during off-state conditions determine the overall increase in Dynamic-RON. The devices prove a substantial reduction in Dynamic-RON at higher proton irradiation fluences higher than 1013cm-2. The Dynamic-RON is completely suppressed at a drain bias voltage (VDS) of 600 V/150 °C at highest fluences (1.5 × 1014 cm−2) [53,54]. Physical defects incorporated during the fabrication process result in surface donor traps. The intentional Fe-doped (i-Fe) deep traps are used to provide deep acceptors to capture free electrons and to provide semi-insulating behaviour to the buffer layer [54] (Figure 2.10).

HEMT space charges ± σpolarization, σ2-D electron gas, σholes and σdonor trap ionization [55].

where

  1. ± σpolarization is polarization-induced dipole charges.
  2. σ2D-electron gas is charge due to the 2D electron gas.
  3. σholes is charge due to holes accumulation at the AlGaN surface.
  4. Σdonor trap ionization is the charge due to donor trap ionization.

A large drain current is likely to result in the kink effect, the trapping/de-trapping of electrons captured by deep traps that are present in the buffer at the interface. The kink effect is due to the traps situating between drain edges of the gate electrode in the GaN buffer layer. The kink effect is generated by hot electron trappings of donor-like ionized deep level traps in the buffer layer [56]. Several traps are located above the channel, in the AlGaN barrier or the surface, and in the buffer. Buffer traps are temperature independent, but AlGaN traps are thermally activated [57]. The presence of passivation effectively eliminates the condition of a significant transconductance decrement. The high energetic electrons cause an impact on ionization. The hot electron traps have been characterized by examining the gate current due to huge gathering of holes by the impact of the ionization phenomenon [8,57]. The electron charge sheet density decreases in the channel because of the trapped electrons injected into the buffer layer. A current reduction takes place because of the slow response of deep level traps in the semi-insulating buffer (Figure 2.11).

Different types of interface charges are as follows: (a) positive polarization charges, (b) negative ionized acceptor-like traps, (c) negatively charged electrons. The group III-nitride metal–insulator–semiconductor heterostructures wide bandgap properties lead to moderately deep interface traps with lengthy emission time constant (τe), which restrict the measurable energy depth at room temperature.

2.5 Measurement Techniques of Traps

Different trap measurement methods are listed below:

  1. Pulsed I–V measurement.
  2. Drain-lag and gate-lag measurement.
  3. The deep-level transient spectroscopy (DLTS) measurement.
  4. The capacitance–voltage (C–V) measurement.

2.5.1 Pulsed Current–Voltage Measurement

This method characterizes the drain current by applying the quiescent bias conditions (vgsq, vdsq). By measuring the rate of change of the drain current, it can be accomplished to determine the rate of the electron-emitting charge (releasing) and capturing time, e and Ctun (electron tunnelling). The temperature and the trap level’s position with respect to the CB’s bottom influence the emission probability. The triangular-shaped AlGaN barrier layer is responsible for large gate electron trap tunnelling, whereas the trap-assisted tunnelling depends on the surface trap location, the energy levels of traps, and the field applied. Multiple forms rely on the measurement of the functional temperature dependency of the release and capture rates of transient spectroscopy, from which the trap activation energy and its capture cross-section can be determined [56,58,59].

Pulsed measurement eliminates device self-heating that is used to distinguish between the trapping and thermal effects. Higher VDS under pulse bias avoids the device to get permanent degrade condition.

2.5.2 Gate-Lag and Drain-Lag Measurement

Gate-lag measurement is the study of the gate voltage variation produced by delayed drain current characteristics of the device [8]. Gate lag is used to analyse the transient drain current consequences. A delay in the channel current’s response to a change in the gate potential is a result of charge trapping in the device’s structure. Gate-lag studies give vital details on the traps that are necessary for identifying the active flaws. Gate lag is measured by a gate pulse of short duration that is applied from pinch-off to on-state with drain voltage at fixed voltage [9]. In gate-lag measurement technique, a pulse of a certain period with a duration is applied at the gate electrode by keeping VDS constant (fixed VDS). Similarly, a drain lag is a measurement technique where a pulse is applied to the drain electrode, keeping the gate bias VGS at a fixed value. Gate lag is defined as the rate of change in drain current that produces a current delay. Because the hole accumulation along the surface cancels, the traps are responsible for the gate lag, and furthermore, the device is unaffected by acceptor traps [60]. The density of 2D electron gas can be altered by surface donor-like traps that are filled and removed. Gate lag happens since the surface trapping affect acts as an electron trap. The dipole charges originate from the polarization-induced charges, and 2D electron gas charges are present in the space charge components, charges due to donor-like trap ionization [61]. The charge is driven on by a collection of holes close to the AlGaN surface. The electrons that immediately tunnel into the Schottky gate contact when a new device undergoes stress can occupy the donor-like traps and make them neutral; when the total number of donor-like traps decreases; 2D electron gas density decreases as well, leading to a decrease in initial drain current (Figure 2.12).

The Poole–Frenkel (PF) effect emission model is used to analyse the field-dependent reverse gate leakage characteristics. Photon-assisted tunnelling (PAT) and direct tunnelling (DT) emission are a slow process, owing to activation emission process [60, 61, 62, 63]. By keeping the gate bias fixed and the channel open, the turn-on pulsating mode of the drain current tends to drop with time, and a decreased drain current is measured in the turn-off pulsating mode after the drain voltage returns to 0 V [14]. The surface traps leading to drastic decrease in drain current in turn affect the microwave characteristics, which lowers output power [62]. A small number of the electrons may tunnel through the barrier during the on-state and get captured by the deep-level trap. The electron traps in the channel causes the drain current reduction. Owing to the filling process enabled by hot electrons, a portion of the channel’s electrons is drained into the buffer layer when a high drain voltage is applied, making it easier for the deep-level traps to capture electrons [63,64]. The trapping process is achieved by empty acceptor traps that have the ability to emit holes or capture electrons, and the de-trapping process is achieved by filled acceptor traps that have the ability to emit electrons or capture holes [65]. The interface charges produce polarization that is responsible for hot electron buffer traps [66]. By the increase in drain bias, the hot electrons (heated up) in the channel are injected into the buffer due to large gate edge electric field. Drain lag is a decay in drain current with slow current transient response when VDS is applied. If the voltage within the pulse is higher when compared with the quiescent bias point, traps capture free electrons and traps release their charges when this voltage is lower than the quiescent bias (Figure 2.13).

The surface traps leading to drastic decrease in drain current in turn affect the microwave characteristics, which lowers the output power of the device. The DLTS measurement: the defects are most likely caused by a lattice mismatch parameter and strain-induced co-efficiency between different GaN epitaxial layers and the substrate when GaN is grown epitaxial. These defects may tend to propagate into the AlGaN layer [67]. To analyse the origin of these defects, the DLTS technique is used. A constant gate voltage with a pulse width is applied to measure the drain current and vds [68]. Current-DLTS measurement is based on the channel temperature-varied transient drain current response. Trap activation energy and cross-sectional information are precisely achieved by using this technique. Current-DLTS measurement is used to characterize the traps under semi-on stress state. When the device is stressed from the state-off to semi-on stress state, the generated hot electrons in the conducting channel being forced to buffer (AlGaN) get trapped by deep traps present in buffer. Capacitance-DLTS measurement is used to analyse the sudden change in voltage produced by transient gate capacitance. This method provides the information of variations of trap properties under the gate terminal. This method does not provide the precise measurements for the small gate area devices [8] (Figure 2.14).

2.5.3 The Capacitance–Voltage Measurement

The C–V measurement is used to measure the C–V characteristics by applying a gate voltage with the frequency of operation. A drain pulse with small time duration is applied to reach the specified final value. After stabilizing the pulse, the gate voltage is forced to move to the RB (reverse bias) in deep pinch-off and then pushed to FB condition. Keeping the longer transition duration allows to gradually shift and capture the response of the device. If the gate voltage is less than the threshold voltage, the 2D electron gas at the ALGaN barrier/GaN channel interface is pinch-off with channel density. By keeping the vertical depletion region constant, the barrier is extended into the buffer. When the device is turned on, channel charge density starts to grow. The gate capacitance starts rising as the channel density starts building from pinch-off and causes a reduced effective barrier. The depletion width edge gradually shifts to the AlGaN barrier/GaN channel interface. A point is reached where this is equal to the threshold voltage. This is the point where the depletion width is equal to the background doping. If the gate capacitance reaches the peak value, it indicates a strong channel conduction. A further increase in gate voltage, exhibiting a slight decay in gate capacitance, indicates that the channel gets saturated, and the depletion region is growing toward source terminal. The C–V curves exhibit a sharp change in variation of drain bias. This causes an extension of the depletion layer in 2D electron gas regions [69].

2.6 Conclusion

The promising properties of GaN devices motivating the device technology led to large signal applications. The GaN high electron mobility transistor power devices are useful for high switching speed and high-power requirement. In this discussion, we studied the origin for the trap generation. The DC and RF characteristics are disturbed due to unwanted traps. Traps are produced due to the physical technology process. To remove the traps, different engineering techniques have been implemented. Gate engineering techniques are used to control the gate leakage and control of electrons within the device. By the implementation of various techniques, the linearity and noise performance can be improved; hence, the devices are used in communication systems and a wide range of radar applications. A good buffer design can control the traps by a proper doping design. Surface traps can be removed by proper passivation. Barrier traps can be removed with barrier layer engineering. Strain engineering has been used to increase transistor speed while reducing the number of traps by employing buffer layers. A trade-off should be done in a transistor design to increase the reliability by reducing traps. Finally, we discussed trap measurement techniques.

  64. K. Horio and A. Nakajima, “Physical mechanism of buffer-related current transients and current slump in AlGaN/GaN high electron mobility transistors,” Jpn. J. Appl. Phys., vol. 47, no. 5 PART 1, pp. 3428–3433, 2008, doi: 10.1143/JJAP.47.3428.
  65. W. D. Hu, X. S. Chen, and W. Lu, “Intrinsic mechanism of drain-lag and current collapse in GaN-based HEMTs,” IEEE Trans. Microw. Theory Tech., pp. 9–11, 2009, doi: 10.1109/WMED.2009.4816142.
  66. W. D. Hu, X. S. Chen, F. Yin, J. B. Zhang, and W. Lu, “Two-dimensional transient simulations of drain lag and current collapse in GaN-based high-electron-mobility transistors,” J. Appl. Phys., vol. 105, no. 8, 2009, doi: 10.1063/1.3106603.
  67. X. A. Cao, J. M. Teetsov, M. P. D’Evelyn, D. W. Merfeld, and C. H. Van, “Electrical characteristics of InGaN/GaN light-emitting diodes grown on GaN and sapphire substrates,” Appl. Phys. Lett., vol. 85, no. 1, pp. 7–9, 2004, doi: 10.1063/1.1767280.
