6 Silicon Chip Design and Testing

Shasanka Sekhar Rout and Jagana Bihari Padhy

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-6

6.1 Introduction

VLSI plays a crucial role in silicon chip design by enabling high levels of integration, handling complexity, improving performance, facilitating miniaturization, enabling system integration, enhancing design productivity, and serving as an enabler for innovation in the semiconductor industry. Its impact is pervasive across various application domains, ranging from consumer electronics and telecommunications to automotive and healthcare. VLSI also plays a critical role in testing by enabling the design of testable chips, implementing efficient testing methodologies, analyzing test coverage, developing test programs, and conducting comprehensive testing to ensure the quality and reliability of semiconductor devices [1]. Silicon chip design and testing lie at the heart of the semiconductor industry, driving innovation and enabling the technological advancements that shape our modern world [2]. From the microprocessors powering our computers to the integrated circuits in our smartphones, silicon chips play a pivotal role in countless electronic devices. This chapter delves into the intricacies of silicon chip design and the rigorous testing processes that ensure their functionality and reliability. From the inception of an idea to the final integration into functional systems, this chapter provides an in-depth exploration of the stages involved in designing and testing silicon chips, which is shown in Figure 6.1.

Absolutely, the chapter on semiconductor chip design and testing provides a comprehensive examination of various testing methodologies employed throughout the semiconductor manufacturing process. From the initial stages of wafer testing to the final quality assurance measures, each step is crucial in ensuring the functionality, reliability, and performance of semiconductor chips. Furthermore, the chapter underscores the tangible impact of chip design and testing on shaping the landscape of modern technology. Semiconductor chips are the backbone of countless electronic devices, and their quality and reliability are paramount in fostering innovation and enhancing the efficiency of various industries.

6.2 Specification and Architecture Design

At the outset of a silicon chip project, engineers embark on a journey to define the chip’s specifications and architectural design. This phase involves close collaboration between design teams and stakeholders to articulate the desired functionalities and performance metrics of the chip. Through feasibility studies and architectural exploration, engineers establish the groundwork for subsequent design stages. Specification and architecture design is a critical phase in the development of silicon chips, laying the groundwork for subsequent stages of the design process. This phase involves defining the requirements and high-level structure of the chip to ensure that it meets the desired functionality, performance, and power targets [3,4].

Example: Consider the development of a microcontroller for Internet of Things (IoT) devices. Engineers collaborate with stakeholders to define requirements such as processing power, memory, and connectivity. Architectural decisions are made regarding the type of processor core, peripherals, and communication protocols to be integrated into the chip.

The block diagram shown in Figure 6.2 provides a high-level overview of the major functional blocks and their interconnections within the silicon chip, illustrating the architecture and design considerations during the specification and architecture design phase [5].

Input/Output Interfaces: These interfaces are for communication with external devices, such as sensors, actuators, and communication modules.

Processing Unit: This block represents the central processing unit (CPU) or digital signal processor (DSP) responsible for executing instructions and performing computational tasks.

Memory Subsystem: This includes various types of memory components, such as cache memory, RAM, and Read-Only Memory (ROM), for storing data and instructions.

Peripheral Controllers: These controllers are for managing peripheral devices, such as timers, DMA controllers, and other input/output peripherals.

Communication Interfaces: These interfaces are for communication with other devices or systems, including Universal Asynchronous Receiver-Transmitter (UART), Ethernet, USB, and wireless communication protocols.

Control Logic and Data Paths: These logic circuits are responsible for controlling the operation of the chip and managing data flow between different functional blocks.

Power Management Unit and Clock Distribution: This controls power distribution and manages clock signals within the chip to optimize energy efficiency and ensure proper timing.

Test and Debug Features: This includes features designed for testing, debugging, and validation purposes, such as test access ports (TAPs), built-in self-test (BIST) circuits, debug interfaces, and security mechanisms.

6.3 Logic Design and Synthesis

With specifications in hand, engineers delve into the logic design phase, where they conceptualize the digital circuitry that will implement the desired functionalities. Leveraging hardware description languages (HDLs), such as Verilog or VHSIC Hardware Description Language (VHSIC stands for Very High-Speed Integrated Circuit) (VHDL), designers craft intricate logic structures, employing a mix of combinatorial and sequential elements. Once the logic design is complete, synthesis tools map the design onto the target silicon technology, optimizing for factors like area, power consumption, and timing [6].

Logic design and synthesis are fundamental stages in silicon chip development, where the conceptual design is translated into a detailed digital circuit implementation. This phase involves converting high-level descriptions of desired functionalities into logic-level representations, optimizing for performance, area, and power consumption. The fundamental stages involved in this phase are as follows.

  1. Hardware Description Languages (HDLs): Engineers use hardware description languages, such as Verilog or VHDL, to describe the behavior and structure of digital circuits. HDLs allow designers to specify logical operations, timing constraints, and interconnections between different components.
  2. Logic Design: Logic design involves conceptualizing the digital circuitry required to implement the desired functionalities of the chip. Designers create logic diagrams, state diagrams, and truth tables to represent the logical relationships and operations within the circuit.
  3. RTL (Register Transfer Level) Design: RTL design focuses on specifying the behavior of the digital circuit at the register transfer level, describing how data is transferred between registers and how operations are performed. Designers define the behavior of individual logic elements, registers, and interconnections using RTL constructs [7].
  4. Component Selection: Designers select appropriate digital components, such as logic gates, flip-flops, multiplexers, and arithmetic units, to implement the desired functionality. Component selection is based on factors such as performance requirements, area constraints, and power consumption considerations.
  5. Optimization Techniques: Various optimization techniques are applied to improve the efficiency and performance of the digital circuit. Techniques include logic optimization, timing optimization, area optimization, and power optimization.
  6. Synthesis: Synthesis is the process of transforming the RTL description of the digital circuit into a gate-level netlist. Synthesis tools analyze the RTL code and map it onto the target technology library, selecting appropriate logic gates and optimizing the circuit for area, timing, and power.
  7. Technology Mapping: Technology mapping involves mapping the synthesized logic onto specific technology libraries, which contain information about the available logic cells, flip-flops, and interconnect resources. Designers select the optimal mapping based on factors such as performance requirements, area constraints, and power targets.
  8. Timing Analysis: Timing analysis ensures that the synthesized circuit meets the required timing constraints, such as setup time, hold time, and clock frequency. Designers use timing analysis tools to simulate the behavior of the circuit and identify any timing violations that need to be addressed.
  9. Verification: Verification is performed to ensure that the synthesized circuit behaves correctly according to the specified requirements. Designers use simulation, formal verification, and other techniques to verify the functional correctness of the synthesized logic.
  10. Iterative Refinement: The logic design and synthesis process often involve iterative refinement, where designers make incremental improvements to the design based on simulation results, synthesis reports, and feedback from verification.

Example: Using Verilog HDL, designers create the logic circuits necessary to implement the desired functionalities of the microcontroller. This includes designing arithmetic logic units (ALUs), memory controllers, and input/output interfaces. Synthesis tools then map this logical design onto the chosen silicon technology, optimizing for factors like power consumption and speed.

6.4 Physical Design

The transition from logical abstraction to physical realization marks the onset of the physical design phase. Engineers grapple with the intricacies of floor planning, placement, and routing, striving to optimize the layout for manufacturability and performance. Advanced algorithms and tools aid in the placement of millions of components on the silicon die, while routing algorithms navigate the complex maze of interconnections, mindful of signal integrity and power distribution. The physical design flow is a sequence of steps involved in transforming a logical circuit representation into a physical layout that can be manufactured on silicon [8,9]. The overview of physical design flow is summarized in Figure 6.3. The Place and Route (PnR) flow mainly follows these stages: Design planning/Import/Partitioning => Floorplan & Powerplan => Placement => CTS => Routing => Sign Off.

  1. Import Design:

    The following input file information is loaded into the PnR tool.

    1. Netlist (.v/.vhd/.edif)
    2. Physical Libraries (.lef)
    3. Timing Libraries (.lib)
    4. Technology File
    5. Constraints (.sdc)
    6. IO Info. File
    7. Power Spec. File
    8. Optimization Directives
    9. Clock Tree Spec. File
    10. DEF/FP
  2. Floorplanning: The process begins with floorplanning, where designers allocate space on the silicon die for different functional blocks and components. Constraints such as chip size, power grid, signal routing, and thermal considerations are taken into account during floorplanning.
  3. Placement: Placement involves positioning the individual components (cells) within the allocated chip area. Algorithms place cells based on optimization objectives such as minimizing wire length, meeting timing constraints, and avoiding congestion.
  4. Clock Tree Synthesis (CTS): Clock tree synthesis generates a hierarchical network of clock distribution lines to ensure synchronized timing signals across the chip. CTS aims to minimize clock skew and ensure consistent clock arrival times at all clocked elements.
  5. Routing: Routing connects the placed components with wires to establish functional connections according to the logical design. Global routing determines the paths of major interconnects, while detailed routing focuses on individual wires and signal paths.
  6. Power Distribution Network (PDN) Design: The PDN design involves creating a network of power supply lines and distribution capacitors to ensure stable power delivery to all components. PDN design aims to minimize voltage drop and power distribution noise to maintain reliable operation of the chip.
  7. Timing Closure: Timing closure ensures that the physical design meets the required timing constraints specified during synthesis. Techniques such as buffering, resizing, and optimization are applied to achieve timing closure and meet the desired performance targets.
  8. Physical Verification: Physical verification checks the layout against design rules, ensuring compliance with manufacturing constraints and standards. DRC (design rule check), LVS (layout vs. schematic), and ERC (electrical rule check) are performed to identify and resolve layout errors.
  9. Design for Manufacturability (DFM): DFM techniques are applied to enhance manufacturability and yield of the chip. Techniques include lithography-aware layout optimization, metal density control, and spacing rules adjustment to address manufacturing challenges [10].
  10. Signoff: After completing physical design and verification, the layout undergoes signoff to ensure readiness for manufacturing. Designers review the layout, perform final checks, and generate design files for fabrication.

Example: Physical design engineers tackle challenges such as floor planning, placement, and routing to ensure efficient use of silicon real estate. For our microcontroller example, considerations include the proximity of memory to the processor core for faster access and the placement of input/output pins for optimal connectivity.

6.5 Verification

Verification is a cornerstone of silicon chip design, ensuring that the implemented design behaves as intended. Engineers employ a plethora of verification techniques, ranging from simulation at various levels of abstraction to formal verification methods. Through exhaustive testing, designers uncover and rectify design flaws, striving for correctness and robustness before proceeding to manufacturing [11].

Verification is a critical stage in the silicon chip design process, aimed at ensuring that the chip functions correctly and meets the specified requirements before it is manufactured. The overview of the verification process is as follows.

  1. Functional Verification: Functional verification verifies that the chip behaves as intended and performs the desired functions correctly. Techniques include simulation, emulation, and formal verification to validate the behavior of the chip against the functional specifications.
  2. Simulation-Based Verification: Simulation involves running tests on a software model of the chip (RTL simulation) or on an actual prototype (gate-level simulation) to verify its functionality. Testbenches, which are sets of stimuli and expected responses, are used to simulate various scenarios and verify the chip’s behavior.
  3. Emulation: Emulation uses specialized hardware platforms called emulators to run the chip design at near-real-time speeds. Emulation is used for testing large and complex designs that cannot be efficiently simulated on traditional simulators.
  4. Formal Verification: Formal verification uses mathematical techniques to formally prove the correctness of the chip design. Model checking, theorem proving, and equivalence checking are common formal verification methods used to analyze the chip’s behavior against its specifications.
  5. Code Coverage Analysis: Code coverage analysis measures the extent to which the chip’s design has been exercised by the verification tests. Coverage metrics such as statement coverage, branch coverage, and condition coverage help assess the completeness of the verification effort.
  6. Assertion-Based Verification: Assertions are statements embedded in the RTL code that specify expected behavior or constraints. Assertion-based verification checks whether these assertions hold true during simulation, helping to detect violations of design intent.
  7. Testbench Automation: Testbenches are automated to generate stimuli, execute tests, and analyze results efficiently. Verification environments such as UVM (universal verification methodology) provide a standardized framework for testbench development and management.
  8. Gate-Level Simulation: Gate-level simulation verifies the functionality of the chip’s synthesized netlist at the gate level. Timing information, delays, and signal propagation are considered in gate-level simulation to assess the chip’s timing behavior.
  9. Performance Verification: Performance verification ensures that the chip meets timing, power, and area targets specified during the design phase. Timing analysis, power analysis, and physical verification checks are performed to validate performance metrics.
  10. Integration Testing: Integration testing verifies the interaction and compatibility of individual chip components when integrated into larger systems. Interface protocols, communication protocols, and system-level functionality are tested to ensure seamless integration.

Example: Simulation is crucial at this stage to verify that the design meets specifications. Engineers create test benches and use simulations to verify functionality under various conditions. For our microcontroller, this involves testing different instructions, memory access patterns, and communication protocols to ensure correct operation.

6.6 Manufacturing

The manufacturing phase heralds the transformation of design blueprints into tangible silicon wafers. Foundries employ cutting-edge lithography techniques to etch intricate patterns onto silicon substrates, laying the foundation for the chip’s physical realization. Doping, etching, deposition, and metallization processes sculpt the silicon into functional circuits, culminating in the birth of countless chips on a single wafer, which is shown in Figure 6.4 [12,13]. The manufacturing process can be represented as follows:

  1. Wafer Preparation: Silicon wafers are prepared by slicing a cylindrical ingot of semiconductor-grade silicon into thin, circular wafers. These wafers undergo polishing and cleaning processes to remove surface imperfections and contaminants.
  2. Wafer Cleaning: Wafer surfaces are thoroughly cleaned using chemical and mechanical processes to remove any particles, residues, or contaminants that could affect the fabrication process.
  3. Thin Film Deposition: Thin films of various materials, such as silicon dioxide (SiO2), silicon nitride (Si3N4), and metal layers, are deposited onto the wafer surface using techniques like chemical vapor deposition (CVD) or physical vapor deposition (PVD).
  4. Photolithography: Photolithography is a key process for defining the intricate patterns of the chip’s circuitry on the wafer surface. A photosensitive material called photoresist is applied to the wafer, exposed to UV light through a photomask containing the desired circuit patterns, and developed to transfer the patterns onto the wafer.
  5. Etching: Etching processes selectively remove material from the wafer surface based on the pattern defined by the photoresist. Wet etching and dry etching techniques are used to etch away unwanted material layers, leaving behind the desired circuit features.
  6. Doping: Doping introduces impurities into specific regions of the silicon wafer to modify its electrical properties and create semiconductor junctions. Dopants such as boron, phosphorus, and arsenic are diffused or implanted into the wafer to create p-type and n-type regions required for transistor formation.
  7. Ion Implantation: Ion implantation is a precise doping technique where dopant ions are accelerated and implanted into the wafer surface at controlled depths using high-energy beams. Ion implantation allows for precise control over dopant concentration and distribution within the semiconductor substrate.
  8. Annealing: Annealing processes heat the wafer to high temperatures to activate dopants, repair crystal lattice defects, and relieve stress induced during fabrication processes. Rapid thermal annealing (RTA) and furnace annealing are commonly used to achieve the desired electrical and structural properties of the semiconductor material.
  9. Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP): CMP is used to planarize the wafer surface and remove any uneven topography resulting from previous fabrication steps. Abrasive slurries and mechanical polishing pads are employed to achieve a smooth and flat wafer surface suitable for subsequent processing.
  10. Metallization and Interconnect Formation: Metal layers, typically aluminum or copper, are deposited onto the wafer surface to form interconnects that connect individual components of the chip’s circuitry. Dielectric layers such as silicon dioxide are deposited and patterned to insulate the metal interconnects and provide electrical isolation between different circuit layers.
  11. Back-End-of-Line (BEOL) Processing: BEOL processing involves the formation of metal interconnects, vias, and bond pads that connect the chip’s active components to external connections such as package pins. Insulating layers and passivation coatings are applied to protect the chip’s surface and improve reliability.
  12. Testing and Quality Assurance: The manufactured chips undergo rigorous testing to ensure they meet the specified electrical, functional, and reliability requirements. Wafer-level testing and final package testing are performed to screen for defects, verify functionality, and ensure that high-quality chips are shipped to customers [14].
  13. Packaging: Packaged chips are encapsulated in protective materials such as ceramic or plastic packages to shield them from environmental hazards and provide mechanical support. Packaging also includes wire bonding or flip-chip bonding to connect the chip to the package leads or external connectors.
  14. Final Testing and Burn-In: Packaged chips undergo final testing and burn-in to verify their functionality and reliability under operating conditions. Burn-in involves subjecting the chips to elevated temperatures and electrical stresses to accelerate aging and detect early failures.
  15. Quality Control and Packaging: After passing final testing, the chips are sorted, labeled, and packaged into trays or tape-and-reel formats for shipment to customers. Quality control measures ensure that only chips meeting the specified criteria are packaged and shipped, maintaining high standards of reliability and performance.

Example: Once the design is verified, it moves to the manufacturing phase. The microcontroller design is translated into a series of photolithographic masks used to pattern the silicon wafer. Semiconductor fabrication facilities (fabs) employ advanced manufacturing processes to etch, deposit, and layer materials onto the wafer, creating the intricate circuitry of the microcontroller.

6.7 Testing

Testing is a critical juncture in the lifecycle of a silicon chip, encompassing a spectrum of tests to ensure functionality, quality, and reliability. Wafer-level testing scrutinizes chips, identifying defects and anomalies before packaging. Subsequent package-level testing subjects individual chips to rigorous examination, encompassing functional, electrical, and reliability tests. Through exhaustive testing regimes, manufacturers safeguard the integrity and performance of their silicon chips [15,16]. Absolutely, wafer-level testing is a critical step in the semiconductor manufacturing process, which includes the following stages.

  1. Purpose: Wafer-level testing is conducted to assess the quality and functionality of individual chips on a silicon wafer before they are separated and packaged. By testing chips in bulk, manufacturers can efficiently identify defects and anomalies early in the production process, thereby reducing waste and improving yield.
  2. Testing Equipment: Specialized testing equipment, such as wafer probers or automatic test equipment (ATE), is used to perform electrical tests on the entire wafer. These tests typically involve applying various signals and stimuli to the chips and measuring their responses to assess functionality and performance [14].
  3. Test Patterns: Test patterns are programmed into the testing equipment to evaluate different aspects of chip functionality, including logic operations, memory integrity, and input/output functionality. Manufacturers may use pre-defined test patterns or develop custom patterns tailored to the specific design and requirements of the chips being tested.
  4. Defect Identification: During wafer-level testing, defective chips are identified based on deviations from expected test results. Common defects include shorts, opens, excessive leakage, and performance variations. Advanced testing techniques, such as BIST and scan testing, help detect and diagnose these defects accurately.
  5. Data Analysis: Test data collected during wafer-level testing is analyzed to assess overall wafer yield and identify trends or patterns that may indicate underlying process issues. Statistical analysis tools are often employed to extract valuable insights from large volumes of test data, enabling manufacturers to optimize production processes and improve quality control.
  6. Yield Enhancement: The primary goal of wafer-level testing is to maximize yield by identifying and segregating defective chips from the good ones. Manufacturers may employ techniques like binning, where chips are sorted into different quality categories based on test results to optimize resource allocation and ensure that only high-quality chips proceed to the next stage of the manufacturing process.
  7. Feedback Loop: Insights gained from wafer-level testing contribute to continuous improvement efforts in semiconductor manufacturing. By analyzing test data and identifying root causes of defects, manufacturers can implement corrective actions to enhance process reliability, reduce defect rates, and ultimately improve product quality.

Testing is significant and multifaceted, encompassing various aspects of semiconductor testing processes, which can be explained as follows [17,18].

  1. Testability Features in Design: VLSI designers incorporate testability features into chip designs to facilitate testing at later stages. These features include BIST structures, scan chains, boundary scan cells, and other design-for-testability (DFT) techniques. BIST structures enable on-chip test pattern generation and result verification, while scan chains facilitate serial access to internal chip nodes for testing purposes.
  2. Scan-Based Testing: VLSI chips often employ scan-based testing methodologies, where test patterns are serially shifted into and out of the chip through scan chains. This allows for efficient testing of sequential and combinational logic elements within the chip. Scan-based testing is widely used for manufacturing testing, as well as for post-manufacturing in-system testing (IST) and field testing.
  3. Functional Testing: VLSI chips undergo functional testing to verify correct operation under normal operating conditions. Functional tests are designed to validate the chip’s functionality against its intended specifications, ensuring that it performs the required operations accurately and reliably. Functional testing may involve the execution of test vectors on the chip’s inputs and monitoring its outputs for correct responses.
  4. Structural Testing: VLSI chips are subjected to structural testing to detect and diagnose manufacturing defects and faults within the chip’s internal components. Structural testing techniques, such as stuck-at fault testing, transition fault testing, and delay fault testing, aim to identify faulty transistors, interconnects, and other circuit elements that may impact chip performance or reliability.
  5. Fault Coverage Analysis: VLSI testing involves analyzing the effectiveness of test patterns in detecting faults and defects within the chip. Fault coverage analysis assesses the percentage of faults that are detected by the test patterns, providing insights into the quality of the test set and the overall test strategy. VLSI designers and test engineers use fault coverage metrics to optimize test patterns and improve test effectiveness.
  6. Automated Test Equipment (ATE): VLSI chips are tested using specialized automated test equipment (ATE), such as semiconductor testers or ATE systems. ATE systems generate test patterns, apply them to the chip under test, capture and analyze the chip’s responses, and report test results. VLSI engineers develop test programs and test vectors for ATE systems to conduct various tests, including functional tests, scan tests, and structural tests.
  7. Post-Silicon Validation: VLSI chips undergo post-silicon validation to verify their performance and functionality in real-world operating conditions. Post-silicon validation involves testing chips in actual system environments, such as evaluation boards or prototype systems, to ensure compatibility, reliability, and performance. VLSI engineers use a combination of hardware and software tools to conduct post-silicon validation tests and debug chip issues [12].

Example: After manufacturing, the silicon wafers undergo testing to identify defective chips. This involves wafer probing, where each chip’s functionality is tested while still on the wafer. For our microcontroller, tests include checking processor functionality, memory integrity, and input/output operations.

6.8 Packaging and Quality Assurance

Packaging encapsulates chips in protective casings, providing electrical connections and safeguarding against environmental stressors. Quality assurance measures permeate every facet of packaging and testing, ensuring adherence to stringent standards and regulatory requirements. Through meticulous quality control, manufacturers uphold the reliability and consistency of their chip offerings.

Example: Functional chips are then packaged into protective enclosures. Quality assurance measures ensure that each packaged chip meets performance and reliability standards. For the microcontroller, packaging considerations include thermal management, ESD protection, and ensuring proper electrical connections.

6.9 Integration and System Testing

The culmination of the silicon chip journey occurs with integration into larger systems, where chips synergize to deliver complex functionalities. System-level testing validates the interoperability and performance of integrated systems, encompassing functional, performance, and compatibility tests. Through seamless integration and comprehensive testing, silicon chips seamlessly integrate into the fabric of modern technology [19].

Example: The packaged microcontrollers are integrated into IoT devices, undergoing system-level testing to validate functionality. This involves testing interactions with sensors, actuators, and communication networks. For instance, the microcontroller might be tested in a smart thermostat to ensure accurate temperature sensing, efficient control algorithms, and reliable communication with a central server.

6.10 Conclusion

Silicon chip design and testing epitomize the integration of creativity and engineering precision, underpinning the technological marvels that define our era. From the inception of design concepts to the seamless integration into complex systems, the journey of a silicon chip is a testament to human ingenuity and perseverance. As the semiconductor industry continues to evolve, the quest for smaller, faster, and more efficient chips perpetuates, driving innovation and shaping the course of technological progress. The design phase involves meticulous planning, where engineers translate specifications into architectural blueprints, leveraging advanced techniques such as VLSI design and logic synthesis to optimize performance, power consumption, and area utilization. Wafer-level testing enables early detection of defects, setting the stage for subsequent testing phases. The comprehensive exploration of testing methodologies in semiconductor chip design underscores the importance of rigorous testing processes in ensuring the functionality, reliability, and performance of semiconductor chips. Testing methodologies play a pivotal role in validating chip functionality, from functional and structural testing to post-silicon validation, ensuring compliance with stringent quality standards. The integration of testability features and automated test equipment streamlines the testing process, enhancing efficiency and accuracy. This chapter highlights how chip design and testing contribute to shaping the technological landscape and driving innovation across various industries. Moreover, the tangible impact of silicon chip design and testing reverberates across diverse industries, fueling innovation, driving technological advancements, and revolutionizing the way we live, work, and communicate. From smartphones and computers to automotive electronics and IoT devices, silicon chips underpin a myriad of applications, shaping the landscape of modern technology.

7 A Novel Deep Learning Approach for Early Brain Tumor Detection

Ankit Singh, Ajay Singh Raghuvanshi, B. Ganesh, and K.V.S. Harsha Vardhan

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-7

7.1 Introduction

A brain tumor is an abnormal proliferation of cells within or adjoining the brain, which can emerge from intrinsic neural tissue or extraneous sites through metastasis. These growths can be classified as either primary, originating from native brain structures, or secondary, resulting from dissemination from other organs. Additionally, brain tumors can be characterized as benign or malignant, contingent upon whether they are non-cancerous or cancerous. Brain tumors can influence numerous aspects of brain functionality, such as thought processes, communication, locomotory capabilities, and perception. The manifestations of brain tumors depend on their type, size, and position within the brain.

Common symptoms related to brain tumors include headache, nausea, vomiting, visual difficulties, muscle weakness, seizures, and changes in character traits and mental capacities. The severity and presentation of symptoms are influenced by the tumor’s size, location, and aggressiveness. Early detection and appropriate intervention are crucial for optimizing outcomes and improving quality of life for individuals affected by brain tumors.

Over 12,000 people a year, including 500 children and young adults, are affected by primary brain tumors, i.e., 33 people per day [1]. Every year, brain tumors claim the lives of more than 5,300 individuals. A tumor that originates in the brain or spinal cord is referred to as a primary brain or spinal cord tumor. A projected 24,810 persons in the US (14,280 men and 10,530 women) will have an initial malignant tumor diagnosis in their brain or spinal cord in 2023 [2]. In 2020, an estimated 308,102 cases of primary brain or spinal cord tumors were identified worldwide [3]. In the United States, 5,230 children under the age of 20 are predicted to receive a CNS tumor diagnosis in 2023 [2]. Of all Americans, 23–24 have a brain tumor (malignant or non-malignant) for every 100,000 [1]. Glioblastoma represents around 14.5% of all brain tumors and is the most prevalent type of malignant brain tumor [3]. Meningioma represents approximately 38.3% of all brain tumor cases and is the most often reported benign (or non-malignant) brain tumor.

Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and computed tomography (CT) can help us identify the presence and location of a brain tumor, as well as its size and shape. MRI and CT can also help differentiate between primary brain tumors (those that originate from the brain) and secondary brain tumors (those that spread from other parts of the body). Classification: MRI and CT can help determine the type and grade of a brain tumor, based on its appearance, enhancement, perfusion, and other features [4].

Brain tumors demonstrate remarkable diversity in terms of morphology, size, location, and appearance. These neoplasms can be further stratified according to their histologic subtypes, grades, and stages, influencing their clinical management and prognostication [5]. For instance, brain tumors can be classified as primary or secondary, benign or malignant, and can present with distinctive histological features, such as gliomas, meningiomas, and pituitary tumors.

Moreover, brain tumors can possess heterogeneity within themselves, featuring distinct regions with differing biological properties [6]. This heterogeneity complicates the assessment of tumor margins during surgical procedures and affects the efficacy of therapy.

Labeled datasets, however, may suffer from noise, incompleteness, or inconsistency due to factors such as human errors or variations in annotation standards. Despite these challenges, ongoing efforts in machine learning and computational modeling aim to improve the accuracy and reliability of brain tumor diagnostics and prognostications [7].

In summary, brain tumors exhibit considerable variation in their morphology, location, and composition, necessitating careful consideration of these attributes when developing diagnostic and treatment protocols. Labeled datasets, although valuable, must be approached cautiously due to the possibility of noise, incompleteness, or inconsistency. Nonetheless, technological advances continue to enhance our capacity to understand and manage brain tumors effectively.

Within the realm of computer vision, Residual Network 50 (ResNet-50) serves as a prominent classification model, demonstrating exceptional precision in distinguishing photographs as tumorous or non-tumorous. Compared to traditional architectures, ResNet-50 offers notable advantages, such as:

  1. High accuracy rates, often exceeding those of alternative models like AlexNet, VGG16, GoogleNet, and ResNet18.
  2. The utilization of a “bottleneck” design for building blocks, enabling faster training by reducing parameter counts and matrix multiplication operations [8].
  3. Implementation of three layers per block instead of two, thereby increasing expressive power without significantly augmenting computational requirements.
  4. Superior performance across various domains, including medical imaging, despite potential differences in content compared to general-purpose images [9].
  5. Application of transfer learning strategies which facilitate knowledge transfer from pre-existing models to expedite training and boost performance.

Despite its strengths, ResNet-50 demands substantial computational resources and memory due to its deep architecture, and the training process can be time-consuming and complex. Nevertheless, its proven effectiveness makes it a preferred choice among both researchers and practitioners [10].

7.2 Literature Review

Because brain tumors are varied, complex, and frequently have overlapping traits, classifying them is a difficult undertaking. Additionally, several imaging techniques, including MRI, CT, positron emission tomography (PET), and single-photon emission computed tomography (SPECT), may yield distinct information about the tumor that needs to be integrated and analyzed. Therefore, advanced methodologies and procedures that can manage these difficulties and achieve high accuracy and resilience are needed for brain tumor classification.

Autonomous brain tumor categorization from MRI scans is non-invasive, removing the need for biopsies and improving the precision of the diagnosis process. The research community has spent a lot of time and energy creating automated methods for classifying brain tumors from the beginning of these millennia until the late 1990s. The categorization of brain tumors into their respective histologic categories is another job that has been accomplished with outstanding performance.

Brain tumor classification using deep learning. This chapter presents a method for brain tumor classification using a convolutional neural network (CNN) that extracts features from brain MRI images and then classifies them using fully connected networks. This chapter also compares different CNN architectures, such as basic CNN and VGG-16, and reports an accuracy of 95.71% on an online data set [11,12].

Studies propose machine learning-based algorithms for brain architecture analysis and classification of pictures. In-depth research on brain MRI scan diagnosis techniques was done by Abd-Ellah et al. [13], who compared and contrasted the benefits and drawbacks of deep learning and conventional machine learning techniques. The authors also introduced a novel semi-automatic segmentation method for brain tumor images [6]. The T1W configuration was used by this model for three-dimensional MRI segmentation. To classify breast cancer images, a CNN-based architecture was introduced. The system’s architectural design, which retrieved information from fitting scales, allowed for the highest level of accuracy in tumor segmentation and localization. In addition, a CNN-based model for brain tumor diagnosis employed AlexNET, DenseNet201, GoogLeNet, and Inception-v3 [14]. The outcomes showed that the suggested approach could accurately identify and classify tumors in MRI. The collection of work demonstrates a significant progress in 3D visualization and the segmentation and classification of brain cancers from MRI data. To improve the effectiveness of extracting features, tumor classification, localization, and novel approaches are still needed.

Somasundaram and Gobinath [15] suggested a number of techniques for using MR images to identify brain malignancies. The efficiency of segmentation enhancement utilizing 3D CNNs, multi-class Support Vector Machines (SVMs), and SVMs were investigated by the researchers. Deep learning techniques and CNNs demonstrated exceptional performance in a range of medical image processing applications, including brain tumor identification. The classification and segmentation of brain tumors using deep learning techniques produced better results than conventional machine learning classifiers.

A different study used a deep learning neural model to extract characteristics from MRI [16]. These features were then fed into machine learning classifiers, including multilayer perceptrons (MLPs), SVMs, and naive Bayes. The suggested approach achieved a 96% classification accuracy when SVMs were used. In the research by Kumar et al. [17], several machine learning and deep learning methods for brain tumor identification and segmentation were investigated. These methods included support vector machines, K-nearest neighbors (KNN), MLPs, naive Bayes, and random forest algorithms. Remarkably, at 92.4%, the classification accuracy of the traditional SVMs was the greatest. The researchers also demonstrated a custom CNN design with five layers for MRI brain tumor identification, which achieved an astounding 97.2% accuracy.

Khan et al. [18] used the VGG19 CNN architecture with K-means clustering to identify and segment brain tumors in MRI data. The proposed approach initially divided the input MR modality into slices before pre-processing the intensities with a statistical normalization procedure. The total accuracy of their approach was 94%.

Machine learning-based brain tumor identification and categorization: an extensive overview. This study offers a thorough assessment of the literature on machine learning methods, such as deep learning, for the identification and classification of brain tumors. The study discusses deep learning approaches for brain tumor analysis, segmentation, feature extraction, classification, augmentation techniques, publicly accessible data sets, and the anatomy of brain tumors. The benefits, drawbacks, advancements, and potential future directions in this field are also covered in the study.

An innovative approach called VGG-SCnet, which combines the VGGNet stacked upon a classifier, was presented by Majib et al. [19]. For automated brain tumor diagnosis from MRI images, the scientists improved a pre-trained VGG-16 model with extra layers suggested by their methodology to enable quicker and more efficient training. During the data preparation stage, the region of interest was identified using the most prominent contours. Using augmentation techniques, the class imbalance in the dataset was corrected. Because it had less features, the sixth layer was chosen for feature extraction. Ultimately, a multilayer classifier was used to detect tumors in imaging.

Deep transfer learning methods for brain tumor detection performance analysis. In order to automatically predict brain tumor cells, this research compares the performance of CNN-pretrained VGG-16, ResNet-50, and Inception-v3 models that use transfer learning. The BRATS 2015 and BRATS 2017 data sets are used in the paper’s evaluation of the models. The article reports an accuracy of 97.5% on BRATS 2017 and on BRATS 2015 [10].

Deep Learning-Based Brain Tumor Detection and Classification. Using the state-of-the-art object detection framework YOLO (You Only Look Once) and the deep learning library fastai, respectively, this study offers two deep learning-based techniques for brain tumor detection and classification. Using a portion of the BRATS 2018 data set, which included 1992 brain MRI images, the research assesses the methodologies. The study indicates YOLO and fastai accuracy of 96.8% and 97.6%, respectively.

7.3 Proposed Methodology

ResNet-50 (Residual Network 50) is a CNN design that has excelled at picture classification and other computer vision applications. It is a deep neural network with 50 layers, and its fundamental innovation is the use of residual connections, which allows it to train much deeper networks without having to deal with the vanishing gradient problem. This improves the performance of deep neural networks by adding additional neural layers while reducing error percentage. In other words, skip connections combine the outputs of prior layers with the outputs of stacked layers, allowing far deeper networks to be trained than previously possible.

CNNs are a class of deep learning models primarily employed for processing grid-like data structures, such as images. They consist of multiple interconnected layers, namely convolutional, pooling, non-linear activation, and fully connected layers, arranged sequentially to extract high-level abstractions from low-level pixel inputs. The cornerstone of CNNs revolves around local connectivity and weight-sharing principles, enabling spatial feature hierarchy formation through progressive aggregation of neighboring pixels. As a result, CNNs exhibit superior representation capabilities over traditional handcrafted features, rendering them highly effective for various computer vision applications.

Residual Networks (ResNets), exemplified by the flagship model ResNet-50, introduce innovative design elements aimed at combating deleterious effects induced by increased depth. Specifically, ResNets incorporate residual blocks composed of multiple convolutional layers equipped with shortcut connections, also termed skip connections. These links allow direct transmission of activations from earlier layers to later stages, effectively forming parallel signal paths within the overall architecture. Notably, residual blocks empower ResNets to learn residual mappings instead of direct feature representations, thereby simplifying optimization landscapes and mitigating vanishing gradient problems plaguing deeper architectures.

To elucidate further, let us examine the internal composition of a typical residual block found in ResNet-50. It generally comprises two or three consecutive convolutional layers sandwiched between batch normalization and rectified linear unit (ReLU) operations. A key differentiator resides in the addition of a shortcut connection that bypasses intermediate layers, performing element-wise summation with activated outputs derived from preceding layers. During backpropagation, these skip connections facilitate smoother gradient flow, reducing optimization difficulties encountered in standard feedforward networks devoid of such provisions.

By stacking multiple residual blocks together, ResNet-50 constructs a hierarchical framework that captures increasingly complex patterns embedded within input data. Additionally, ResNet-50 adopts bottleneck designs for computational efficiency, wherein each residual block consists of three nested sub-blocks featuring dimensionality reduction, transformation, and restoration operations. Overall, ResNet-50 epitomizes the power of residual learning paradigms, demonstrating marked improvements in performance relative to its contemporaries while maintaining comparable complexity levels.

In summary, both CNNs and ResNet-50 belong to the family of deep learning models specializing in grid-like data analysis. While CNNs lay the foundation for modern computer vision architectures, innovations introduced by ResNet-50 address critical limitations imposed by escalating depth, culminating in enhanced expressiveness and stability. Through a judicious combination of convolutional layers, batch normalization, activation functions, and residual connections, ResNet-50 emerges as a potent tool for tackling diverse real-world challenges encompassing medical diagnostics, autonomous driving, and surveillance systems.

At the core of ResNet-50 lies a sophisticated deep architecture, meticulously constructed to harness the potential of residual connections and deliver outstanding performance on challenging computer vision tasks. Composed of 50 concatenated residual blocks, this pyramidal edifice systematically expands and contracts channel dimensions while retaining spatial resolution, thus striking an optimal equilibrium between representational capacity and computational feasibility. Each residual block incorporates a triad of convolutional layers: a 1 × 1 convolution for dimension reduction, followed by a 3 × 3 convolution tasked with mining contextual dependencies and concluding with another 1 × 1 convolution for restoring initial dimensionality. This elegant configuration not only streamlines information flow but also curtails parameter proliferation, contributing to ResNet-50’s lauded parsimony.

A salient characteristic of ResNet-50’s deep architecture is the presence of skip connections, which traverse residual blocks and establish direct communication channels between distant layers. These conduits ensure that authentic input signals percolate through the network, reinforcing feature hierarchies and tempering the detrimental impacts of amplified noise or distortion. Furthermore, they imbue ResNet-50 with immunity against vanishing gradient adversities, sustaining robust learning dynamics even when confronted with daunting depths.

Collectively, ResNet-50’s deep architecture weaves an intricate tapestry of learned representations, progressively disentangling latent factors governing visual concepts and unlocking novel opportunities for transferring knowledge across related endeavors. Whether deployed as standalone entities or integrated within larger ensemble frameworks, these deeply rooted models continue to serve as linchpins propelling advances in artificial intelligence research and industrial application development.

Skipping Connections: Because they enable gradients to “skip” over layers, these residual connections are often referred to as skip connections, which enable the training of extremely deep networks.

Bottleneck Architectures: Using the same number of filters in each layer of deeper networks is computationally costly. With its “bottleneck” design, ResNet-50 has three layers per residual block: 1 × 1, 3 × 3, and 1 × 1 convolutions. It is computationally more efficient due to the 1 × 1 convolutions, which first lower and then expand the dimensions.

Global Average Pooling: Instead of completely linked layers, global average pooling is utilized at the network’s conclusion. This method reduces overfitting and the amount of parameters in the network.

Pre-Trained Models: ResNet-50, whose weights have previously been learned on a huge dataset (such as ImageNet), is frequently utilized as a pre-trained model. With smaller datasets, these pre-trained weights can be adjusted for certain tasks.

The comparison of different models is shown in Table 7.1, highlighting the variations in performance metrics.

TABLE 7.1Comparison with Various Models
Model Training Parameters Validation Accuracy Validation Loss Test Accuracy Test Loss
Mobilenet 3206976 0.9648 0.1321 0.9531 0.3220
Inception-v3 21768352 0.9688 0.2712 0.9621 0.3091
DenseNet-121 8068994 0.9812 0.1708 0.9745 0.0723

7.4 Results

During the training process, our deep learning model is classified into two distinct categories. The initial category, labeled “yes,” comprises a collection of images displaying evident signs of brain tumors. Conversely, the second category, labeled “no,” consists of images depicting brains that appear healthy or non-tumorous.

Upon completion of the prediction phase, our model renders verdicts regarding each input image, designating them as either belonging to the “yes” or “no” class. When an image falls under the “yes” label, it signifies the presence of a brain tumor within the depicted brain tissue. Alternatively, if an image receives a “no” classification, it implies that the analyzed brain image does not exhibit any discernible indicators of a tumor.

In summary, our deep learning model serves as a reliable tool for distinguishing between brain images showcasing tumors and those appearing healthy. By accurately categorizing inputs, it enables medical professionals to make informed decisions about further diagnostic procedures and potential treatment plans.

Our extensive dataset encompasses a grand total of 21672 individual MRI images, carefully curated for the purpose of training and validating our deep learning model. Among these images, there exist 3066 instances that represent healthy or non-tumorous brains. On the contrary, a substantial number of 18606 images reveal the presence of varying types of brain tumors (Figure 7.1).

Figure 7.1a demonstrates examples of upper-side MRI images, while Figure 7.1b highlights side-angle MRI images included in our dataset. These diverse representations enable our model to develop a more comprehensive understanding of the intricacies involved in brain tumor detection across multiple viewpoints.

To ensure a balanced and representative distribution of data during the training and validation processes, we meticulously divided our dataset into two portions. An impressive 80% of the entire collection was allocated for intensive training, allowing our model to learn effectively from a vast array of samples. Meanwhile, the remaining 20% served as valuable test data, enabling us to rigorously evaluate the model’s performance and fine-tune its parameters accordingly.

Throughout the course of our research, we remain committed to exploring innovative approaches and refining our deep learning model to achieve unparalleled precision and accuracy in the realm of brain tumor detection.

Our deep learning model, trained on a comprehensive dataset of brain tumor images, demonstrated commendable performance in classifying tumors. The model achieved an accuracy of 99%, sensitivity of 99.36%, and specificity of 98.78%. These metrics indicate the robustness of the model in distinguishing between tumorous and non-tumorous brain images.

$\text{Sensitivity} = \frac{\text{True positives}}{\text{True positives} + \text{false negatives}}$

$\text{Specificity} = \frac{\text{True negatives}}{\text{True negatives} + \text{false positives}}$

7.4.1 Confusion Matrix

True Positives (TP): 3737

In these instances, your model accurately inferred that the examples are members of the positive class (tumorous).

True Negatives (TN): 566

These are the instances when your model accurately predicted that the given examples are from the non-tumorous negative class.

False Positives (FP): 7

These are the cases where your model predicted tumorous, but the actual class was non-tumorous.

False Negatives (FN): 24

These are the cases where your model predicted non-tumorous, but the actual class was tumorous (Figure 7.2).

7.5 Discussion

In this comprehensive exploration, we present a cutting-edge deep learning solution devised for detecting brain tumors at their earliest stages using MRI. Our groundbreaking approach relies upon the powerful capabilities of ResNet-50 as its underlying structure. To validate the efficacy of our proposed methodology, we conduct an extensive evaluation comparing it to prevailing tactics employed for classification. Moreover, semantic segmentation, a critical component encompassing tumor localization, is meticulously considered in our analysis. Simultaneously, we investigate the influence of transfer learning using ResNet-50.

A comparative analysis of the proposed model with existing approaches is provided in Table 7.2. All investigations were executed using the widely recognized BRATS dataset. However, our advanced model incorporates distinct datasets featuring both top and side angles of the brain.

TABLE 7.2Comparative Analysis of the Proposed Model
Paper Source Method Accuracy
Brain tumor classification using deep learning Springer CNN with fully connected networks 95.71%
Deep transfer learning approaches in performance analysis of brain tumor detection International Journal of Advanced Computer Science and Applications Transfer learning-based CNN-pre-trained VGG-16, ResNet-50, and Inception-v3 97.5% on BRATS 2017

Key disparities between the presented models and ours include:

  1. Data Composition: Previous models utilize solely top-angle MRIs, whereas our model integrates side-angle data.
  2. Model Architecture: Our model builds upon the robustness of ResNet-50.
  3. Transfer Learning Integration: We explore the implications of implementing transfer learning using ResNet-50.
  4. Evaluation Metrics: A thorough assessment of these factors contributes to a comprehensive understanding of the models’ performances.

By presenting this detailed account, we aim to shed light on the significance of considering diverse perspectives and expanding the scope of data usage in developing sophisticated deep learning solutions for brain tumor detection.

7.6 Conclusion

Our research demonstrates the effectiveness of deep learning, specifically the ResNet-50 model, in identifying and categorizing brain tumors from MRI scans. By employing a multi-stage network design that integrates both feature extraction and classification, we achieved high levels of accuracy. Our model has shown exceptional performance, achieving a noteworthy accuracy rate of 99.1% in differentiating between brain images with tumors and those without.

In addition, our methodology utilizes an extensive dataset consisting of MRI images taken from multiple angles, including both the top and side views. This diverse dataset enhances the comprehensiveness of brain tumor detection, as it considers various perspectives. The combination of this extensive dataset with the robustness of the ResNet-50 architecture and the implementation of transfer learning techniques significantly improves the performance of our proposed approach.

Through rigorous testing and analysis, we have proven the reliability and accuracy of our deep learning model in identifying and categorizing brain tumors. The model’s impressive accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity underscore its significance as a valuable resource for healthcare professionals in diagnosing brain tumors at an early stage. Early detection, facilitated by our model, can lead to timely intervention and, ultimately, better patient outcomes.

Looking ahead, we plan to fine-tune and enhance our deep learning model, as well as investigate additional datasets and imaging techniques, to expand its versatility and utility. Furthermore, ongoing research and innovation in deep learning applications for medical imaging hold great potential for enhancing disease diagnosis and treatment, ultimately leading to enhanced healthcare outcomes for patients on a global scale.

8 TCAD-Augmented Machine Learning for the Prediction of Device Behaviour and Failure Analysis

Sandeep Moparthi and Gopi Krishna Saramekala

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-8

8.1 Introduction

In order to keep up with the increasing momentum in industrial automation and address time-to-market concerns, the integrated circuit (IC) industry is integrating machine learning (ML) at several levels of its process hierarchy. ML-assisted TCAD simulation of devices has emerged as a feasible solution for the performance analysis and reverse engineering of emerging devices. The idea here is to predict the electrical characteristics of a device to carry out the device-level performance analysis and optimization with the help of a well-trained ML algorithm in very little time. The TCAD-aided ML simulation of emerging devices offers reverse engineering and faster decision-making in R&D. A TCAD-ML model is presented in this chapter with the help of a cylindrical silicon nanotube. The critical device parameters such as channel length (L) and core radius (tc) are varied to create the necessary dataset.

For an efficient ML design, an accurate modelling of the input and output parameter dependency is needed. This can be achieved by a simple polynomial equation. However, the complexity of the ML model is directly proportional to the degree of polynomial which makes the model challenging [1,2]. It is quite difficult and impractical to model a non-linear problem of a higher degree with polynomials. The use of the polynomial model in turn limits the number of input variables in the dataset. Besides this, decision tree (DT) and neural network (NN)-based algorithms offer flexibility to model the problems with a higher degree of non-linearity. The literature reveals that the majority of TCAD-assisted ML efforts have employed the linear regressor (LR), NN, and random forest (RF) techniques to model the problem. Despite its simple design, the LR method restricts the prediction of non-linear dependencies. On the other hand, when it comes to non-linear regression, NN results in increased processing time and design complexity. However, because of its straightforward DT-based design, the RF regressor offers fast computing performance with minimal complexity for issues with non-linearity [3].

Furthermore, it is observed that the literature extensively addressed linear models with a single variable. It is seen that none of the newly developed gate-all-around devices used TCAD-assisted ML for performance prediction as far as we are aware. As a result, a ML model is developed in this chapter to forecast the electrical characteristics by altering important device parameters like tc and L on the Si NTFET’s performance. We adopt and evaluate the performance of two popular DT-based algorithms: the gradient-boosting (GB) regressor and the RF regressor. The performance of algorithms that are being trained is evaluated using metrics such as maximum error (ME) and mean square error (MSE). During the testing phase, the method that performs better during training is chosen based on performance metrics to forecast the threshold voltage VTH, SS, ION, and IOFF of Si NTFET. The type and size of the dataset as well as the quantity of input parameters have a major impact on the appropriateness of the RF and GB algorithms. Thus, it is crucial to compare the algorithms’ performances in order to determine which one is most appropriate for a given dataset. The chapter’s remaining sections are arranged as follows. The creation of the dataset and the ML methods is explained in detail in the following section. The training and testing results are shown in the results section along with a discussion of the algorithms under consideration.

8.2 Data Generation and Machine Learning Methodology

An ML method supported by TCAD is presented to forecast the Si NTFET’s output characteristics. Figure 8.1 and Table 8.1 depict the cross-sectional view of Si NTFET and device parameters taken into account for the Si NTFET simulation, respectively. Shockley-Read-Hall (SRH) recombination model and Carrier Velocity-Temperature (CVT) (Lombardi) mobility model are used in a 3D TCAD tool Atlas from Silvaco to simulate the device structures. By adjusting the two most important parameters, tc and L, within the given range while holding all other parameters constant, as indicated in Table 8.1, a dataset of 4641 samples is produced. With a step size of 0.1 nm, the critical parameter tc is changed from 2 nm to 7 nm. The parameter L is adjusted with a step size of 1 nm, ranging from 10 nm to 100 nm, in order to capture the short-channel and long-channel trend. Thirty percent of the 4641 extracted data samples are used for testing, and 70% are used for training. It is important to remember that the amount of data samples mostly determines the split ratio of the dataset [3]. The transfer characteristics of Si NTFET structures for each data sample, as determined by TCAD simulation, are displayed in Figure 8.2. Figure 8.2 also displays the simulated dataset’s mean and median values. The transfer characteristics of each device are used to obtain the output parameters VTH, SS, ION, and IOFF. The gate voltage (Vgs) at which the parameters ION and IOFF are measured is 1 V and 0 V, respectively.

TABLE 8.1Device Parameters
Parameters Values
Core radius (tc) 2–7 nm
Channel length (L) 10–100 nm
Silicon channel thickness (tsi) 10 nm
Gate dielectric thickness (tox) 1.5 nm
Gate work function (WF) 4.71 eV
Channel doping (NA) 1016 cm−3
Source/drain doping (ND) 1020 cm−3
Drain voltage (Vd) 0.5 V
Temperature (T) 300 K

The non-linear dependency between the input parameter variables and all of the output parameters can be seen from Figure 8.3. Furthermore, as the input parameters vary, all of the output parameters also change continuously. Regression is an ML technique that is used for the training and prediction of continuous values (outputs), whereas classification is meant for the prediction of discrete values. Owing to the previously described factors, the output parameters for the supplied input parameters are predicted using a multi-variable non-linear regression. The two popular DT-based ML algorithms are GB and RF regressors [3,4]. The Python scikit-learn library [4] is where the RF and GB algorithms are imported from. When a trained algorithm is applied to new data in ML, data overfitting reduces its accuracy. To limit the data overfit issue which is more serious in a single DT, GB and RF regressors are constructed using a group of DTs. The characteristics of the DT have an impact on how well the RF and GB regressor algorithms function. To improve the efficiency of these algorithms, these characteristics known as hyperparameters must be tuned.

In this study, the number of estimators, maximum tree depth, minimum samples per split, and minimum samples per leaf are taken into account as the main hyperparameters. The number of estimators represents the highest number of DTs that may be used to successively minimize the MSE in RF and GB. The maximum length of a DT is determined by its maximum tree depth. The smallest quantity of data samples needed to split an internal node in a DT is known as the minimum samples per split [3,4]. The bare minimum of samples needed for a leaf (end) node is specified by the minimum number of samples per leaf. For both RF and GB regressors, the default parameters for the number of estimators, minimum samples per split, and minimum samples per leaf are 100, 2, and 1, respectively [4]. However, for RF and GB regressors, the default value for maximum tree depth is NONE and 3, respectively [4]. When it comes to RF regressors, the default value of NONE for maximum tree depth denotes that the programmer has not provided a predefined value. As a result, the RF algorithm is free to extend the tree depth to its optimal value, which is when all leaves fall below the minimum samples per split value. The optimum values of maximum tree depth for the provided dataset are found to be 18, 20, 16, and 34 for the associated output parameters VTH, SS, ION, and IOFF, respectively, according to the RF regressor. In addition, the GB regressor’s default maximum tree depth of 3 is far less than the ideal values that the RF regressor was able to achieve. This is the reason why, when compared to their default hyperparameter values, the GB regressor outperforms the RF regressor. To provide a level playing field in comparison, this study takes the maximum tree depth values that were produced from the RF regressor and applies them to the GB regressor. Table 8.2 displays the hyperparameter values that were utilized to compare the RF and GB regressor methods.

TABLE 8.2Key Hyperparameters Considered for GB and RF Regressors
VTH (V) SS mV/decade ION (A) IOFF (A)
Number of estimators 100 100 100 100
Maximum tree depth 18 20 16 34
Minimum samples per split 2 2 2 2
Minimum samples per leaf 1 1 1 1

The MSE for the GB regressor attained its optimal value of 2.86 × 10−12, 2.81 × 10−6, 2.68 × 10−19, and 1.89 × 10−21 for VTH, SS, ION, and IOFF, respectively, as can be shown in Figure 8.4 and Table 8.3. 8.3.2 Section 2 Enhanced TCAD-ML Dataset Design

In the conventional ML model, train dataset does not allow the use of input parameters other than those used in the train dataset during the testing of the trained algorithm and the prediction of outputs. The dataset used in the previous section is designed as an array of two input variables L and tc with three outputs VTH, SS, ION, and IOFF. The previous dataset does not allow input variables other than L and tc during the algorithm testing for the prediction of output. Besides this, the limits of extrapolation associated with the ML model imply that the prediction of output fails if the given values of the input variables (L and tc) are outside the range of train dataset. As these algorithms cannot extrapolate (out-of-train range prediction), a large dataset is required for the algorithm training to cover the entire range and the combinations of all the considered inputs. The extraction of such huge data with several input combinations results in a drastic increase in the TCAD simulation time and size of the train dataset. The above-mentioned challenges which are involved in the ML model and dataset design in turn diminish the viability and furtherance of TCAD-aided ML. To enhance the practicality of TCAD-aided ML by eluding the above-mentioned challenges, this section of results proposes an alternate design of the previous dataset for the algorithm training and testing. The range and step size of parameters L and tc and models used in TCAD simulation remain unchanged.

A new parameter [(L * tc)/(L + tc)] + log(L) is defined from the values of L and tc in the considered range. The new parameter is chosen to overcome the redundancy caused by various combinations of L and tc values in the given range. Furthermore, [(L * tc)/(L + tc)] + log(L) is multiplied with a factor 109 (the resultant parameter is denoted as M) to amplify the difference between its adjacent values which results in better training of the algorithm. This is due to the fact that the values of [(L * tc)/(L + tc)] + log(L) are very small and are in the order of 10–9. The two input parameters, i.e., L and tc in the previous dataset, are replaced with the newly defined input parameter, i.e., M. The inclusion of M in the proposed new dataset allows the selection of L and tc values in any range such that M must be within the range of the proposed train dataset. Hence, the boundaries of L and tc which are used for dataset generation can be surpassed during prediction. This in turn eludes the limit of extrapolation in the ML model. Furthermore, two extracted device characteristics IOFF and ION/IOFF are also included in the proposed new dataset as input parameters. The inclusion of IOFF and ION/IOFF as input parameters in the proposed new dataset permits the use of any device parameter (along with L and tc) as a variable during testing/prediction. This can extend the test range beyond train range and elude the limitation of inputs associated with conventional train datasets as mentioned. Hence, the proposed new train dataset which is used for the algorithm training has three input variables, i.e., M, IOFF, and ION/IOFF along with output, i.e., VTH. All the three input parameters of the new train dataset are normalized. The range of M, IOFF, and ION/IOFF in the train dataset is from 1E+09 to 8542056074, 3.8E-14 to 1.69E-05, and 7.95833 to 754122000, respectively, for the considered range of variable parameters L and tc.

Figure 8.11 depicts the comparison of actual VTH and trained VTH which is presented with only two inputs, i.e., M and ION/IOFF for the 3D visualization of the trend. The overlapping of both actual and trained outputs in Figure 8.11 is evidence for the successful training of the algorithm. The performance metrics MSE and ME are calculated between actual and trained VTH to estimate the training accuracy of the GB regressor. The obtained MSE and ME under training are 4.18E-15 and 8.65E-7, respectively. As mentioned, the new train dataset permits the use of any of the device parameters along with L and tc as input variables during testing/prediction. Moreover, the new train dataset also permits the use of L and tc values out of the train range during testing/prediction. To demonstrate these merits, this study considered channel thickness (tsi), gate-oxide thickness (tox), and channel doping concentration (NA) as variable device parameters along with L and tc for the generation of the test dataset which is used for testing/prediction. The considered values of these parameters are shown in Table 8.5 (column 1–5) which are used in TCAD simulations to extract the test dataset as shown in Table 8.5 (column 6–9).

8.4 Conclusion

Challenges in TCAD-aided ML are addressed to make it viable. A train dataset design approach is proposed to elude the issues of extrapolation and train data size. The proposed approach is demonstrated with Si NTFET by training a GB regressor algorithm. A set of 4641 data samples are extracted from the TCAD simulations by varying the L and tc of Si NTFET. The merits of the proposed train dataset are tested with an independent test dataset created by varying the tsi, tox, and NA along with L and tc. The training of the regressor algorithm with the proposed train dataset permits the use of any device parameters (including the device parameters which are kept constant for training) as variables for the design of the test dataset. The proposed model can predict the threshold voltage (VTH) of Si NTFET for the given values of M, IOFF, and ION/IOFF. This dataset design can be applied to any device structure for a different combination of inputs for the prediction of selected outputs.
