10 Role of Machine Learning Applications in VLSI Design

Mohd Javed Khan, Pooja Gaur, Indrasen Singh, and Saif Ahmad

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-10

10.1 Introduction

10.1.1 Overview of VLSI Design Challenges

The technique of integrating millions or billions of transistors on a single chip is known as “very-large-scale integration (VLSI).” Microprocessors, memory chips, communication systems, and other advanced electronic devices can be developed by employing VLSI design. However, the growing complexity, power consumption, timing constraints, manufacturing problems, and design verification hurdles also pose significant challenges to VLSI design. Some of the major challenges associated with VLSI design are as follows [1]:

  1. Complexity: With millions of components and connections, VLSI designs have grown remarkably complex. In order to ensure that the design satisfies power, area, and reliability restrictions, designers must handle the complexity of architectures, data pathways, memory hierarchies, as well as performance specifications. Designers employ a variety of strategies, including abstraction, hierarchy, modularity, and reuse, to handle the complexity. To help them with the design process, they also use design automation technologies and processes. But these methods and instruments also bring with them additional challenges like complicated design validation, verification, and debugging [2].
  2. Power Management: The power consumption rises with the number of transistors. The requirement to strike a balance between energy efficiency and performance, as well as lower cooling and heat dissipation expenses, presents power management issues. Power gating, voltage scaling, and clock gating are just a few of the strategies that designers must use to maximize power efficiency without compromising reliability or performance. They must also take into account how power management strategies affect other design elements including area, time, noise, and variability [3].
  3. Timing Closure: This technique guarantees that all signals arrive at their designated locations within predetermined time constraints. Meeting timing requirements is significantly hampered by the declining clock cycles and rising operating frequencies. To achieve timing closure, designers must carefully plan and optimize the design, use time-driven approaches, and make use of sophisticated place-and-route and advanced synthesis tools. They must additionally take into consideration how temperature, aging, and process variables affect the design’s timing behavior [4].
  4. Design for Manufacturability (DFM): Design for manufacturability (DFM) is essential in order to guarantee that the design can be produced in large quantities with a satisfactory yield and quality. Additional challenges including yield optimization, lithography constraints, and process variability appear when technology nodes get smaller. To overcome DFM obstacles and guarantee good manufacturing yield, designers must optimize layouts, carry out statistical analysis, and use strategies like design-technology co-optimization (DTCO). To make sure the design is feasible and compatible, they must also work with the foundry and the fabrication process [5].
  5. Physical Design Challenges: Millions of components must be placed and routed on a chip while adhering to timing, power, and area restrictions during the physical design stage. To create a physically robust design, designers must maximize floor layout, make use of sophisticated algorithms for placement and routing, and deal with problems like signal integrity and congestion. Additionally, they must handle the difficulties brought on by interconnect scaling, which include increasing resistance, capacitance, and crosstalk and which have an impact on the design’s power, performance, and reliability [6].
  6. Verification Complexity: Verification makes sure the design fulfills performance and functional requirements. The size and complexity of the design cause the verification complexity to increase exponentially. To test the design at multiple levels of abstraction and ensure its accuracy and quality, designers must use a variety of verification approaches, including simulation, formal methods, emulation, and prototyping. They must also overcome the difficulties involved in validating intricate architectures, including those with many cores, heterogeneous systems, and reconfigurable components [7].

10.1.2 Introduction to the Role of Machine Learning in Addressing These Challenges

A specialized branch of artificial intelligence called machine learning (ML) gives computers the ability to learn from data and become more efficient without the need for explicit programming. By offering automated and data-driven solutions, ML can be extremely helpful in tackling the problems associated with VLSI design. The following are some instances of how ML can support various facets of VLSI design [8]:

  1. Complexity: By automating the creation and optimization of architectures, data pathways, and memory hierarchies, ML can assist in lowering the complexity of VLSI design [9]. ML algorithms have the ability to learn from current designs and produce new ones that satisfy power and performance constraints. By employing strategies like formal methods, emulation, and prototyping, ML may also help with the verification, validation, and debugging of intricate designs.
  2. Power Management: By anticipating and optimizing the power consumption of various components and modules, ML can assist in raising the power efficiency of VLSI circuits. With the help of previous data, ML algorithms are able to precisely calculate a circuit’s power dissipation and leakage under various operating scenarios. ML can assist with low-power circuit design by employing strategies including voltage scaling, clock gating, and power gating [10].
  3. Timing Closure: Through the analysis and inference of past data, ML has the potential to assist in timing closure by precisely predicting a circuit’s timing behavior under various operating scenarios. ML algorithms have the ability to learn from past timing reports and optimize a circuit’s timing margins and limitations. By utilizing methods like timing-driven approaches and sophisticated algorithms, ML can also assist in the synthesis and place-and-route of VLSI circuits [11].
  4. Design for Manufacturability (DFM): Through the analysis and learning of process variations, lithography constraints, and yield optimization data, ML can contribute to the improvement of VLSI circuits’ manufacturability and yield. In order to reduce the impact of variations and faults, ML algorithms can optimize a circuit’s layouts and masks by learning from prior fabrication data. ML can assist in VLSI circuit layout optimization and mask synthesis through the use of methods like statistical analysis and DTCO [12].
  5. Physical Design challenges: Through data analysis and learning from interconnect scaling, congestion, and signal integrity, ML can assist in overcoming the physical design issues associated with VLSI circuits. The placement and routing of a circuit can be optimized by ML algorithms to reduce interconnect delay and noise by learning from prior physical design data. By applying strategies like sophisticated algorithms and optimization approaches, ML can also assist in the placement and routing of VLSI circuits [13].
  6. Verification Complexity: By evaluating and learning from enormous amounts of circuit data as well as functional and performance specifications, ML can assist in addressing the verification complexity of VLSI circuits. More effectively and precisely, ML algorithms can test a circuit’s performance and functionality by learning from past verification data. Pattern recognition and anomaly detection are two methods that ML can use to aid in the diagnostic and fault detection of VLSI circuits [14].

10.1.3 Objectives

A subfield of artificial intelligence called ML allows computers to learn without explicit programming by using data and experience. The process of assembling millions of transistors and other components on a single chip to create integrated circuits (ICs) is known as VLSI. The art and science of creating and refining ICs for a range of uses, including sensing, computation, and communication, is known as VLSI design [15]. Applying ML to VLSI design aims to increase the efficiency, quality, and reliability of IC design and manufacture by automating data-intensive and complicated processes like:

  1. Device Modeling: ML can assist in developing concise and precise models, based on simulations or measurements, of the physical behavior and functionality of transistors and other devices [13,16]. For example, ML can be used to discover the nonlinear relationship between the electrical characteristics, including capacitance, voltage, and current, and the device parameters [13]. Through the use of methods like dimensionality reduction, sparsification, and compression, ML) can also assist in lowering the computational cost and memory requirements of device simulations [16].
  2. Layout Verification: ML can assist in confirming that an IC’s layout is robust and correct by identifying and fixing mistakes such as lithography defects, design rule violations, and parasitic effects [16,17]. In order to apply various verification techniques appropriately, ML can be used, for instance, to categorize layout patterns into groups like crucial, non-critical, and ambiguous [17]. By employing strategies including feature extraction, anomaly detection, and optimization, ML can also aid in enhancing the precision and effectiveness of layout verification [16].
  3. Yield Prediction: By examining how process variations and defects affect circuit performance, ML can assist in estimating the probability that an IC will meet requirements and operate as intended [13,16]. For example, ML can be used to determine the statistical distribution of circuit characteristics like power, delay, and noise, and then compute the yield while taking into account the specified constraints [13]. Additionally, by utilizing methods like regression, classification, and clustering, ML can improve yield prediction [16].
  4. Post-Silicon Validation: By locating and analyzing errors and optimizing the testing and debugging procedures, ML can assist in verifying the quality and operation of an IC after it has been fabricated. To create the best test responses and sequences, for instance, ML can be used to develop test patterns and fault models. By applying methods like fault injection, fault localization, and fault rectification, ML can also assist in lowering the duration and expense of post-silicon validation [16].
  5. Reliability Analysis: Through modeling and mitigating the impacts of aging, stress, and environmental conditions, ML can assist in evaluating and enhancing an IC’s lifetime and reliability. For example, ML can be used to understand the failure modes and degradation mechanisms, and then forecast the IC’s reliability and remaining useful life. By utilizing methods like reliability modeling, reliability monitoring, and reliability management, ML can also improve the reliability analysis [16].
  6. Analog and Digital Synthesis: Through design space exploration, the generation of potential solutions, and an assessment of the trade-offs between performance, power, area, and cost, ML can assist in the synthesis and optimization of analog and digital circuit design. For instance, ML can be used to understand the design constraints and objectives, after which the synthesis problem can be formulated as an optimization problem. By utilizing methods like reinforcement learning (RL), evolutionary algorithms, and neural networks, ML can also aid in enhancing the effectiveness and quality of analog and digital synthesis [16].

10.2 Fundamentals of VLSI Design

10.2.1 Brief Explanation of VLSI Design Process

The technique of assembling several thousand or millions of transistors and other electronic components on a single chip to create ICs is known as VLSI [18]. VLSI design is an iterative process that includes various steps, including:

  1. Specification and Architecture: The key components of the IC or system-on-a-chip (SoC), such as processors, memory, peripherals, and interfaces, are defined in this step by translating the system-level specifications into a high-level architecture [19]. The data flow, control signals, and communication protocols between the components are all specified in the architecture. Typically, block diagrams, data flow graphs, or state machines are used to depict the architecture. The architecture is assessed using a range of analysis and estimating methods for cost, power, performance, and practicality [20].
  2. Design Entry: Using a graphical tool like a schematic editor or a hardware description language (HDL) like Verilog or VHSIC (Very High-Speed Integrated Circuit) Hardware Description Language (VHDL), the architecture is implemented in this step [21]. At various abstraction levels, such as the behavioral, structural, or register-transfer level (RTL), the HDL or schematic represents the structure and functioning of the IC or SoC [22]. In order to verify the design’s functionality, testbenches and stimuli must also be created for the design entry. An integrated development environment (IDE) with editing, debugging, and simulation functions is typically used for the design entry [23].
  3. Logic Design: A netlist, or representation of the IC or SoC in terms of logic gates and connections, is created from the HDL or schematic in this step [24]. Then, using logic synthesis tools, the netlist is optimized for power, area, and performance [25]. To enhance the quality of the netlist, the logic synthesis tools carry out a number of modifications, including retiming, technology mapping, logic replication, and logic minimization [26]. Logic simulation tools are also used to verify the timing and functionality of the netlist [27]. The outputs of the netlist are compared to the anticipated results from the testbenches and stimuli using the logic simulation tools. Additionally, reports on the netlist’s coverage, power, and delay are produced by the logic simulation tools.
  4. Circuit Design: The netlist is mapped to a particular technology library in this step, which includes models and properties of the transistors and other devices that are accessible during the fabrication process [28]. In order to meet the performance, area, and power constraints, the devices must also be sized, biased, and placed as part of the circuit design [29]. Circuit simulation tools, like Simulation Program with Integrated Circuit Emphasis (SPICE), are used to verify the circuit design by analyzing the electrical behavior of the circuit. Reports on the circuit’s voltage, current, power, and noise are also produced by the circuit simulation tools.
  5. Physical Design: The circuit design is transformed into a layout in this step, which is a representation of the SoC or IC in terms of layers and geometric shapes. In addition to placing and routing the wires and devices, the layout includes DFM elements like metal density, antenna diodes, and dummy fill [30]. Physical verification technologies including parasitic extraction (PEX), layout versus schematic (LVS), and design rule check (DRC) are used to verify the layout [31]. The DRC ensures that the layout complies with the fabrication specifications, including minimal overlap, width, and spacing. The LVS ensures the consistency of the layout with the netlist, which includes aspects such as orientation, polarity, and connectivity. The PEX removes the layout’s parasitic capacitance, resistance, and inductance, which have an impact on the circuit’s power, noise, and performance [32].
  6. Fabrication and Testing: The layout is transmitted to a foundry during this phase, where a series of photolithography, etching, deposition, and doping processes are used to manufacture the IC or SoC [33]. Using a variety of test equipment and techniques, including automatic test pattern generation (ATPG), scan chain, built-in self-test (BIST), and fault diagnosis, the fabricated IC or SoC is subsequently evaluated for quality, functionality, and performance [34]. Circuit faults, including stuck-at, bridging, and delay faults, can be identified by the test patterns generated by the ATPG. A tester can access the test points inserted into the circuit via the scan chain in order to apply test patterns and observe the outputs. In order to enable self-testing and self-diagnosis, the BIST embedded test logic inside the circuit. The circuit faults are identified by the fault diagnosis in terms of their location and nature [35].

  7. Verification and Validation: This crucial stage guarantees the correctness and quality of the design throughout each phase of the VLSI design flow. Validation is the process of determining whether the design satisfies the demands and expectations of the end-users, whereas verification examines whether the design conforms to the specifications and requirements [36]. Formal methods, simulation, emulation, prototyping, and testing are all viable approaches for conducting verification and validation (Figure 10.1).

10.2.2 Key Parameters of VLSI Design

The key parameters of fundamentals of VLSI design are the characteristics that impact IC performance, cost, and reliability. A few of these parameters include:

  1. Power Consumption: The result of multiplying supply voltage by current is power consumption. The current relies on the circuit’s switching activity and capacitance. During each clock cycle, transistors change their states (on and off), resulting in switching activity [37]. The total amount of charge held in the circuit elements is known as capacitance. VLSI designers can minimize capacitance, minimize switching activity, lower the supply voltage, and use low-power process methods to cut down on power usage [38].
  2. Area: The product of the size of each transistor and the number of transistors yields the area. The size of each transistor relies on the design rules and the process technology. The collection of techniques and materials needed to fabricate the chip is known as process technology. The constraints and instructions for placing and routing the transistors are referred to as design rules. VLSI designers can employ methods including scaling, layout optimization, logic minimization, and high-density process technologies to minimize area [39].
  3. Performance: Performance is defined as the inverse of the circuit’s execution time or delay. The delay relies on the interconnect delay and the propagation delay. The time it takes a signal to propagate through a logic gate is known as propagation delay [40]. The time required for a signal to travel via a wire is known as interconnect delay. VLSI designers can employ strategies like pipelining the stages, reducing the delay, parallelizing the computation, raising the clock frequency, and utilizing high-speed process technologies for enhanced performance [41].
  4. Design Time: The amount of time needed to create, check, and test the semiconductor, reduced development costs, and a quicker time to market are the results of a shorter design time. The chip’s design time relies on the functionality and complexity of the chip, the design methodology and tools, and the testing and debugging process. The quality and performance of the chip are also impacted by the design time. A variety of methods, including modular design, hierarchical design, design automation, and design for testability, are employed by VLSI designers to shorten the design time [42].
  5. Testability: Testability is the chip’s capacity to detect and correct errors with ease. Greater yield and reliability are correlated with increased testability [43]. A chip’s testability is contingent upon its fault coverage, test pattern creation, test application, and fault diagnosis. The chip’s performance and cost are also impacted by its testability. A variety of strategies, including scan design, BIST, design for debug, and fault-tolerant design, are employed by VLSI designers to enhance testability [44].
  6. Cost: The entire cost for producing and utilizing the chip, profit and affordability both increase with reduced costs. The chip’s expense relies on the fabrication cost, the packaging cost, the testing cost, and the operating cost [45]. The market and consumer demand for the chip are also impacted by cost. Yield improvement, design optimization, process improvement, and cost-benefit analysis are just a few of the methods VLSI designers employ to cut costs [46].

10.3 Machine Learning Basics and Techniques in VLSI Design

10.3.1 Overview of Machine Learning Concepts Relevant to VLSI Design

Complex and difficult processes like device modeling, layout verification, yield prediction, post-silicon validation, and reliability analysis are all part of VLSI design. ML can assist VLSI designers in automating these processes, enhancing their efficiency and precision, and managing the growing intricacy and unpredictability of contemporary IC technology [47].

The following are some ML ideas that are pertinent to VLSI design:

  1. Supervised Learning: Supervised learning is a sort of ML in which the computer learns from labeled data in the form of input–output pairs. The objective is to identify a function, like a regression or classification function, that maps the input to the output [48]. For instance, you can use supervised learning to identify a function that estimates a house’s price based on its features if you have a data collection of house prices and features, such as size, location, number of rooms, etc. The output of this problem is continuous, making it a regression problem. On the other hand, supervised learning can be used to identify a function that categorizes an image according to its label if you have a data collection of photos and labels, such as cat, dog, bird, etc. The output of this task is discrete, making it a classification problem.

    Supervised learning can be applied to VLSI design tasks like yield prediction, where the input is the design parameters and the output is the probability of passing manufacturing tests, or device modeling, where the input is the device geometry and the output is the electrical behavior [49].

  2. Unsupervised Learning: Unsupervised learning is a sort of ML in which the computer learns from unlabeled data that simply contains inputs. Using techniques like dimensionality reduction or clustering functions, the objective is to identify structures, patterns, or clusters in the data [50]. For instance, unsupervised learning can be used to identify a function that lowers the dimensionality of an image data collection by compressing the images or removing the most significant features. Since the output is a lower-dimensional representation of the input, this is an issue involving dimensionality reduction. On the other hand, unsupervised learning can be employed to create a function that organizes the images into similar clusters, such as by identifying common qualities or themes among the images. This is a clustering problem, as the output consists of a set of labels indicating the input’s cluster membership.

    For VLSI design tasks like layout verification—where the layout geometry is the input and the output is the identification of possible errors or violations—or post-silicon validation—where the chip measurements are the input and the detection of faults or anomalies are the output—unsupervised learning can be applied [51].

  3. Semi-Supervised Learning: This is a technique that integrates unlabeled and labeled data. It employs a reduced quantity of labeled data (in which the output labels are specified) and an increased quantity of unlabeled data (in which the output labels are unknown) [52]. Analogous to supervised learning, the objective is to develop a model that provides precise predictions of the output variable using the input features. However, the model is trained on a dataset that includes both labeled and unlabeled data. However, the model is trained on a dataset that includes both labeled and unlabeled data., for instance, anomaly detection, performance prediction, image classification, and text classification [53]. This method has numerous applications, including the classification of web content, speech analysis, and more. This method offers numerous advantages, including enhanced model generalization in comparison to solely supervised approaches and decreased annotation expenses.

  4. Reinforcement Learning: This form of ML involves the computer acquiring knowledge through its own actions and feedback, which is comprised of rewards, states, and actions. By utilizing a control or optimization function, the objective is to identify a policy that optimizes the expected cumulative reward. Logic synthesis, in which the input is a behavioral description and the output is the optimal gate-level implementation, and analog design, in which the input consists of design specifications and the output is the optimal circuit configuration, are two VLSI design tasks that can benefit from RL [54,55] (Figure 10.2).

10.3.2 Introduction to Feature Selection and Dimensionality Reduction

Two methods, feature selection and dimensionality reduction, are intended to minimize the quantity of input variables in an ML model, particularly in the case of high-dimensional data. For VLSI design, high-dimensional data can present difficulties by driving up the cost of computing and data storage, affecting the speed and accuracy of problem-solving, and contributing to the “curse of dimensionality” [56].

  1. Feature Selection: The process of choosing a subset of pertinent features from the original data and eliminating the redundant or irrelevant ones is known as feature selection [57]. In addition to lowering computational complexity and the risk of overfitting, feature selection can enhance the performance, interpretability, and generalization of ML models. Filter methods, wrapper methods, and embedding methods are the three categories into which feature selection can be classified [57]. The features are selected using filter methods according to certain statistical criteria like the chi-square test, information gain, or correlation, and the top-ranked features are selected [58]. Using an ML model as a black box, wrapper techniques assess the features according to the model’s performance metrics, such as accuracy, precision, or recall, and then choose the optimal subset of features [57]. Embedded approaches, such as regularization, decision trees (DTs), or neural networks, incorporate the feature selection process into the training of an ML model and select the features with the greatest impact on the model’s output.
  2. Dimensionality Reduction: The process of converting original high-dimensional data into a lower-dimensional representation while maintaining the data’s structure or key information is known as dimensionality reduction [59]. In addition to lowering noise and redundancy in the data, dimensionality reduction can help with classification, clustering, and visualization. There are two types of dimensionality reduction techniques: linear techniques and nonlinear techniques [60]. For reducing dimensionality, linear techniques such as principal component analysis (PCA), linear discriminant analysis (LDA), or singular value decomposition (SVD) employ linear projections or transformations on the assumption that the data is on or near a linear subspace. In nonlinear techniques, the data is assumed to be on or near a nonlinear manifold; to reduce the dimensionality, nonlinear mappings or embeddings are applied, such as multidimensional scaling (MDS), kernel PCA, or t-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding (t-SNE) [61].

10.3.3 Machine Learning Techniques

With the use of ML approaches, computers may learn from data and carry out operations that are challenging or impossible for them to accomplish using traditional algorithms. ML approaches can be used for fault tolerance, optimization, verification, testing, diagnostics, and other elements of VLSI design (Figure 10.3).

10.3.3.1 Design Optimization

The process of using ML techniques to enhance the area, power, and performance of VLSI circuits is known as design optimization of ML techniques in VLSI design [62]. ML approaches have the potential to automate and accelerate a number of processes in the VLSI design flow, including yield prediction, reliability analysis, logic synthesis, placement, routing, timing analysis, power estimation, and defect detection and fault diagnostics. In order to determine the optimum trade-offs between competing goals, ML techniques can also be used to explore and optimize the design space of VLSI circuits. VLSI circuit design optimization commonly uses a number of ML approaches, including:

  1. Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs): ANNs are computer models that can learn intricate and nonlinear functions from data. They are useful for a number of optimization tasks, including timing analysis, logic synthesis, placement, routing, and power estimation for VLSI circuits. ANNs can also be implemented in hardware by means of VLSI circuits that mimic the functionality and behavior of biological neurons and synapses, such as memristors, spintronics, and neuromorphic chips [63].

    1. Artificial neurons are arranged in layers and connected by weights to form ANNs. To generate an output, each neuron calculates the weighted sum of its inputs and applies a nonlinear activation function. Throughout the learning process, the weights are modified in accordance with the error between the desired and actual outputs.
    2. With enough information and hidden neurons, ANNs can learn to approximate any function. The loss function, which estimates the discrepancy between the outputs and the targets, can be minimized using a variety of algorithms, including stochastic gradient descent, gradient descent, and backpropagation, to train ANNs [64].
    3. Depending on whether or not the connections are directed, ANNs can be categorized as feedforward or recurrent networks. Feedforward networks can be utilized for static input–output mapping because they don’t have any cycles in their connections. Recurrent networks can be utilized for sequential and dynamic data processing because they contain cycles in their connections.

    4. ANNs can be applied to a range of VLSI design tasks, including:

      1. Logic Synthesis: A Boolean function can be mapped to a network of logic gates using ANNs, which minimizes the circuit’s area, delay, and power consumption [65].
      2. Placement: ANNs can be employed to determine the best locations for the components on the chip, reducing wire length and congestion.
      3. Routing: By preventing overlaps and crossovers, ANNs can be utilized to determine the best paths for the interconnections between the components.
      4. Timing Analysis: By considering the impacts of temperature, voltage, and process variations, ANNs can be utilized to predict the circuit delay [66].
      5. Power Estimation: ANNs can be utilized to estimate the power consumptions in circuits by taking into account the wires’ resistance, capacitance, and switching activity.

  2. Support Vector Machines: Support vector machines (SVMs) are supervised learning models that are capable of performing regression and binary or multiclass classification. SVMs are capable of handling high-dimensional and nonlinear data and can determine the best hyperplane to divide the data into distinct classes. SVMs can be applied to a variety of optimization problems, including yield prediction, fault diagnosis, defect detection, and VLSI circuit reliability analysis [67]. SVMs are additionally suitable to optimize building energy, as they are able to predict a building’s energy demand and consumption depending on a range of parameters, including occupancy, weather, and mode of operation.

    1. SVMs employ a kernel function to map the data to a higher-dimensional space, from which a linear hyperplane is found to divide the classes. A subset of the data points that are on the margin of the classes—referred to as support vectors—define the hyperplane [68].
    2. Depending on the nature of the issue and the data, SVMs can employ a variety of kernel functions, including sigmoid, polynomial, linear, and radial basis functions. In the feature space, the kernel function calculates the distance or similarity between the data points.
    3. The trade-off between the model’s accuracy and complexity can be managed by SVMs using a variety of regularization parameters, such as gamma or C. The width of the margin and the penalty for the misclassified points are established by the regularization parameters.

    4. SVMs can be applied to a range of VLSI design tasks, including:

      1. Defect Detection: SVMs can reduce false positives and false negatives by classifying the chips as defective or non-defective based on the test results.
      2. Fault Diagnosis: SVMs can be employed for fault detection in order to pinpoint the kind and position of circuit faults by analyzing the malfunctioning outputs, which lowers the expense and time required for diagnosis.
      3. Yield Prediction: Using process parameters, design attributes, and test outcomes, SVMs can be employed to predict the chip yield, which is the percentage of functional chips within a batch.
      4. Reliability Analysis: SVMs can be employed to analyze a chip’s reliability, which is the probability that it will continue to function correctly over time, taking into account stress factors, aging effects, and failure mechanisms [69].

  3. Genetic Algorithms (GAs): Genetic algorithms (GAs) are methods of optimization based on the principles of natural process of evolution. GAs are able to adapt to changing environments and discover nearly optimal solutions to complicated and multimodal issues. Several optimization activities in VLSI circuits, including test pattern generation, circuit design, layout optimization, and fault tolerance VLSI circuit, can be addressed with GAs [70]. Through the identification of optimum parameters for population size, crossover rate, and mutation rate, GAs can also be utilized to maximize the energy usage and performance of parallel ML algorithms.

    1. GAs employ a population consisting of individuals who, when compared to a fitness function that evaluates their quality, represent potential solutions to the problem. To create new generations of individuals who are more fit than the previous ones, GAs employ operators like selection, crossover, and mutation.
    2. GAs can select individuals for reproduction using a variety of techniques, including tournaments, roulette wheels, and rank-based systems [71]. The population’s quality and diversity are intended to be maintained by the selection procedures.
    3. The way the individuals are merged to produce offspring is determined by the crossover techniques that GAs employ, which include one-point, two-point, and uniform [72]. The crossover techniques seek to enlarge the search space and produce novel solutions.
    4. Different mutation techniques, like bit-flip, swap, or invert, can be used by GAs to determine how the individuals are changed to add variation. The goal of the mutation techniques is to avoid local optima and stop premature convergence.

    5. GAs can be applied to a range of VLSI design tasks, including:

      1. Circuit Design: GAs can be applied to optimize the performance, area, and power of analog and digital circuits by designing their topology, size, and biasing.
      2. Layout Optimization: By reducing the area, wire length, and parasitic of the circuit, GAs can be utilized to optimize the chip’s layout [70].
      3. Test Pattern Generation: Using GAs, test patterns can be created that identify the maximum number of faults present in the circuit, cutting down on test duration and expense.
      4. Fault Tolerance: Using GAs, circuits can be designed to be fault-tolerant, meaning they can either tolerate or retrieve from faults, hence enhancing the circuit’s availability and reliability [70].

  4. Reinforcement Learning (RL): Reinforcement learning (RL) is an ML technique that allows a system to learn from its own actions and rewards. The design space of VLSI circuits can be explored and optimized using RL to determine the optimum trade-offs between area, power, and performance. Adaptive control techniques for VLSI systems, including error correction, thermal management, and voltage and frequency scaling, can also be learned by RL [73].

    1. In RL, an agent interacts with the environment and gains knowledge from the feedback it gets. The value function calculates the expected return, the reward function estimates the results, and the policy dictates the agent’s course of action.
    2. There are two types of RL methods: model-based and model-free, based on whether the agent has access to an environment model or not. Model-free techniques depend on trial-and-error learning, but model-based techniques can employ simulation and planning to enhance the policy [74].
    3. RL can be divided into value-based and policy-based approaches, contingent on whether the agent directly learns the policy or the value function. In policy-based approaches, actions are sampled using the policy, but in value-based methods, the optimal action is chosen using the value function.

    4. RL can be applied to a range of VLSI design tasks, including:

      1. Design Space Exploration: Resistant logic can be utilized to determine the optimum possible configuration of circuit parameters, like transistor sizes, wire widths, and supply voltages, in order to maximize power, area, and performance objectives.
      2. Adaptive Control: RL can be utilized to train dynamic control policies for VLSI systems, which include controlling the thermal dissipation, modifying the voltage and frequency levels, and correcting errors based on temperature, workload, and reliability conditions [75].

  5. Neural Networks (NNs): Neural networks (NNs) are a unique class of ANNs that draw inspiration from the functions and structures of biological brains. NNs are useful for a variety of VLSI applications, including computer vision, speech recognition, image processing, and natural language processing. They are also capable of learning complex and nonlinear functions from data. Neuromorphic devices, spintronics, memristors, and other VLSI circuits can be employed to implement NNs in hardware [76].

    1. Neurons in NNs are arranged in layers and joined by synapses. Every neuron generates an output by applying a nonlinear activation function to the weighted sum of its inputs. Based on the error between the desired and actual outputs, the synapses retain the weights that are learned during the training phase.
    2. Depending on whether or not the connections are directed, NNs can be categorized into feedforward and recurrent networks. Feedforward networks can be utilized for static input–output mapping because they don’t have any cycles in their connections. Recurrent networks can be utilized for sequential and dynamic data processing because they contain cycles in their connections.
    3. Based on the number of hidden layers, NNs can also be categorized into shallow and deep networks. Shallow networks are useful for simple, linear issues and can contain one or more hidden layers. Complex and nonlinear issues can be addressed by deep networks, which have numerous hidden layers [77].

    4. NNs can be applied to a range of VLSI design tasks, including:

      1. Image Processing: NNs can be applied to a range of image processing applications, including restoration, enhancement, segmentation, compression, and recognition [76].
      2. Speech Recognition: NNs have the ability to identify speech signals and translate them into commands or text.
      3. Natural Language Processing: NNs are capable of understanding and generating texts in natural language for tasks including sentiment analysis, question answering, translation, and summarization3.
      4. Computer Vision: NNs are capable of handling a wide range of computer vision applications, including action recognition, object recognition, scene understanding, and face detection [76].
      5. Hardware Implementation: Implementing NNs in hardware can be implemented through the application of VLSI circuits that mimic the functionality and behavior of biological neurons and synapses, like memristors, spintronics, and neuromorphic chips.

  6. Decision Trees (DTs): Regression and classification can be carried out by DTs, which are supervised learning models. Based on specific criteria, DTs can divide the data into smaller subsets, creating a structure similar to a tree [78]. DTs can be applied to VLSI activities such as test pattern generation, fault diagnosis, reliability analysis, and yield prediction [79].

    1. DTs employ a recursive partitioning algorithm, which divides the data into two or more child nodes according to a feature and a threshold, beginning at the root node. The process of splitting keeps going until a predetermined threshold—such as a minimum quantity of samples, a maximum depth, or a purity level—is reached.
    2. Based on the nature of the problem and the data, DTs can employ a variety of splitting criteria, including information gain, the Gini index, or mean squared error. The objective of the splitting criteria is to reduce the impurity or increase the homogeneity of the child nodes.
    3. DTs can enhance the generalization performance and prevent overfitting by employing various pruning strategies, such as minimum error pruning, cost complexity pruning, or reduced error pruning [80]. The goal of pruning procedures is to eliminate branches or nodes from the tree that are hazardous or superfluous.

    4. DTs can be used for VLSI jobs like:

      1. Test Pattern Generation: By employing fault coverage, fault detection probability, or fault activation probability as the splitting criterion, DTs can be utilized to generate test patterns that are capable of detecting circuit faults.
      2. Fault Diagnosis: By classifying fault types or locations as classes and employing faulty outputs, fault models, or fault signatures as features, DTs can be used to diagnose circuit faults.
      3. Yield Prediction: Utilizing process parameters, design features, or test outcomes as features and the yield rate or yield loss as the target, DTs can be employed in predicting the chip’s yield.
      4. Reliability Analysis: By utilizing stress factors, aging effects, or failure mechanisms as features and the failure rate or failure mode as the target, DTs can be utilized to analyze the chip’s reliability [79].

10.3.3.2 Timing Analysis and Closure

Artificial intelligence and data-driven techniques are used in ML models for timing analysis and closure in VLSI design to maximize the reliability and performance of ICs. Automating the processes of determining and enhancing circuit delays, meeting timing constraints, and cutting down on design complexity and turnaround time are all possible with these techniques.

The following are some instances of ML models used in VLSI design for time analysis and closure:

  1. A Machine Learning-based Automatic Timing Closure Solution for Relative Timed Circuits [81]:

    1. One type of asynchronous circuit that employs relative timing constraints rather than absolute timing limitations is known as a relative timed circuit. This indicates that rather than the precise time values, the behavior of the circuit depends on the relative order of events.
    2. The machine learning-based approach minimizes the total run-time to timing close a design by using a RL algorithm to learn from the features throughout each iteration. The circuit topology, timing constraints, slack values, gate sizes, and buffer locations are some of the features.
    3. To determine the optimal action, such as resizing, buffering, or rewiring, for each gate or net, the RL method employs a policy network. A reward function that determines improvements in timing closure as well as decreases in area and power consumption is used to train the policy network.
    4. A value network is also used by the RL algorithm to evaluate the expected future reward for each state and direct the trade-off between exploration and exploitation [82]. Using temporal difference learning, the value network is trained by updating the value function according to the variation between the predicted and actual rewards.

  2. A Machine Learning-based Static Timing Analysis Framework:

    1. A technique for validating a circuit’s timing performance is static timing analysis, which involves computing the worst-case delays of all feasible paths [83]. The paths with the biggest delays or the smallest slacks are known as the critical paths, and they can be identified through static timing analysis.
    2. Based on parameters including gate types, fan-in and fan-out, wire lengths, and process variations, the machine learning-based framework employs a deep neural network for predicting the slack violations and path delays of a circuit.
    3. A traditional static timing analysis tool provides the target values for the supervised learning approach that is employed to train the deep neural network. In comparison to the traditional tool, the deep neural network is capable of achieving excellent accuracy and efficiency, particularly for large-scale circuits with millions of paths.
    4. In addition, the machine learning-based framework optimizes gate sizing and buffer insertion using a GA. These two methods allow circuit timing performance to be enhanced by varying drive strengths and lowering interconnection delays [84]. The deep neural network is used by the GA as a fitness function for evaluating each solution’s quality, and crossover and mutation operators are applied to generate novel solutions.

  3. A Machine Learning-based Delay Modeling Technique:

    1. A method for estimating a circuit’s interconnect delays—that is, the delays brought on by the cables connecting the gates—is called delay modeling [85]. Delays at interconnects rely on the coupling capacitance, loop resistance, transportation topology, and wire geometry.
    2. Utilizing a support vector regression model, the ML approach estimates a circuit’s interconnect delays by analyzing several characteristics such as wire diameter, wire length, wire spacing, number of bends, and slew rate [86].
    3. The training process for the support vector regression model involves supervised learning. The target values utilized in this approach are determined through a parasitic extraction tool, which extracts the resistance and capacitance values of the wires. In contrast to the parasitic extraction tool, the support vector regression model can provide superior accuracy and efficiency, particularly when applied to intricate routing topologies comprising numerous wires and vias.
    4. The technique that is based on ML additionally optimizes the routing topology, which is the configuration of the wires connecting the gates, using a gradient boosting algorithm [87]. The gradient boosting algorithm measures the interconnect delays using the support vector regression model as a loss function. It then employs a greedy search technique to identify the optimal routing topology that reduces the delays.

The vast quantity of data produced throughout the VLSI design process can be utilized by these ML models, which can offer quick and precise timing analysis and closure solutions. They are also capable of enhancing the yield and quality of the VLSI circuits by adjusting to variations and uncertainties in the fabrication process.

10.4 Challenges and Considerations

10.4.1 Data Quality and Quantity

Numerous processes related to VLSI design and technology, including layout synthesis, placement and routing, timing closure, DFM, verification, and testing, can be automated and optimized through the use of ML. Nevertheless, the implementation of ML in the design and technology of VLSI presents numerous challenges, most notably concerning the availability and quality of training data.

The data utilized to train an ML model to carry out a particular task is known as training data. The quantity and quality of training data have a direct impact on the ML model’s accuracy and performance [88]. Several challenges pertain to the availability and quality of training data in ML models for VLSI design and technology. These include:

  1. Data Scarcity: This refers to the insufficient amount of data that is available to train ML models for VLSI design and technology. This may occur as a result of the complexity and specialization of VLSI design and technology, which call for a high level of domain knowledge and expertise [89]. As a result, it is challenging and expensive to find and collect relevant data. Furthermore, access to and exchange of data between various parties may be restricted by the possibility that certain data is proprietary or confidential. The accuracy and performance of the ML model might be impacted by a scarcity of data since the model may not be able to adequately represent the complexity and variability of the data and make good generalizations to new situations [90].
  2. Data Quality: This refers to the degree of noise, incompleteness, unpredictability, or imbalance in the data that is available for training ML models for VLSI design and technology. This may occur due to the fact that VLSI design and technology deal with a number of different sources of variation and uncertainty, including environmental factors, device aging, and process variations. These may introduce the data with errors and noise. Furthermore, faulty measurement or transmission could result in some data being lost or corrupted. Furthermore, an unequal distribution of samples or labels may lead to bias or skewing of some data. Since the ML model may be overly sensitive to noise and outliers in the data and perform poorly on unseen data, data quality might have an impact on the model’s robustness and reliability [91].
  3. Data Labeling: This means that precise and consistent labels are needed for supervised learning in order to use the data that is available for training ML models for VLSI design and technology. This can occur because labeling data—such as analog signals, circuit defects, or design errors—requires a great deal of human participation and judgment in VLSI design and technology. Nonetheless, data labeling can be labor-intensive, time-consuming, and error-prone, particularly when dealing with big and complicated datasets [92]. Furthermore, labeling certain data may be challenging or ambiguous, which introduces subjectivity and human bias into the data. Data labeling may not accurately reflect the true nature and properties of the data, which might have an impact on the validity and generalizability of the ML model.
  4. Data Diversity: This indicates that the data used to train ML models for VLSI design and technology is not diverse and representative of the various scenarios and conditions the model may come across. This is possible because there are extensive number of tasks, objectives, and constraints associated with VLSI design and technology, which vary between design stages, abstraction levels, and technology nodes. As a result, it might be difficult to find and collect diverse data because it may necessitate numerous resources, experiments, and simulations. Diversity of data may prevent the ML model from being able to handle the various requirements and scenarios that it may encounter, which could have an impact on its scalability and adaptability [93].

These are a few of the major challenges with training data quality and availability in ML models for VLSI design and technology. Here are a few potential solutions to address these challenges:

  1. Data Augmentation: This method creates new data by transforming existing data using transformations like rotation, scaling, cropping, flipping, or noise addition. Reducing overfitting and enhancing the quantity and diversity of data are two benefits of data augmentation. For instance, in the field of VLSI design and technology, artificial data can be created by data augmentation and generative adversarial networks (GANs) for predicting the performance of circuit. One kind of neural network called GAN is able to learn to create realistic data from a provided distribution, like circuit data. ML models that were trained on a small training dataset may become more accurate with the use of GANs for data augmentation [94].
  2. Data Cleaning: This is a process that uses techniques like filtering, interpolation, normalization, or resampling to eliminate or correct noisy, incomplete, inconsistent, or imbalanced data [95]. Reducing underfitting or overfitting and enhancing data quality and reliability are two benefits of data cleaning. For instance, data cleaning can be used in VLSI design and technology to address a variety of causes of variation and uncertainty, including process variations, device aging, and environmental factors that may introduce errors and noise into the data. Reducing the influence of these issues on the design and testing of VLSI circuits can be achieved by data cleaning [13].
  3. Data Annotation: This is a methodology that uses automatic, semi-automatic, or manual annotation to apply labels to data for supervised learning. Data annotation may minimize human bias and error while assisting to give consistent and accurate labeling for data. For instance, data annotation can be employed in VLSI design and technology to label data for various design tasks, including physical design verification, layout generation, and optimization. ML models trained on labeled data may benefit from data annotation in terms of validity and generalizability [96]
  4. Data Synthesis: This method uses techniques like generative models, simulation, and emulation to generate novel data through scratch. In order to alleviate data scarcity and creating sufficient and relevant data for training ML models, data synthesis can be beneficial [97]. Data synthesis, for instance, can be employed in VLSI design and technology to generate data for various design scenarios and conditions, such as various design stages, abstraction levels, and technology nodes. The scalability and adaptability of ML models trained on synthetic data can be enhanced through data synthesis [13].

10.4.2 Interpretability and Explainability

Due to the fact that ML is being implemented more and more in VLSI design for tasks like layout optimization, fault detection, and performance prediction, this is a highly relevant and intricate topic. Nevertheless, the perception of ML models as “black boxes” frequently makes it challenging to comprehend how they decide what to do and what factors influence their results [98]. This presents a number of challenges for VLSI designers, including:

  1. Reliability and Robustness: In the design of VLSI, ML models must be robust and reliable. This entails the ability of the models to consistently generate accurate results amid a variety of scenarios and conditions [99]. As an illustration, a circuit performance prediction model based on ML should possess the capability to deal with fluctuations in the input data, the fabrication process, and environmental factors. Nevertheless, the reliability and robustness of ML models may be compromised by factors including bias, noise, overfitting, and underfitting. Consequently, it is imperative that VLSI designers comprehend the origins of these complications and devise methods to alleviate them through the implementation of regularization, cross-validation, and ensemble methods [100].
  2. Validation and Verification: In the design of VLSI, ML models must be validated and verified, which entails testing and inspecting them to ensure their accuracy and correctness. When maximizing a circuit layout, for instance, an ML model should be able to adhere to design constraints and specifications including area, power, and time. However, errors and uncertainties in ML models may exist, thereby impacting validation and verification processes. Consequently, VLSI designers must comprehend how to utilize metrics including accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score to determine and evaluate the quality and performance of ML models [101].
  3. Communication and Justification: In the design of VLSI, ML models must be justifiable and communicable, which means they must be capable of providing explanations and insights regarding the reasoning behind their recommendations and decisions. An illustration of this can be seen in the capability of an ML model to identify faults in a circuit, as well as to pinpoint their locations and root causes, as well as to propose potential solutions. In contrast, the communication and justification of ML models may be compromised due to their complexity and opaqueness [102]. Hence, it is imperative for VLSI designers to possess the knowledge and skills necessary to derive and display pertinent data and evidence from ML models by employing methodologies like feature importance, DTs, and saliency maps.
  4. Scalability and Efficiency: Efficiency and scalability are critical considerations in the design of VLSIs; ML models must possess the capability to process large and intricate datasets and problems [103]. An instance of this would be an ML model tasked with generating optimal solutions within a reasonable timeframe and capable of handling millions of transistors and cables when synthesizing the layout of a chip. However, the high memory and computational costs associated with ML models may impact their scalability and efficiency [104]. Hence, it is imperative for VLSI designers to comprehend the methods by which ML models’ resource and time demands can be diminished and optimized through the implementation of approximation, parallelization, and compression.
  5. Privacy and Security: In order to safeguard the sensitive and confidential information and data associated with the design process, ML models utilized in VLSI design must be both private and secure. As an illustration, an ML model that acquires knowledge from a corporation’s design data ought to refrain from disclosing or otherwise exposing the company’s trade secrets or intellectual property to unauthorized individuals. Nevertheless, ML models could potentially be susceptible to a range of threats that compromise their privacy and security, including adversarial examples, data contamination, and model theft. Consequently, VLSI designers must comprehend how to employ techniques such as adversarial training, homomorphic encryption, differential privacy, and homomorphic encryption to protect and encrypt the data and information utilized by ML models [98].

10.4.3 Hardware Constraints

ML models frequently necessitate substantial quantities of memory and power to operate, resulting in high computational demands. Certain applications, such as embedded systems, Internet of Things devices, or printed circuits, may necessitate the deployment of these models on hardware platforms with constrained resources. In regard to size, weight, power, and performance, these platforms are severely constrained (SWAP) [105]. Consequently, the integration of ML models onto such platforms presents considerable challenges and necessitates extremely careful hardware–software co-design and optimization.

The implementation of ML models on resource-constrained platforms is impacted by the following hardware constraints:

  1. Memory Capacity and Bandwidth: The amount of data that may be stored and moved between a hardware platform’s memory and its processing units depends on two factors: memory size and bandwidth. The term “memory size” describes the total amount of memory—either on-chip or off-chip—that is accessible on the platform. In general, on-chip memory (e.g., SRAM or eDRAM) exhibits enhanced speed and energy efficiency; however, it entails a higher cost and a restricted capacity. Off-chip memory, including flash and DRAM, is typically more affordable and has a greater capacity, but it operates slowly and is more energy-hungry. The rate of data transfer between the memory and the processing units is referred to as the memory bandwidth. This rate is contingent upon the bus width, clock frequency, and memory access pattern. The implementation of ML models on resource-constrained platforms may be hindered by memory capacity and bandwidth, which can impact the model’s size and complexity, the inference’s speed and energy efficiency, as well as the results’ accuracy and robustness [106].
  2. Computing Resources and Throughput: These parameters dictate the amount of computation that a hardware platform’s processing units are capable of performing [107]. The quantity and kind of processing units—which might be either general purpose or specialized—that are available on the platform are referred to as computing resources. Central Processing Units (CPUs) and Graphics Processing Units (GPUs) are some examples of general-purpose processing units that are typically more programmable and adaptable, but they are also more intricate and power-hungry. Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs) are some examples of specialized processing units that are typically more expensive and rigid, but also more efficient and customized. Throughput, which is dependent on clock frequency, latency, and pipelining of the processing units, is the amount of computing that the processing units can accomplish per unit time [108]. The implementation of ML models on resource-constrained platforms may be restricted by computing resources and throughput, which can impact the models’ complexity and parallelism, the inference process’ speed and energy efficiency, and the accuracy and performance of the results.
  3. Power Consumption and Heat Dissipation: These two parameters dictate the amount of energy that the hardware platform uses and wastes throughout the inference. The term “power consumption” describes the amount of electrical energy needed for the operation of the hardware platform, which is contingent upon component voltage, current, and switching activity. There are two types of power consumption: static power refers to the power consumed by components when they are inactive and dynamic power concerns the power consumed when the components are in operation (active). The amount of thermal energy that the hardware platform generates and releases throughout the inference is referred to as heat dissipation, and it is dependent upon the temperature, cooling mechanisms, and power consumption of the individual components. Since heat dissipation can lead to component failure, degradation, or thermal stress, it can have an impact on the hardware platform’s reliability and lifetime [109]. The implementation of ML models on resource-constrained platforms may be limited by power consumption and heat dissipation, as these factors may impact the platform’s sustainability and battery life, as well as the precision and dependability of the outcomes.

Some potential solutions to get around these hardware constraints are as follows:

  1. Model Compression and Quantization: By eliminating redundant or irrelevant layers, parameters, or operations, or by employing lower-precision arithmetic, like binary or ternary, these strategies seek to minimize the size and complexity of ML models [110]. These methods can keep the models’ accuracy and performance within reasonable bounds while lowering their memory footprint, computational complexity, and power consumption.

    1. Here are a few instances of model compression methods:

      1. Pruning: Pruning is a method of eliminating weights or neurons from a neural network that is superfluous or irrelevant, based on certain standards like magnitude, gradient, or relevance [111]. Pruning can enhance the neural network’s inference speed and energy efficiency by reducing the number of parameters and processes.
      2. Distillation: Through reducing the disparity among their outputs or intermediary representations, distillation is a strategy that moves knowledge from a large and complicated teacher model to a simplified and smaller student model. Distillation can maintain a neural network’s accuracy and performance while reducing its size and intricacy.

    2. A few instances of quantization methods are as follows:

      1. Fixed-Point Quantization: One technique that is commonly used for representing the weights and activations of a neural network is called fixed-point quantization. It involves taking floating-point numbers and converting them to fixed-point numbers, which have a defined number of bits for the integer and fractional components [112]. In addition to facilitating the use of faster and simplified arithmetic units, such as multipliers and adders, fixed-point quantization can minimize the bandwidth and memory size.
      2. Binary or Ternary Quantization: One way to further minimize the number of bits needed to represent the weights and activations of a neural network is to utilize binary or ternary quantization, which can reduce the number of bits to either one bit (binary) or two bits (ternary) [112]. In addition to the use of bitwise operations, such as Exclusive OR (XOR) and Exclusive NOR (also known as equivalence) (XNOR), which are considerably quicker and more energy-efficient than arithmetic operations, binary or ternary quantization can substantially reduce memory size and bandwidth.

  2. Neural Architecture Search and Hardware-Aware Optimization: One approach that seeks to identify the optimum neural network architecture and implementation for a specific hardware platform and task is through the utilization of hardware-aware optimization and neural architecture search [113]. In order to explore the design space of neural architecture, hardware implementation, and quantization, as well as to maximize the trade-off among accuracy, latency, and energy efficiency, these methods may employ learning-based approaches like RL or evolutionary algorithms. The following are instances of techniques involving hardware-aware optimization and neural architecture search:

    1. ProxylessNAS: ProxylessNAS is a methodology that employs no surrogate or proxy models, including accuracy or latency predictors, in its search for the optimum neural network architecture on the target hardware platform [114]. ProxylessNAS uses gradient-based techniques that simultaneously maximize accuracy and latency goals in order to determine the optimal architecture for each given hardware platform, including smartphones, GPUs, and FPGAs.
    2. HAQ: Hardware-Aware Quantization (HAQ) is a methodology that autonomously ascertains the optimum bit-width for each layer of a neural network, taking into consideration the hardware feedback and the accuracy constraint [115]. HAQ has the capability to discover a quantization policy that reduces the neural network’s latency or energy consumption on the target hardware platform, including CPUs, GPUs, or FPGAs, through the utilization of RL.

  3. Non-Conventional Computing Approaches: The objective of non-conventional computing approaches is to perform ML processes more effectively and scalably by taking benefit of the physical characteristics of innovative materials or devices, including memristors, spintronics, or quantum dots [116]. In order to construct ML models with reduced power consumption, higher density, and enhanced speed, these methods can take advantage of the inherent parallelism, stochasticity, or approximation of these materials or devices. A few instances of non-conventional computing methods are as follows:

    1. Memristive Computing: This method employs memristors—resistive devices that can store and alter their resistance state in response to applied voltage or current—to implement neural network functions like activation functions, matrix multiplication, and weight storage. Memristive computing employs crossbar arrays of memristors to accomplish parallel and analog computations, allowing for high-density and low-power neural network implementation [117].
    2. Spintronic Computing: Spintronic computing is a neural network technology that leverages spintronics—devices that control the spin of electrons instead of their charge—to perform neural network operations like logic gates, oscillators, and weight storage. By utilizing spin torque nano-oscillators, domain wall magnets, or magnetic tunnel junctions, spintronic computing can implement NNs with reduced power and high speed while carrying out non-volatile and stochastic computations [118].

10.5 Future Trends

Emerging technologies and trends in this field include the following:

  1. Machine Learning for the Automation of VLSI Design: The layout optimization of VLSI circuits, for instance, can be automated, utilizing ML. The process of arranging the circuit components and interconnections on a chip so as to reduce its area, power consumption, and delay is known as layout optimization. This process has traditionally been performed manually or with heuristic algorithms, both of which are time-consuming and prone to error. In contrast, the layout optimization can be accomplished autonomously and effectively through the use of ML techniques, including RL, GAs, and NNs. In order to generate optimized layouts that adhere to the design specifications and constraints, ML algorithms have the capability to acquire knowledge from pre-existing layouts and data [119].

  2. Machine Learning for VLSI Circuit Performance Prediction and Optimization: ML, for instance, can be utilized for predicting and optimizing VLSI circuit performance in a variety of operating conditions [120]. A number of factors, including temperature, voltage, and process variations, can have an impact on the performance of VLSI circuits. These factors may lead to deviations in the circuits’ expected behavior and performance, which may result in failure or degradation. In addition to optimizing circuit parameters like power consumption, speed, and reliability, ML algorithms are also capable of analyzing and modeling the behavior and performance of VLSI circuits under various operating conditions. ML algorithms have the ability to acquire knowledge from historical data and make precise predictions about how a circuit will perform under various operating conditions [121]. This makes it possible for designers to efficiently attain the targeted performance goals and optimize the circuit parameters. These VLSI circuits and systems include, for instance:

    1. Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) and Application-Specific Integrated Circuits (ASICs) are reconfigurable and customizable VLSI circuits and systems that possess the ability to implement particular ML algorithms and architectures with maximum flexibility and efficiency. In addition to supporting dynamic reconfiguration, pipelining, and parallelism, ASICs and FPGAs can offer specific hardware resources, such as multipliers, adders, memory, and logic, for ML computations [122].
  3. Machine Learning for VLSI Circuit Innovation and Exploration: Through its ability to explore unconventional design spaces and produce creative solutions, ML, for example, can be utilized to generate unique and innovative VLSI architectures and circuits. Neural network accelerators, quantum computing circuits, and nanoelectronic devices are just a few examples of the unique and innovative VLSI architectures and circuits that can be generated by ML algorithms. Additionally, ML techniques have the potential to bridge the gap between VLSI design and forthcoming technologies like nanoelectronics, quantum computing, and neuromorphic computing [123]. In order to provide innovative and optimum solutions that go beyond conventional design paradigms, ML algorithms may acquire knowledge from pre-existing data and designs.

  4. VLSI for ML Inference and Training: The two primary stages of ML applications are ML inference and training, and VLSI is capable of providing the hardware support for both [124]. The procedure of implementing a trained ML model to novel data is referred to as ML inference, whereas the procedure of learning an ML model from data is known as ML training. VLSI is capable of implementing training and inference engines that can accurately and efficiently carry out ML algorithms. Moreover, VLSI is capable of supporting a variety of ML models, including supervised, unsupervised, and RL.

    1. For instance, inference engines, which are hardware devices that are capable of performing ML inference tasks like speech recognition, image classification, and natural language processing, can be implemented using VLSI [125]. The high-speed, low-power, parallel computing capabilities needed for ML inference can be achieved with VLSI. NNs, convolutional networks, and recurrent networks are just a few examples of the specialized architectures and circuits that VLSI can implement for certain ML models. The accuracy, latency, and energy consumption of the inference engines can all be optimized with VLSI.
    2. One other instance is the application of VLSI for training engines, which are hardware devices that are capable of carrying out machine learning training tasks, like figuring out an ML model’s parameters from an extensive dataset. The scalable, high-memory, high-performance computing capabilities needed for ML training can be achieved through VLSI. Stochastic gradient descent, backpropagation, gradient descent, and other ML models and algorithms can be facilitated by a variety of general-purpose architectures and circuits that can be implemented using VLSI. The training engines’ stability, convergence, and speed can all be optimized with VLSI [126].

ML has a very wide and promising future in the integration of VLSI design because it can offer a variety of advantages and answers to the issues and challenges that the VLSI industry faces. The following are a few potential future applications of ML in VLSI design:

  1. Machine Learning for Advanced Lithography and Nanofabrication: The lithography and fabrication processes grow more intricate and challenging as VLSI technology scales descend to the nanometer regime, necessitating a high degree of precision and accuracy. The lithography and fabrication processes, including mask design, optical proximity correction, etching, and deposition, can be modeled and optimized with the assistance of ML [127]. Defects and variations in nanoscale devices can also be detected and corrected with the guidance of ML.
  2. Machine Learning for Hardware Security and Reliability: These are essential features of VLSI design, particularly for applications including aerospace, biometrics, and cryptography that deal with sensitive and crucial data and operations. ML has the potential to improve the security and reliability of VLSI circuits through the detection and prevention of hardware attacks, including counterfeiting, hardware Trojans, and side-channel attacks, as well as by enhancing the circuits’ fault tolerance and error correction capabilities [128].

10.6 Conclusion

We have introduced the fundamental concepts and principles of VLSI and ML, as well as their interrelation, in this chapter. The application of ML techniques to diverse facets of VLSI design and manufacturing, including layout optimization, fault detection and diagnosis, performance prediction and optimization, and exploration of novel design possibilities, has been the subject of our discourse. Recent research projects and applications that illustrate the advantages and difficulties of integrating ML into VLSI have been examined. For instance, we have seen how ML can automate the time-consuming and error-prone process of layout optimization, which is traditionally performed manually, by generating optimized layouts that reduce power consumption and enhance signal integrity through learning from existing layouts. Furthermore, we have observed how ML can improve the reliability and robustness of VLSI circuits through the identification of anomalous patterns in vast amounts of circuit data, diagnosis of the faults, and recommendation of potential solutions. Furthermore, we have seen how ML can enhance the power efficiency and performance of VLSI circuits through the optimization of design parameters, including voltage, frequency, and temperature, and by analyzing and learning from historical data to accurately predict the performance of a circuit under various operating conditions. In addition, we have observed how ML can enable the investigation of novel design possibilities by generating inventive circuit architectures and exploring unconventional design spaces, including quantum computing and neuromorphic computing, using ML algorithms.
