11 Application of Artificial Intelligence/Machine Learning in VLSI Design

Abhay Pratap Singh, Vimal Kumar Mishra, and Shamim Akhter

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-11

11.1 Introduction

Historically, computers were operated by large vacuum tubes. At the time, they had the title of being the most rapid, but the advent of semiconductor transistors in 1948 by Bell Laboratories ushered in a new era for the entire computing system. This allows the devices to be smaller, more affordable, and more efficient. In the 1960s, we employed a method called small-scale integration (SSI) that utilized approximately ten transistors, using a low-density integration approach. During the late 1960s, we achieved the integration of approximately 100 transistors on a single chip, a technological advancement referred to as medium-scale integration (MSI). In the early 1970s, this integration increased with the help of transistor-transistor logic (TTL), which enables space for higher density integration. In the late 1980s, the integration of transistors crossed the mark of 1000 transistors on a single chip and was called VLSI. Due to power consumption and the need for a higher number of gates on a single chip, Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (MOS) gradually replaced TTL. These integration evaluations follow Moore’s law, which predicts that the number of transistors on a single chip will increase exponentially and double in nearly two years. For the last five decades, this industry has followed suit [1,2]. Reducing the transistor size achieves this phenomenon. As the transistor size decreases, the transistor density on the chip increases and the transistor’s performance improves [3]. This process will help the semiconductor industry grow as it faces the need for portable devices. Scaling VLSI circuits can achieve the small size and efficient power consumption necessary for portability. This continuous scaling growth is bringing our semiconductor industries to a new high. Devices are currently reducing in size to sub-3 nm gate size regimes and even smaller. Currently, Complementary Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (CMOS) technology is the dominant and revolutionary technology in the field of microelectronics. As CMOS technology downscaling increases, it enables short-channel effects (SCEs), which include drain-induced barrier lowering (DIBL), leakage, a decrease in gain, channel length modulation, and threshold voltage roll-off [4,5]. As a result, as miniaturization increases, the dependence of device characteristics on various parameters increases, and the sensitivity of process variation in manufacturing also increases, ultimately influencing chip yield [6]. Therefore, we must use advanced and affordable design techniques in our VLSI design processes that are sustainable for future growth in the semiconductor industry. The VLSI Computer-Aided Design (CAD) tool offers an essential role in multiple stages of the chip design process. Modern chip design and performance evaluation are dependent on the capabilities of these CAD tools. The semiconductor chips’ turnout time heavily varies on the effectiveness of the Electronic Design Automation (EDA) tools to overcome the aforementioned limitations. The conventional EDA works on rule-driven methodologies, and when the complexity of the IC increases, it results in process variation and environmental variables. The result is a time delay [7]. As the count of transistors on a chip increases, advancements in CAD tools are required. Many studies show that we can use an artificial network-based solution to our EDA process to achieve quick convergence, which provides lesser time consumption and high efficiency [8]. To provide a solution to these constraints, many researchers are looking toward artificial intelligence (AI). AI mirrors human intelligence and can perform complex tasks. AI includes machine learning (ML), which can readily identify trends and patterns in massive datasets and forecast the output of the desired input. This field has witnessed significant expansion in recent times, mostly due to its resilience and extensive range of applications. Recently, ML has found extensive applications in various disciplines such as mathematics, physics, chemistry, computer vision, medicine, and image processing [9, 10, 11, 12]. These ML methods operate at high computational rates and are capable of handling heterogeneous and multivariant data. Because of these advantages, researchers are incorporating AI and ML into various design processes within the VLSI design flow. This chapter summarizes the various research studies conducted in VLSI design at various levels.

11.2 VLSI Design Flow

The typical integrated circuit (IC) design process consists of multiple hierarchical layers, as shown in Figure 11.1. This encompasses the back- and front-end stages of IC design. The design flow delineates the functions, interface, and overarching structure of the IC being examined. Design specifications provide a detailed description of a digital circuit’s functionality, interface, and architecture. This includes information like block diagrams, propagation lags, packaging type, and design limitations. Design specifications also serve as a formal contract between the customer and the manufacturer. The architectural planning layer encompasses the fundamental structure of a system, including the functionality descriptions of its subsystem elements. The behavioral design level, also known as the functional design level, is typically described using Verilog HDL/VHDL. The functionality is described in a general way, while the specific details of how it is implemented are hidden. A high-level synthesis (HLS) tool can convert system specifications written in C or C++ into high-level hardware description language (HDL) or create a gate-level interpretation based on the high-level behavioral interpretation. Test bench simulation is used to verify the logic, while design for testability (DFT) is used for formal verification and scan insertion. These methods are employed to investigate the Register Transfer Level (RTL) mapping [13]. The process of system partitioning is used to break down large and complex systems into smaller modules. This is followed by floor layout, placement, and routing. The floor planner is responsible for estimating the necessary IC space for implementing standard cell/module design and optimizing design performance. The place-and-route tools are accountable for the positioning of sub-modules, gates, and flip-flops. Verifying the layout is essential to ensure that the design aligns with the source schematic and adheres to the electrical and physical design principles.

Once the post-layout simulation is finished, the chip undergoes the sign-off stage, where parasitic resistance and capacitance are extracted and verified [14]. The fabrication of ICs includes a multitude of intricate chemical and physical processes, including lithography, which encompasses the creation, verification, and delineation of masks and materials in specific locations. Repeating this step multiple times is crucial due to alterations in the process and the downsizing of technology nodes. Once the chips have been manufactured, we proceed to divide the wafers into smaller sections, separate the individual chips, package each chip, and conduct thorough testing to ensure that they function properly and meet the specified design criteria [15,16].

11.3 Artificial Intelligence/Machine Learning

Statistical learning is an important tool in many scientific and technological disciplines because it enables the study of huge volumes of data to make future predictions. AI, a technology that simulates human behavior, has a subset of ML and deep learning, as shown in Figure 11.2. We may categorize ML under three types: supervised learning, unsupervised learning, and reinforcement learning. Supervised learning assigns output labels to all elements in the given data, while unsupervised learning simply uses input variables, and semi-supervised learning uses data in which some are labeled examples and the remaining are unlabeled [17].

11.3.1 Supervised Learning

Supervised learning is an ML technique that involves algorithm learning patterns using labeled information. It entails training a model on a set of data in which every input is linked to a corresponding output. Throughout the training process, the model optimizes its parameters to reduce the discrepancy between its predictions and the actual labels. This allows the model to generalize well and make precise predictions on fresh, unseen data. Supervised learning can be categorized into two primary forms: classification and regression. Classification is a supervised learning task where algorithms assign input data to predefined categories or classes. Models learn from labeled examples to provide predictions on previously unobserved data. These models, known as classifiers, provide predictions about discrete categorical class labels. Regression is a supervised learning task that predicts continuous numerical values by analyzing the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables. We use this fundamental statistical technique to forecast and understand data trends [18,19]. These processes are obtainable as a learning function that aims to anticipate a cartography of Y = f(X), where Y represents a set of variables that are outputs for a given set of input variables X. We calculate the cartography function to forecast the corresponding class label Y for a given set of new input variables X, as shown in Figure 11.3.

Some of the most common methods for regression and classification are decision trees (DTs), gradient boosting machines (GBMs), random forests (RFs), support vector machines (SVMs), and ensemble learning [20].

11.3.2 Unsupervised Learning

Unsupervised learning is a type of ML where the model is trained using input data that does not have labeled responses. The algorithm autonomously analyzes the data to reveal patterns or structures without any explicit instructions on what to search for. Instead, its objective is to identify intrinsic links or groupings within the data, typically using methods such as clustering, dimensionality reduction, or density estimation. Unsupervised learning is especially beneficial for problems with limited or nonexistent labeled data, as it enables the discovery of the underlying structure of the dataset [21]. Some common methods used in unsupervised learning are clustering, association rule learning, dimensionality reduction, and anomaly detection. Clustering is the process of grouping together data points that are alike, while dimensionality reduction techniques reduce feature complexity. Association rule learning identifies relationships between variables, while anomaly detection flags outliers. Density estimation estimates the distribution of data, and generative modeling learns to generate new samples. Common algorithms include K-nearest neighbors, Apriori algorithm, Isolation Forest, kernel density estimation, K-means clustering, and principal component analysis [22].

11.3.3 Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement learning is a subfield of ML that focuses on finding the best actions to take in different scenarios to achieve the maximum numerical reward. Its primary emphasis is on learning that is oriented toward achieving certain goals through interactions. Reinforcement learning is the method of acquiring knowledge via experience and identifying the most optimal approach that optimizes a reward signal [23].

11.3.4 Deep Learning

Deep learning is a kind of ML that employs artificial neural networks having numerous layers to uncover intricate patterns across huge datasets. These neural networks, which are designed based on the anatomy and functioning of the brain of a person, have the capacity to automatically learn representations of data [24]. Multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) are very important parts of systems for deep learning. There are many levels of nodes (neurons) in an MLP. There is an input layer, one or multiple hidden layers, and an output layer. As shown in Figure 11.4, we refer to it as a densely connected network because each neuron in one layer connects to every neuron in the layer below it. Various deep learning models are shallow neural networks, deep reinforcement learning, generative adversarial networks, recurrent neural networks, and deep neural networks.

11.4 Application of AI/ML at Circuit Simulation Level

Simulation is critical in IC device modeling because performance evaluation is difficult in the nano-scale zone due to process and environmental variables [25,26]. To get the appropriate characteristics, the device needs to be carefully examined. Early detection of functional and electrical performance changes can increase IC yield. AI and ML algorithms can assist, which improves turnaround time and performance, resulting in less design effort. Many researchers presented substitute methods for defining device characterization parameters [27]. Researchers proposed numerous learning algorithms that characterize devices at numerous layers of abstraction, including device and gate levels. Many researchers worked on different stages of simulation, including device, gate, circuit, RTL, and post-layout simulation levels. Many researchers are developing ML/AI models to predict the performance of devices using small sets of simulation results. The ML/AI algorithms help to analyze and optimize the characteristics of the device, computing the parametric yield with extremely high speed with an accuracy greater than 98%. Figure 11.5 shows the basic building blocks of the application of ML and AI at the device-level simulation process.

In 2019, Carrillo et al. developed an NN model for TCAD device modeling, utilizing 1380 training data points from junction-less nanowire transistors. The model demonstrated that ML techniques could reduce a simulation worktime of 5520 to 2 minutes [28]. In 2021, Yoon and team used TCAD and ML to maximize the efficiency and effectiveness of vertical nanowire field-effect transistors. The completely linked multilayer perceptron neural network accurately predicted input parameters, minimizing RC delay [29]. In 2022, Akbar and team used an ML model to find an efficient solution for sophisticated device simulations of line TFETs. The model had an R2-score greater than 99% and below 1% of error rate, reducing computing expenses by nearly 99% [30]. Stillmaker et al. [31] offered a dataset and a method for modeling the performance of transistors at technology nodes spanning from 180 nanometers to 7 nanometers. In 2019, Amuru and colleagues [32] presented a regression model for estimating static leakage power and total power dissipation in a sub-nanodevice. They suggested multivariate linear regression (MLR) and multivariate polynomial regression (MPR), and their models had less than 1% inaccuracy. Khang et al. (2015) demonstrated an ML-based methodology that can predict incremental delay induced by SI and path delay, and this methodology got the worst-case error for these two as 5.2 and 8.2 ps each [33]. The paper [34] proposed a precise power estimation method for CMOS VLSI circuits using random forest, which outperforms neural networks. The results demonstrated a strong correlation with the ISCAS’89 benchmark circuits. The paper [35] introduced a ResNet-based digital circuit optimization framework that is both quick and efficient. The framework focuses on minimizing leakage and delay. The findings from the study of 22 nano-meter Metal Gate High-K digital cells demonstrated a significant decrease of 36.7% in delay and 18.8% in leakage when employing a genetic algorithm.

11.5 Application of AI/ML at Architectural Level

The modern era inspires most architectural improvements with the need for high processing speed, a dependable system, and reduced development costs and time. AI and ML significantly enhance architectural level VLSI design by enabling HLS and swift exploration of architectural options. AI models help designers quickly assess a wide range of design options by predicting parameters related to performance, power usage, and area. ML techniques enhance the quality of outputs regarding performance, area, and power efficiency by optimizing the translation of high-level code (such as C/C++) into HDLs. Architects may create more effective and efficient VLSI designs by using AI and ML to make better-informed judgments, cut down on design iterations, and speed up the entire process. The inference phase of neural networks was sped up by Neural Network Processing-In-Memory (NNPIM), which was suggested by Gupta and his team [36] in 2019. In order to make the inference phase of neural networks work more quickly, they used crossbar memory-based architecture and weight sharing to lower the amount of computation that neural networks need to do. They achieved an energy efficiency that is 131.5 times higher and a speedup that is 48.2 times faster than conventional neural networks. Yang et al. [37] examined the energy usage of data transfer in the von Neumann architecture using a computing-in-memory architecture based on Static Random-Access Memory (SRAM). The architecture is transformed from being focused on computing to being focused on data, resulting in an energy-efficient design that fully eliminates the computing bottleneck of the von Neumann architecture. They addressed the issue of read/write stability in SRAM. The study demonstrated that a computing-in-memory architecture consisting of 96000 SRAM arrays successfully resolved the problem of read/write stability in 6T SRAM.

In the VLSI architecture design, systolic arrays use a grid of processing elements (PEs) to handle data in parallel and in a pipeline, which speeds up computations as much as possible. This design makes tasks like matrix multiplication and neural network operations faster. It also lowers the amount of extra work that needs to be done when sending data, and it makes semiconductor devices more scalable and energy-efficient. In 2019, Asgari and team [38] demonstrated that systolic array solutions can accelerate DNN inference and are extremely valuable for dense linear algebra. However, implementing sparsification mechanisms during or after training makes the issue sparse. To circumvent this issue, they proposed an Eridanus method for structured pruning that generates DNNs that operate with a constant flow of data from memory to systolic arrays. In 2020, Parmar et al. [39] introduced a hardware-effective and non-multiplier method for convolutions in CNNs. Furthermore, in comparison with their time structures, they suggested a novel dataflow model featuring a reconfigurable systolic ring architecture that increases resource efficiency by 55% and decreases power consumption by the same amount. In 2024, Inayat et al. [40] presented two novel systolic arrays that use factoring and radix-8 multipliers. The first model reduced the delay and area by increasing the number of registers, but this was balanced by the lower requirement of registers in the pipeline of radix-8 offsets. This model gets better results by 15%, 13%, and 23%, respectively, for delay, area, and power. The second one can compress interconnects with two steps of hard-multiple addition; this achieves maximum reductions in area of 11.7% and maximum reduction in power of 16.7%.

11.6 Application of AI/ML at Physical Design Level

AI and ML greatly improve the physical design stage of VLSI by simplifying critical operations, including placement, routing, and layout verification. ML algorithms have the capability to forecast and enhance placement tactics, resulting in the reduction of wire length and improvement of timing. AI tools in routing aid in the effective exploration of route paths to manage congestion and minimize delays. AI-powered design rule checking (DRC) utilizes AI to anticipate and address possible rule infractions, hence enhancing the feasibility of manufacturing processes. In addition, ML models optimize layouts to improve power distribution and thermal management, hence boosting the overall performance of the design. These technological breakthroughs decrease the amount of time required for designing, increase the productivity of VLSI circuits, and improve their dependability.

In 2021, A. Mirhoseini et al. demonstrated a deep reinforcement learning model for chip floor layout and developed an edge-based graph convolutional neural network architecture capable of learning rich and transferable chip representations. This architecture can save hundreds of hours on modern accelerator design. Google used this approach to create their processors [41]. In 2021, Xie et al. [42] established a deep RL solution for an ML model by utilizing a constraint solver. This methodology improves random search and simulated annealing by 6.11% and 5.85%, respectively, while also cutting search time from 3 hours to 9 minutes. Tsung-Wei Huang [43] focused on the optimization of GPU acceleration in electronic design automation through placement and timing analysis. In order to accomplish this, he utilized ML to implement a novel heterogeneous parallel EDA solution. He was able to reach speeds that are 500 times faster for GPU acceleration path-based timing analysis. In 2021, Huang et al. [44] addressed the solution to global routing congestion in physical design with the help of an ML model. This model generates predictions of routing violations and provides them back to the system, which creates new placements with improvements.

In 2022, Yeh et al. [45] employed supervised ML techniques to tackle the challenges associated with substrate signal routing in high-density packages. The researchers introduced an innovative rerouting framework that can anticipate inefficient routing and yield improvements of 36.5% and 1.6% for wire band count and wire length, respectively.

11.7 Application of AI/ML at Manufacturing and Reliability Level

AI and ML are transforming VLSI production by improving process control, defect prediction, and reliability analysis. AI improves process parameters in real time, increasing yield and eliminating faults in semiconductor manufacturing. ML methods forecast potential faults based on historical data, allowing for proactive corrective actions and reducing production losses. Furthermore, AI-driven reliability analysis uses stress test data to estimate long-term device performance, resulting in robust and reliable devices. These applications streamline manufacturing, cut costs, and improve overall VLSI device quality and yield, thereby promoting semiconductor industry efficiency and innovation. Mansi Phute and her team [46] emphasized the significance of lithography in VLSI fabrication. In addition, they examined the utilization of different ML models, including artificial neural networks (ANNs), convolutional neural networks (CNNs), and SVMs, for the purpose of identifying hotspots. The authors illustrated the potential for false-negative outcomes resulting from imbalanced datasets and proposed the use of Gaussian process assurance in order to address this problem. In 2021, Meng and team [47] suggested innovative ML models for etch bias applications and a unique data structure for these models. Additionally, they presented a new theory based on the edge placement error. Ying Chen and his colleagues [48] addressed the problem of acquiring a large dataset for ML applications in hotspot detection. The authors provided a method for detecting hotspots using a self-paced, multi-task learning approach in a semi-supervised manner. They achieved a higher accuracy of 4.6%–6.5% compared to conventional ML methods, while maintaining the same false alarm levels. This improvement is shown even when using only 10%–50% of the training data.

AI and ML improve the testing process of VLSI design by optimizing the development of test patterns, diagnosing faults, and predicting yield. ML algorithms produce optimized test patterns that enhance fault coverage and minimize test duration, hence guaranteeing thorough testing with little resource utilization. AI-powered fault diagnosis rapidly detects and pinpoints flaws, hence minimizing the duration required for troubleshooting and fixing. In addition, ML algorithms make predictions about manufacturing yield and detect possible problem sites by analyzing test data and historical patterns. These developments raise the precision of tests, decrease the expenses associated with testing, and improve the overall dependability and excellence of VLSI devices. Soham Roy and his colleagues [49] demonstrated a system for generating test patterns automatically using ML. They combined an unsupervised ML model with a decision-making system that is focused on paths. This approach minimized the need for backtracking and significantly decreased the amount of Central Processing Unit (CPU) time required. In 2021, Liu and Ou [50] developed a method using ML to increase the efficiency of system-level testing, reduce testing duration, and manage the number of defective components per million. The pseudo-probability of a chip’s failure in the system-level test procedure was estimated using SVM and auto-encode. They proved that their approach has the capacity to decrease the expense of system-level testing by 40%.

11.8 Conclusion

This chapter demonstrated the utilization of AI and ML in multiple areas of VLSI logical and physical planning, which include circuit simulation, architecture, physical design, testing, and production. These examples illustrate the broad range of uses of AI and ML in the initial phases of VLSI design. AI/ML at the system level assists in architecture discovery and HLS. These technologies assist in optimizing code and detecting bugs for RTL design, thereby improving code quality and reducing the need for verification. AI/ML enhances the test generation, formal verification, and simulation processes during verification, ensuring comprehensive and efficient validation. AI in logic synthesis optimizes synthesis parameters and assists in obtaining timing closure, hence improving design quality. AI/ML techniques enhance the placement and routing of components, conduct design rule verification, and optimize the layout during the physical design phase to ensure efficient manufacturing and high yield. DFT utilizes AI to produce optimized test patterns, identify flaws, and forecast manufacturing yield, thereby guaranteeing reliable testing and achieving high production yields. AI/ML plays a crucial role in manufacturing by improving process control, forecasting faults, and evaluating reliability. This ultimately leads to enhanced production quality and an increased gadget lifespan. Integrating AI and ML into the VLSI design pipeline has substantial prospects for optimizing efficiency, decreasing time to market, and improving the overall quality of semiconductor designs. We anticipate that the ongoing progress of AI and ML technologies will broaden their utilization in VLSI design, thereby bringing about a further revolution in the sector. To achieve the desired implementation, it is crucial to possess interdisciplinary competence, which involves integrating an understanding of VLSI design principles with modern AI and ML methodologies. Furthermore, a persistent partnership between academics and industry would stimulate advancements and tackle the changing obstacles in VLSI design.

12 FinFET-Based 9T SRAM for Enhanced Performance in AI/ML Applications

Yagnadatt Sarangi, Kartik Singh, and Shelja Kaushal

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-12

12.1 Introduction

In the current world, the need for more efficient chip design has increased and therefore the need for more efficient circuits that consume less power with a fast response. In the realm of VLSI (very large-scale integration), the quest for a transistor which meets the requirement has increased. For a long time, CMOS (complementary metal oxide transistor) has significantly contributed to develop the electronic devices and attain our objectives. But by looking at the present scenario, there is a need for higher performance, less power consumption, and less delay. CMOS inabilities are becoming very evident to compete these needs. CMOS-based circuits face major problems like current leakage, more power consumption, and extreme short channel effects (SCEs) which limit its performance. To confront all these problems, the semiconductor industry has emerged with a revolutionary alternative: FinFET (fin field-effect transistor). The FinFET technology resists various SCEs very efficiently and hence provides calibre to reach new stages of efficiency in VLSI [1].

One of the important components in chip design is the SRAM (static random access memory) arrays. An essential component within SRAM arrays, the SRAM cell serves as the cornerstone for temporary data storage in computer systems and various electronic devices. Being a form of volatile memory, it necessitates a continuous power supply to uphold the stored data. Within the realm of VLSI design, SRAM cells hold significant importance by facilitating rapid data access and are frequently integrated as cache memory and internal registers within Central Processing Units (CPUs). The straightforwardness and swiftness of SRAM render it well-suited for tasks demanding swift data retrieval and the necessity for refreshing cycles. Moreover, modern microprocessors incorporate SRAM cells extensively across expensive buffers and caches, thereby enhancing their operational efficiency and overall performance. SRAM cells have a latch that stores data for as long as the power is on, eliminating the need for refresh operations. SRAM is commonly utilized as cache memory in microprocessors, mainframe computers, engineering workstations, and handheld devices due to its high speed and low power consumption. An SRAM stores each bit on four transistors, forming two cross-coupled inverters. The power consumption and speed of SRAM have led to several designs aimed at minimizing power usage during read and write operations. Modern computing systems rely heavily on high-performance on-chip caches to support their memory structure [2].

Emerging SRAM circuits signify a significant leap forward in memory technology, surpassing traditional 6T SRAM designs in several crucial aspects. Emerging SRAM circuits, incorporating elements from various configurations such as 8T, 7T, 9T, 10T, and 11T, present a compelling amalgamation of reliability, efficiency, and performance, positioning them as pivotal components in the landscape of future memory architectures. Moreover, these innovative circuits exhibit remarkable stability during low-power operation, rendering them exceptionally suitable for energy-efficient applications, such as Internet of Things (IoT) devices and mobile electronics. Among these architectures, the 8T and 10T SRAM configurations contribute their unique attributes; the former excels in balancing area efficiency and stability, while the latter amplifies robustness and performance, albeit at the cost of increased area overhead. Additionally, the 7T and 11T SRAM designs offer specialized solutions, with the former prioritizing low-power operation and the latter emphasizing enhanced reliability in harsh environments. But among the array of emerging designs, the 9T SRAM configuration emerges as a standout due to its heightened resilience against diverse forms of noise and disturbances, including process variations and radiation-induced errors. Furthermore, the 9T SRAM excels in low-power operation, making it an ideal choice for energy-efficient applications, which may not be as effectively addressed by the 7T or 11T designs. The 9T SRAM emerges as the preferred choice among its counterparts, offering a compelling combination of area efficiency, stability, reliability, and low-power operation, thus positioning it as a key component in advanced memory architectures.

In this chapter, we have proposed a 9T SRAM using 18 nm FinFET technology. To employ with the circuit design and various simulations, Cadence Virtuoso tool is used [3,4]. The various sections in this chapter are as follows: Section 12.2 explains the working of 9T SRAM and its various operations, Section 12.3 employs the optimization of FinFET-based 9T SRAM for low power and increased stability. While Section 12.4 shows comparative and environment variation analysis followed by conclusion in Section 12.5.

12.2 FinFET-Based 9T SRAM

In this section, the proposed 9T SRAM using FinFET technology is explained. As the characteristics of FinFET technology is a substantial improvement over traditional CMOS technology, notably in terms of stability enhancement, by enabling better control over current flow, FinFET technology reduces the hazards of data corruption caused by leakage currents or noise in SRAM cells. This increased stability correlates to greater reliability and data integrity in a variety of computing settings [4]. The 9T FinFET-based SRAM cell has nine transistors placed in an optimal arrangement for stability, durability, and performance as shown in Figure 12.1. The cell’s heart is made up of six storage transistors, which include two access transistors and two cross-coupled inverters. The read and write operations are controlled by access transistors, which provide access to the SRAM cell’s storage nodes (bit lines). The cross-coupled inverters comprise the cell’s latching mechanism, which stores data in a stable manner. Each inverter is made up of an n-type Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (NMOS) and a p-type Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (PMOS) transistor coupled in a feedback loop to ensure reliable data retention and rapid access times. In addition to the storage transistors, the 9T FinFET SRAM cell contains three auxiliary transistors that improve its stability and dependability. The pass transistors add extra routes for manipulating the states of the cross-coupled inverters, hence enhancing the cell’s noise immunity and read stability. The assist transistor improves the read margin while reducing the effects of process fluctuations and external noise. Hence, the FinFET-based 9T SRAM cells may provide higher energy efficiency, particularly during standby or idle states, which contributes to overall power savings and longer battery life in mobile and battery-powered devices.

12.2.1 Operations of SRAM

SRAM circuits play a crucial role in modern electronic devices, providing fast and reliable data storage. Understanding the operational modes of SRAM cells, such as the hold, write, and read modes, is essential for comprehending their functionality.

Hold Operation: During the hold operation, the SRAM cell remains in an idle state, with data held in its latch without any alterations until the next read or write command is initiated. To maintain the stored data during this phase, the access transistors within the cell are disconnected by setting the word line (WL) to a low voltage or ground level (WL = 0). As a result, access transistors NM2 and NM7 are turned off, effectively isolating the storage nodes, which are composed of cross-coupled inverter FinFETs (PM0, PM1, NM1, and NM4) from the bit lines. This isolation ensures that the stored data remains intact and unaffected by external disturbances.

Write Operation: In the write mode, the objective is to modify the data stored in the SRAM cell. This process involves placing the desired data values, either logic 0 or logic 1, on the Bit Lines (BL) and BL-bar. Typically, logic 0 is stored at node 1, while logic 1 is stored at node 2, leading to specific configurations of FinFETs within the cell, i.e. PM3 and NM1 being turned ON while PM1 and NM4 are turned OFF. To perform the write operation, the WL is activated, setting it to a high voltage (WL = 1), thereby turning on the access FinFETs. Subsequently, the bit lines BL and BL-bar are set to the desired data values intended to be written into the cell. This action triggers a voltage difference across the cell, causing certain FinFETs to turn on or off selectively, e.g. if BL = 1 and BL-bar = 0, then PM1 and NM4 will turn ON while NM1 and PM3 are forced to turn OFF. Consequently, the stored data is overwritten, with node 1 being updated to logic 1 and node 2 being updated to logic 0.

Read Operation: In the read mode, the SRAM cell retrieves the stored data for further processing. Assuming logic 0 is stored at node 1, M4 and M1 are turned on, leading to node 2 charging up to the supply voltage (VDD) while PM1 and NM4 remain off. Enabling the access transistors connecting the bit lines to the storage nodes by activating the WL (WL = 1), the bit lines BL and BL-bar are precharged to VDD. This precharging initiates a potential voltage difference, causing a current to flow through NM2. Consequently, the sensed voltage difference between BL and BL-bar determines the stored data value, where a higher voltage on BL-bar indicates a stored logic 1, while a higher voltage on BL indicates a stored logic 0. The read data is then made available on the bit lines for subsequent processing, completing the read operation of the SRAM cell.

The various control signals to perform these operations in proposed FinFET-based 9T SRAM cell are well tabulated in Table 12.1.

TABLE 12.1Control Signals for the Proposed 9T FinFET SRAM Cell
Signals Hold Read Write 0 Write 1
WL 0 1 1 1
BL 0 1 1 0
BL-bar 0 1 0 0
RL 0 1 0 0

SRAM cells are characterized by several key parameters that define their performance and reliability. These parameters include:

Static Noise Margin: Static noise margin (SNM) refers to the stability or robustness of a FinFET 9T SRAM cell against noise-induced errors in maintaining stored data. SNM serves as a critical metric in assessing the reliability of the memory cell. In the context of a 9T SRAM cell, SNM is evaluated using various operational parameters, including voltage levels and transistor characteristics. It quantifies the voltage difference at which the cell transitions from one stable state to another, whether from ‘0’ to ‘1’ or vice versa, in the presence of noise. A higher SNM value indicates superior noise immunity and, consequently, enhances the overall reliability of the SRAM cell [5,6].

Access Time: The access time for both read and write operations is a critical parameter that directly influences the overall performance of the memory. Read access time refers to the duration required to retrieve data from an SRAM cell. It involves activating the appropriate WL and sensing the data stored in the cell, typically by detecting voltage differences on the bit lines. SRAMs are renowned for their fast read access times, often measured in nanoseconds, making them suitable for applications requiring rapid data retrieval, such as cache memory in processors. On the other hand, write access time denotes the time taken to modify or store data into an SRAM cell. This process involves setting the desired data values on the bit lines, activating the WL, and updating the content of the cell accordingly. Write access times in SRAMs are generally comparable to read access times, although they may vary depending on factors such as cell configuration and circuit design. Ensuring balanced read and write access times is crucial for maintaining efficient memory operations and achieving optimal system performance in various computing applications.

Power Consumption: Power stability is crucial for SRAM design, ensuring reliable operation and data integrity. Techniques like voltage regulation, power gating, and static power management are employed to minimize fluctuations in power supply and reduce both dynamic and static power consumption. By prioritizing power stability, SRAMs deliver consistent performance across various operating conditions, making them essential components in a wide range of computing systems.

12.3 Optimization of 9T SRAM

In the realm of digital circuit design, achieving efficient and reliable operation is paramount. A key factor in this pursuit is optimizing transistor sizing, especially in FinFET-based SRAM. Transistor sizing in FinFET SRAM is intricately linked to the number of fins per finger, directly influencing both the SNM and power consumption. This section undertakes a systematic exploration of transistor size, focusing particularly on manipulating the fins per finger within a FinFET design. Our research aims to unravel the intricate relationship between number of fins per finger and key circuit performance parameters, such as SNM and power consumption. Through meticulous examination and analysis of the ramifications of various fin topologies, we aspire to offer valuable insights into the design landscape of circuits, ultimately facilitating the advancement of more efficient and resilient digital systems [7]. In Figures 12.2a and b, the number of fins for access NMOS transistor, i.e. NM2 and NM7, is varied simultaneously and analysed for SNM and power stability, respectively. It is observed that with increase in number of fins in access transistor, the drive strength of the access transistors also increases. This heightened drive strength leads to faster switching speeds and higher current flow, resulting in a smaller voltage difference between the stable states (‘0’ and ‘1’) of the SRAM cell. Consequently, the decreased voltage difference makes the cell more susceptible to noise-induced errors, ultimately reducing the SNM. Due to this, the power in read operation is also increasing with the upsurge in number of fins.

In Figures 12.3a and b, the number of fins for inverter NMOS transistor, i.e. NM1 and NM4, is varied simultaneously and analysed for SNM and power stability, respectively. It clearly seems that the Write Static Noise Margin (WSNM) is reduced because inverter NMOS transistor may enhance certain performance metrics, such as speed; it can also compromise the noise immunity and stability of the SRAM cell, leading to a decrease in the SNM. Due to this, the static power is also increased during the write operation. As the number of fins in the NMOS inverter transistor increases, the Read Static Noise Margin (RSNM) also improves. This increase in the number of fins alters the effective channel doping concentration, which in turn affects the threshold voltage. As a result, the improved subthreshold slope, which measures the rate of change of drain current concerning gate voltage, with more fins enhances the cell’s ability to retain data reliably, further contributing to the increased SNM.

Finally, Figures 12.4a and b show the variation in SNM and power stability, with the variation in number of fins per finger of inverter PMOS transistor, PM0 and PM1. Increased drive strength and potential threshold voltage adjustment with more fins in the inverter PMOS transistor can lead to an overall increase in the SNMs of the SRAM cell. The increase in drive strength will increase collision and hence the power dissipation. Therefore, the read power is also increasing with increase in number of fins of PMOS. Whereas the write power is increasing with increase in number of fins till number 5, and after that, it started reducing because of better current flow and less leakage current.

The results for SNM and static power were analyzed with varying numbers of fins. The standard hierarchical sizing order of the transistors—starting with the inverter PMOS, followed by the inverter NMOS, and then the access NMOS—was maintained, based on their specific roles within the cell [8]. According to this hierarchical pattern of sizing, the PMOS transistor, responsible for the pull-up functionality during write operations and the standby mode, tends to be the smallest in size to ensure adequate drive strength for stable operation. The access NMOS transistor, facilitating data read and write access to the cell, is usually larger than inverter PMOS in size and smaller than the inverter NMOS, as it primarily serves to enable efficient access to the storage node without requiring excessive area. The inverter NMOS essential for data sensing and amplification during read operations is typically sized largest. Finally, this sizing hierarchy ensures balanced performance, power efficiency, and area utilization in the 9T SRAM cell design. Also, the SNM and static power consumption plays an important role in determining the overall efficiency of the SRAM cell. Thus, the number of fins is optimised by taking the simulated results as well as the hierarchical pattern of sizing into consideration to increase stability with low power operations. The optimised number of fins per finger for 9T SRAM cell is tabulated in Table 12.2.

TABLE 12.2Optimized Number of Fins per Finger for the Proposed 9T SRAM Cell
Transistor Number of Fins per Finger
Fins per finger for access NMOS, i.e. NM2 and NM7 4
Fins per finger for inverter NMOS, i.e. NM1 and NM4 10
Fins per finger for PMOS, i.e. PM0 and PM1 3

12.4 Result Analysis

12.4.1 Comparative Analysis

When evaluating the proposed 9T SRAM design, it becomes essential to conduct thorough comparisons with existing SRAM models. This approach is crucial for gaining a comprehensive understanding of its performance and benefits. By juxtaposing the 9T SRAM with other designs like the conventional 6T SRAM and possibly more recent advancements such as 10T SRAM, these comparative analyses enable us to pinpoint specific strengths and weaknesses of the proposed 9T SRAM design in comparison to its counterparts. Furthermore, benchmarking against established SRAM architectures provides valuable insights into potential trade-offs and areas for refinement, facilitating the optimization of the proposed 9T SRAM for superior performance in real-world applications. Table 12.3a shows the comparative analysis of stability margins with already published results for various SRAM designs. While Table 12.3b shows the comparative analysis of read and write access time with already published results, it has been found that the proposed 9T SRAM is showing 2.14- and 4.05- times fast read and write operation, respectively, as compared to already published 10T SRAM [8]. While stability is reduced a little by 1.7- and 1.9- times for read and write operation, respectively, the proposed SRAM is also compared with an already published 8T SRAM cell [9]. It has been found that the proposed 9T SRAM is almost 42- and 20- times faster as compared to already published 8T SRAM cell. The stability is reduced enough as compared to 8T SRAM cell. Hence, it has been shown that the proposed 9T SRAM cell provides fast read and write operations and is very beneficial for upcoming AI and ML applications. It still provides the stability within the required range and further can be improved by varying the number of fins as per requirement.

12.4.2 Environmental Variation

In this section, the performance of the proposed 9T SRAM is analysed with varying power supply as well as temperature. Figure 12.5a, Figure 12.5b, Figure 12.5c show the variation of SNMs, access time, and power consumption, respectively, of the proposed 9T SRAM with power supply. Figure 12.5a shows that as the supply voltage (VDD) levels increase, a noticeable trend emerges in the proposed 9T SRAM performance. As the high power mitigates the leakage currents in the SRAM cells, leakage currents can corrupt stored data over time, especially in low-power scenarios. By providing ample power, these leakage currents can be minimized, promoting stability. But it is noticed that VDD above 1.1 V will start reduction due to higher voltage levels exacerbating various forms of noise, such as process variation and transistor mismatch, leading to increased susceptibility to errors and reduced stability. Additionally, in Figure 12.5b, the speed of SRAM operations tends to decrease at higher VDD levels due to longer signal propagation times caused by larger voltage swings and increased parasitic capacitance. While Figure 12.6c shows that with increased VDD levels in SRAM, there is a corresponding rise in power consumption, this escalation occurs due to the direct relationship between supply voltage and power dissipation in integrated circuits. When VDD is elevated, each memory cell within the SRAM array requires more energy to store and retain data, contributing to higher overall power consumption. Hence, it is observed that the increased VDD will fasten up the proposed 9T SRAM with minute reduction in stability. But it will lead to increased power consumption. Hence, the operation VDD can be optimized to achieve a balance between minimizing static power and maintaining adequate performance and reliability in 9T SRAM cell design.

Figure 12.5d, Figure 12.5e, Figure 12.5f, Figure 12.5g, Figure 12.5h, Figure 12.5i show the variation of stability, access time, and power consumption of the proposed 9T SRAM with temperature. Figure 12.5d, Figure 12.5e, Figure 12.5f, Figure 12.5g show that the SNM of SRAM typically decreases as temperature rises. As the elevated temperatures lead to increased leakage currents in transistors, it affects the stability of stored data. Additionally, higher temperatures can exacerbate process variations, leading to inconsistencies in transistor characteristics and reducing SNM. While the read access time of SRAM tends to increase as temperature rises as shown in Figure 12.5h, this phenomenon occurs due to increased thermal agitation of carriers which results in higher parasitic and increased delay. The write access time is reduced up to some extent as mobility increases but it’s almost similar at different temperatures. Finally, Figure 12.5i shows the variation in power consumption of the proposed 9T SRAM with regard to temperature. It is observed that during the hold and write operation the mobility increases with rise in temperature and hence the leakage current. Because of this, the power consumption during the hold and write operation is increased with temperature. While it seems counterintuitive, power consumption in the proposed 9T SRAM during the read operation decreases with a rise in temperature. Because the increase in temperature increases the thermal energy that can activate carriers in the semiconductor material, it allows them to more effectively fill the traps and states that would otherwise contribute to leakage currents. Consequently, the leakage current decreases, leading to lower overall power consumption. Therefore, it can be said that the proposed 9T SRAM will lead to overall constant power consumption with reduced stability and slower read operation with the rise in temperature.

12.5 Conclusion

In this chapter, a comprehensive study on the design and performance evaluation of FinFET-based 9T SRAM for advanced AI/ML applications is presented. Through exhaustive analysis and simulation, this chapter has showcased the exceptional attributes of the proposed architecture, highlighting its heightened stability, diminished leakage power, enhanced read and write performance, and fortified resilience against various process variations and environmental factors. The result shows that the innovative integration of FinFET technology alongside the novel 9T SRAM cell design presents a promising avenue to fulfil the demanding prerequisites of forthcoming AI/ML systems. The optimized results and trade-off between various parameters for crafting efficient and dependable memory subsystems essential for realizing high-performance computing platforms tailored for AI/ML workloads is well explained in this chapter. It has shown that the FinFET-based 9T SRAM leads to fast read and write operation with some trade-off with stability and power. While the FinFET-based 9T SRAM is very less prone to environmental changes such as temperature, it increases power consumption with increase in supply voltage. By addressing the proposed SRAM, forthcoming research can further analyse it for various process variations, multi cell design, and memory arrays, etc. for next-generation AI/ML hardware, meeting stringent performance and power requisites.

13 Power Consumption and SNM Analysis of 6T and 7T SRAM Using 90 nm Technology

Devanshi Tripathi, Vedha Tripathi, Siddhant Bhushan, and Prachi Sharma

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-13

13.1 Introduction

Static random access memory (SRAM) is one of the most commonly used components in today’s Very Large Scale Integrated Circuit (VLSI)-based circuits for their small size, low power consumption, and high-speed ability [1]. They also take a large portion in VLSI circuit designs because of the number of transistors involved in making one cell [2,3]. As the technology advances and the size of the SRAM cell reduces to micron and even sub-micron levels, the density of these chips increases significantly [3]. The positive part is that the speed of these chips increase significantly, but at the same time, the power consumption increases also.

The topologies of SRAMs can vary based on the number of transistors, for example, 6T SRAMs use six transistors to realize memory operations and 7T SRAMs use seven transistors to realize memory operations and so on; various types of SRAMs with different transistor numbers can be made depending on the application [4]. Another way SRAMs can differ is based on the size used, i.e. 45, 65, 90, 180 nm, etc. Based on the usage of the chips in various applications, the appropriate SRAM is used.

Low power consumption and minimal delay, resulting in high speed, are essential characteristics of SRAM circuits. These attributes have driven numerous researchers and scientists to explore methods to enhance or maximize their potential [5]. But many issues are faced while exploring; due to the size, lesser voltage has to be provided. This increases the delay of the SRAM, and in addition, the threshold voltage of the chip also has to be reduced.

There are many key factors to designing an optimized SRAM. An SRAM is chosen for a particular task depending on its architecture and purpose fitting the performance required. When designed while keeping in mind the architecture, algorithm, logic, layout, etc., there are many possibilities to create various types of SRAMs [4].

The next section discusses the model and working of 6T and 7T SRAM cells. The design criteria which we have adopted for power dissipation are discussed. Then, with the help of the butterfly curve of each SRAM, we can determine the static noise margin (SNM), and it is used to make a stability analysis of the cells.

13.2 Six Transistor SRAM or 6T SRAM

The 6T SRAM consists of six transistors which are interconnected to serve as memory holders for the information we intend to supply. Two CMOSs are cross coupled to each other. The data stored within these inverters is accessed via the bit and bit line bar, using access inverters. The bit and bit line bar function as input lines during memory writing, while the (BN) Bit-line Bar serves as an output line for reading from the memory [6] (Figure 13.1).

When the word line (WL) is activated alongside the appropriate bit line and its complement (bit line bar), the system transitions into a state primed for read and write functionalities; if not, then the system transitions to a hold state [7].

Read Operation:

When the WL is activated, and the accessed memory cell is in a high state, the pre-charged capacitors maintain a stable voltage level of VDD, as no voltage difference between Q and N3 (access transistor) occurs, thus no changes happen. However, in the case of Q bar and N4, a voltage disparity exists due to Q being at zero, and the access transition near Q is at VDD, leading to capacitor discharge and a subsequent decrease in the bit bar voltage, hence providing an output of 1 [8].

Conversely, when the accessed memory cell holds a low state, the presence of a voltage differential between the bit and N3 nodes triggers an output of 0.

Write Operation:

Assuming low state, the bit line bar is grounded to create a voltage disparity, which is affected by the threshold voltage of N2: If V is less than Vth of N2, then P2 is turned on while the N2 is at off state, getting us an output as 1; hence, Q is successfully written to the memory from 0 to 1 [6].

Conversely, when writing to a memory cell set to a high state, and bit is grounded, if V is less than Vth of N1, then P1 is turned on, shutting off N1, respectively, giving Q as zero; hence, writing over is successful.

13.3 Seven Transistor SRAM or 7T SRAM

The 7T SRAM circuit too consists of two CMOS cross coupled to each other; in this circuit, we additionally connect an N-type Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (NMOS) transistor to the write line. And it too has to pass NMOS transistors connected to the bit and bit bar line. The transistors, NMOS 3 and NMOS 4, are connected to the read and write line to perform the read and write operations [5] (Figure 13.2).

13.3.1 Read Operation

In a given scenario, when activating the write line and read signals at a value of 1, while keeping NMOS5 active, the functionality of the SRAM cell hinges on the data stored in the memory bit Q. If Q assumes a value of “0”, the read path involves just NMOS2 and NMOS4 transistors, enabling smooth data retrieval. However, if Q is “1”, the read path becomes more intricate and sensitive. Now, it comprises a series connection of transistors NMOS1, NMOS5, and NMOS3. This setup presents a challenge as the series configuration reduces the cell’s driving capability, potentially impacting its performance. To address this challenge, precise adjustments to the sizes of these transistors are necessary. This ensures the SRAM cell maintains reliability and operates optimally even in these demanding read scenarios [9]. This highlights the significance of meticulous transistor sizing in SRAM cell design, ultimately contributing to improved memory system performance.

13.3.2 Write Operation

The write operation of the 7T SRAM cell initiates by deactivating NMOS 5 to sever the feedback connection. BL_Bar, carrying the complement of the input data, activates NMOS 3 while keeping NMOS 4 inactive. Structurally, the 7T SRAM cell resembles two consecutive inverters, with inverter 2 preceding inverter 1. Through NMOS3 transistor, data is transferred from BL_Bar to Q2, subsequently driving inverter 2 along with PMOS 2 and NMOS 2 to generate Q, representing the cell’s data. Similarly, Q drives inverter 1, comprising PMOS 1 and NMOS 1, to generate Q_Bar, which mirrors Q2 when the data is “0” and slightly exceeds Q2 when the data is “1”. Following this, the write line is deactivated, and NMOS5 is activated to reestablish the feedback link between the two inverters, ensuring stable storage of the updated data [9,10].

13.4 Results and Conclusion

This chapter presents the design and implementation of SRAM memory and its power consumption and SNM analysis. We designed 6T and 7T SRAM memory cells for this research and have simulated the circuits in Cadence Virtuoso Software. In Figure 13.3a, we observe the circuit diagram of 6T SRAM in Cadence Virtuoso in 90 nm technology. We have added a small circuit in the bottom containing pin writedata and a CMOS inverter in order to increase the efficiency of the SRAM circuit model. We are feeding in values to pins VDD, writedata, WL, and write so as to observe the dc and transient analysis of the circuit accordingly. Similarly, in Figure 13.4a, we have designed the 7T SRAM cell in 90 nm technology with same pin parameters so that we can observe the comparison of both based on same input values. The delay of 6T and 7T can subsequently be observed through transient analysis of 6T (Figures 13.3b and c) and transient analysis of 7T (Figures 13.4b and c). By performing dc analysis, we can observe the graph of qb vs q for both the circuits. On plotting q and qb of each graph with each other, we can obtain the butterfly curve of 6T SRAM (Figure 1.3d) and butterfly curve of 7T SRAM (Figure 13.4d). The butterfly curve is a very important component through which we calculate the values of SNM (static noise margin) of both 6T and 7T SRAM (Figures 13.3e and 13.4e).

Power consumption of a component depends on various factors such as load capacitance and voltage supply [11]. Therefore, there is a significant focus on reducing power consumption as it offers numerous benefits, especially in energy-constrained applications such as portable devices and IoT-based sensors. This reduction in power consumption is instrumental in extending battery life and enhancing the overall energy efficiency of a device [12]. Our findings in Table 13.1 indicate that the power consumption of 6T SRAM is lower than that of 7T SRAM.

TABLE 13.1Power consumption and SNM comparison
SRAM Supply Voltage (V) Power (W) SNM Value 1 SNM Value 2
6T 1 1.00854 × 10−7 4.2025 1.96
7T 1 2.088854 × 10−5 4 1.9044

SNM is another crucial factor to consider when integrating SRAM into circuits. SNM evaluates the memory cell’s ability to withstand errors induced by noise [12]. Through analysis using the butterfly curve, we observe that the SNM of 7T SRAM surpasses that of 6T SRAM. This highlights the better performance of 7T SRAM, indicating its reduced susceptibility to data errors caused by noise. Such resilience to noise-induced errors makes 7T SRAM particularly beneficial for communication systems applications [13].

To conclude, our observations and analysis lead us to the conclusion that the performance of 7T SRAM, in terms of power consumption and resistance to data errors caused by noise, exceeds that of 6T SRAM.

14 Transforming ElectronicsAn Extensive Analysis of Hyper-FET Technological Developments and Utilisation

Debashish Dash and Manwendra Chauhan

DOI: 10.1201/9781003483038-14

14.1 Introduction

The development of transistors, the essential components of contemporary electronics, has been fuelled by the semiconductor industry’s unrelenting march towards miniaturisation. Every generation of transistors, starting from the modest bipolar junction transistor (BJT) and ending with the widely used metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor (MOSFET), has improved performance and efficiency, accelerating technological progress in a variety of disciplines. However, as conventional MOSFET scaling approaches its limits, the search for a new transistor architecture that can maintain Moore’s Law has been more intense. The hyper-FET is a cutting-edge competitor that has the potential to completely change the transistor market. Unlike the planar construction of MOSFETs, hyper-FETs claim a gate-all-around (GAA) architecture, where the channel is fully encompassed by the gate electrode [1, 2, 3, 4]. This creative design provides several significant benefits.

Decreased Gate Tunnelling: Leakage currents are reduced by the close contact between the gate and channel, which lowers power consumption and boosts energy efficiency.

Improved Mobility: Higher carrier mobility and quicker device operation are made possible by the GAA structure’s greater electrostatic control over the channel.

Better Scalability: By reducing the channel length without sacrificing gate control, the GAA shape pushes the limits of transistor density [5,6].

These inherent advantages have propelled hyper-FETs to the forefront of transistor research, attracting significant attention from both academia and industry [7, 8, 9]. Numerous research groups worldwide are actively exploring the potential of this technology, and the field is witnessing rapid advancements in material engineering, device fabrication, and circuit design.

This chapter explores the fascinating field of hyper-FETs, carefully analysing their basic ideas, features, and state-of-the-art at the moment [10]. We set out on a quest through the complex field of study, examining significant discoveries, recognising pressing issues, and considering viable remedies. We also illuminated how new device designs and developing materials will play a critical role in determining the direction of hyper-FET technology in the future (Figure 14.1).

14.2 Hyper-FET Devices and Applications

The search for efficient, high-performance transistors for next-generation electronics has prompted the investigation of novel materials and structures. Among these fascinating competitors is the hyper-FET, a new transistor with a GAA design and the tantalising prospect of vanadium dioxide (VO2) integration [11, 12, 13, 14, 15].

Of the various ways to improve the performance of hyper-FETs, one intriguing path involves the use of vanadium dioxide (VO2) [16]. This material is interesting because it shows an amazing insulator-to-metal phase transition that can be induced by electrical or thermal stimulation. Through the intentional integration of VO2 into hyper-FET architectures, scientists are opening new and fascinating avenues for ultra-low-power electronics.

In its insulating condition, VO2 functions as a dynamic gate, essentially turning off current via the hyper-FET channel while in standby mode. This significantly reduces leakage currents and static power consumption, which are a major bottleneck in conventional transistors. By introducing a voltage or heat pulse, VO2 quickly transitions to its metallic phase, restoring conductivity and allowing active device functioning. This breakthrough method provides the path for new circuit designs with near-zero standby power, significantly increasing overall energy efficiency.

The integration of VO2 presents its own set of challenges, requiring precise control over material properties and device fabrication techniques. However, the potential rewards are substantial, pushing the boundaries of low-power electronics and opening doors for applications in battery-powered devices, mobile computing, and the ever-expanding internet of things (IoT) (Figure 14.2).

Near 68°C, this unusual material transitions from an insulator to a metallic state. Below this temperature, its I–V curve mimics that of an insulator, with very little current flow. Above the transition, the curve becomes linear, indicating metallic behaviour and higher conductivity.

The distinct I–V properties of phase transition materials have applications in a variety of sectors, including memory devices: Hysteretic behaviour can be utilised to produce non-volatile memories, such as memristors, whose resistance state is determined by the voltage history [17].

Sensors: The sensitivity of conductivity to temperature, pressure, or magnetic field can be used for sensing purposes.

Electronic Switches: The rapid change in conductivity during the phase transition can be used to switch and control devices. Understanding the I–V properties of phase transition materials allows us to realise their enormous promise for novel technology and intriguing future applications (Figure 14.3).

This complicated dance of electrons and phases opens the way for revolutionary circuit designs, promising ultra-low-power operation is ideal for wearables, IoT devices, and mobile computing [18, 19, 20]. Hyper-FETs help to make devices more environmentally friendly by reducing energy usage.

Faster Data Processing: Increased transistor speed enables powerful, efficient computing and communication systems.

14.3 Power and Speed Evaluation of Hyper-FETs

Hyper-FETs, with their GAA architecture and improved electron control, are promising candidates for the next generation of transistors [21, 22, 23]. However, the pursuit of quicker, more efficient electronics necessitates a careful examination of their power and speed capabilities. This section digs deeply into the methodology and criteria used to assess these essential features of hyper-FETs, providing a thorough analysis.

However, before they can dominate the circuits, we must first grasp their power consumption. This requires analysing three essential metrics:

DC Power Consumption: This shows how much power the hyper-FET consumes while idle (leakage) and during switching. Minimising both is critical to efficiency.

Leakage Current: This cunning culprit keeps the current flowing even while the hyper-FET is cooling. Hyper-FETs, with their improved gate control, significantly minimise this when compared to ordinary MOSFETs.

Energy Efficiency: This indicates how much energy is consumed by each flip of the hyper-FET switch, measured in Joules per operation. The lower the number, the more effective the device (Figure 14.4).

To accurately assess these variables, we use a three-pronged attack:

Device Level: We directly measure voltage and current on the hyper-FET, peeking inside like a microscopic detective.

Circuit Level: We place the hyper-FET in real-world circuit simulations, observing how it reacts under various workloads and determining its practical power consumption.

Benchmarking: We compare the hyper-FET to known champions such as FinFETs, using power measurements to objectively analyse efficiency advances. By using these methods, we can unravel the secrets of hyper-FET power, paving the path for a future in which transistors operate harder but not on our batteries. To determine the full speed potential of hyper-FETs, researchers use a set of critical metrics and evaluation methods. Switching delay [24] is an important metric that reveals how long it takes for the hyper-FET to gracefully flip between logic states, much like a dancer changing positions. Minimising this delay is critical for increasing circuit speed and facilitating faster processing. Cut-off frequency (fT), a metric of the hyper-FET’s high-frequency capability, denotes the frequency at which its current gain gracefully drops to unity. A higher fT, like a musician’s broader vocal range, indicates faster signal amplification and opens possibilities for high-speed applications. Propagation delay estimates how long it takes for a signal to travel through a single stage of a multi-stage hyper-FET circuit, like a relay race in which each stage must pass the baton quickly. Reducing this delay increases the overall circuit speed and ensures a smooth flow of information. To evaluate these metrics, researchers use a variety of techniques. Electrical measurements require time-domain characterisation via pulsed pulses, like a stopwatch measuring a sprinter’s split timings [25,26]. This directly evaluates switching delays and other transient responses, demonstrating the hyper-FET’s agility. Frequency-domain analysis examines the hyper-FET’s gain and phase response, like mapping a dancer’s choreography onto a musical score. This examination reveals its high-frequency performance and cut-off frequency, demonstrating its ability to execute graceful manoeuvres at high speeds. Circuit simulations provide a virtual stage for testing hyper-FET-based circuits under a variety of conditions, much like a dress rehearsal before the big show. Observing propagation delays and total speed across diverse applications demonstrates their versatility and adaptation to different choreographies. Understanding hyper-FET performance necessitates recognising the complex link between power and speed. Optimising one frequently impacts the other, creating a fascinating dance of trade-offs: high speed, high power: aggressive voltage scaling for faster switching results in increased dynamic power usage. Balancing speed needs with adequate voltage scaling is critical for optimal performance (Figures 14.5 and 14.6).

Material Selection: Materials with higher mobility have lower leakage currents, perhaps resulting in a better power-speed trade-off. Exploring innovative materials is critical to achieving more optimisation [27, 28, 29].

Circuit Design: Tailoring circuit design to individual application requirements can improve power-speed balance. Techniques like logic families and power gating can provide considerable advantages. Evaluating hyper-FETs primarily based on power and speed measurements provides an incomplete picture [30]. Real-world performance requires a more holistic approach, considering additional factors.

Fabrication Complexity [31,32]: The manufacturing issues connected with GAA [33] structures might have an impact on scalability and cost, affecting their overall viability.

Integration Challenges: To ensure seamless integration into real-world systems, compatibility with existing circuits and design processes must be carefully considered.

Emerging Technologies: Integrating innovative materials such as vanadium dioxide for dynamic threshold control has the potential to boost power efficiency even more [34, 35, 36, 37, 38, 39].

14.4 Conclusion

Hyper-FETs are on the verge of revolutionising the world of electronics. Their GAA architecture and improved electrostatics point to a future of faster, more efficient transistors capable of catapulting computers to new heights and altering areas ranging from mobile devices to renewable energy technology.

This chapter has explored the complex environment of hyper-FET research, focusing on power and speed characteristics, integration issues, and prospective applications. While various challenges remain, such as fabrication complexity and material restrictions, the research community is persistently seeking novel solutions. Novel materials, such as vanadium dioxide, hold great promise for additional efficiency improvements, while advances in circuit design and manufacturing techniques pave the way for more seamless integration with existing systems.

As we conclude this chapter, the enthusiasm for hyper-FETs is obvious. Their dance of force and speed is rife with possibilities, and the potential rewards are apparent. While complete acceptance of this disruptive technology may take time, the seeds of change have been planted, and the future of electronics is undeniably bright with the promise of hyper-FETs.
